Abstract: Long-video question answering is wasteful and lossy when a multimodal model must re-encode the same video for every query: context limits force sparse sampling or truncation, and no reusable representation remains for later questions. We present VidEngram, an agentic video QA system that builds a persistent hierarchical memory once at ingest and answers later questions from that memory. VidEngram captions video and audio jointly with Qwen2.5-Omni, consolidates segment memories into episode and entity layers, and uses a text-only agentic planner with specialized retrieval tools to produce grounded answers. The web interface makes this process inspectable: users can watch memory formation, inspect retrieval traces, verify cited evidence in the video player, and explore entity relationships. On the full OmniVideoBench, VidEngram with a GPT-5-mini planner outperforms a strong single-pass Qwen2.5-Omni baseline (37.0% vs. 32.3%), with gains concentrated on longer videos where single-pass coverage becomes sparse. Because perception is paid once, subsequent queries use low-cost text-only planning.

</details>


### [2] [ReMoVe: Refining a Video Model's Own Motion for Coherent Human Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18666)
*Jie Li,Muxin Pu,Hongyi Cai,Mingkang Dong,Jianyuan Guo,Tao Huang*

Abstract: Video generation models have become increasingly capable of synthesizing realistic human motion, yet they often lose track of the very structure that makes the motion plausible. As generated human motion becomes more complex, videos can suffer from progressive body deformation and appearance drift across frames. Existing motion-controlled methods use explicit representations to stabilize generation, but typically rely on pre-defined motion sequences as references. This raises a fundamental question: if the base generator already produces a usable coarse motion, why replace it with an external one? ReMoVe answers this question by treating the coarse output as a motion hypothesis rather than a final result. ReMoVe recovers and refines the underlying motion, then renders the refined motion as 3D mesh and transfers facial appearance onto it, thereby improving motion accuracy and identity consistency. We further derive depth from the same 3D scene and camera trajectory to impose scene-level spatial consistency beyond the mesh. By jointly conditioning video generation models on mesh and depth, ReMoVe synthesizes high-quality motion videos with controllable camera trajectories and enhanced spatio-temporal consistency. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReMoVe improves human structural stability, appearance consistency, temporal coherence, and motion-following fidelity, while retaining the base generator's motion prior and offering accurate camera control.

</details>

Abstract: Visual forced alignment (VFA) locates phoneme and word boundaries in a silent talking-face video given its transcript. Existing methods mainly enforce transcript order at the decoding stage, leaving the encoder without an explicit mechanism to anchor its temporally evolving representation to transcript progress. As a result, phoneme-specific evidence is blurred and alignment drift accumulates over long sequences. In this paper, we propose Speaking-Clock Mamba, which makes transcript progress part of the temporal recurrence. A selective state-space (Mamba) backbone is inserted between the visual frontend and the Conformer cross-attention encoder, and a Phoneme-Conditioned Selective Scan (PCSS) conditions its input-dependent step size on a supervised, structurally monotone speaking clock that estimates progress through the transcript. Since causal and bidirectional variants of this backbone make complementary errors, a training-free selector picks the more confident alignment per utterance. Experiments on LRS2 and LRS3 show consistent gains over protocol-matched baselines, and the supervised clock provides an explicit estimate of alignment progress.

</details>

Abstract: Unified multimodal models can support both visual understanding and generation, yet generative training does not necessarily improve understanding. We argue that this limitation may arise from a mismatch between the representation targeted by generation and the perceptual representation used for understanding. Conventional generation objectives primarily optimize synthesis-oriented spaces such as discrete visual codes or generative latents. We therefore introduce UMM-JEPA, which reformulates generation as progressive prediction of continuous visual-understanding representations. The model constructs a multi-scale representation hierarchy from coarse to fine, while pixel rendering is decoupled from representation pretraining and attached only afterward. Controlled comparisons show that the representation-prediction objective accounts for most of the understanding improvement, while a data-matched control leaves the group means nearly unchanged. UMM-JEPA reaches a 16-benchmark visual mean of 51.71, against 46.36 for the understanding-only Qwen-VL baseline, and the text mean also rises. Compared with discrete-code and latent-flow generation objectives, perceptual representation prediction yields stronger and more consistent transfer to understanding. Linear probes and visual-evidence interventions further reveal richer object and spatial structure and stronger use of image-specific information. These results suggest that generation can benefit understanding when it directly supervises the perceptual representations that understanding consumes.

</details>

Abstract: Clinical adoption of ophthalmic Artificial Intelligence remains limited by low interpretability and a lack of finely annotated multimodal data. Most existing datasets provide only coarse disease labels without the granular pathological findings required for differential diagnosis. We present LogicEye$^{Agent}$, a dual-pool inference and verification agent that emulates clinical reasoning by jointly verifying positive inclusion signs and negative exclusion criteria. Using LogicEye$^{Agent}$, we curate LogicEye$^{Data}$, a large-scale multimodal dataset of 804,461 fundus and OCT images paired with structured pathological reports spanning 38 diseases and 29 subtypes, with quality assured through joint verification by multimodal large language models and clinical experts. Building on this dataset, we further develop LogicEye$^{Bench}$, which incorporates a category-constrained loss for hierarchical disease recognition and report generation. Grounding coarse labels into verified visual findings effectively curtails diagnostic hallucinations and establishes a solid foundation for interpretable vision-language modeling in ophthalmology. The code is available at:https://github.com/Eden35/LogicEye-Agent-Driven-Multimodal-Dataset-for-Interpretable-Ophthalmic-Diagnosis.

Abstract: Visual question answering systems can produce confident responses that are weakly supported by their input images. Contrastive decoding can reduce this dependence on language priors, but a fixed correction may also overturn a correct answer, while selective answering can conceal errors by reducing coverage. We develop evidence-calibrated selective decoding, a framework that separates three decisions: whether local visual evidence supports a candidate, how strongly to correct the original distribution, and whether the resulting answer should be released under a verification budget. Evidence Verification across Crops (EVC) measures visual support and disagreement; Adaptive Contrast Correction (ACC) applies a bounded modification within the backbone's plausible candidate set; Budgeted Selective Answering (BSA) calibrates an answer-admission threshold on held-out images. We provide a complete mathematical specification, bounded-perturbation analysis, and an evaluation protocol that distinguishes forced answering from selective risk. Our quantitative analysis re-examines published baselines on POPE instantiated over MSCOCO, A-OKVQA, and GQA, together with four MME subtasks. These public measurements reveal variation across backbones, tasks, and correction settings that motivates adaptive control. The proposed framework has not yet undergone model-level evaluation; its effectiveness remains a falsifiable hypothesis rather than an established empirical result.

</details>


### [7] [Thinking from Another Side of the Box: Spacetime Images for Computer Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18671)

Abstract: Computer vision methods for video understanding, such as object detection and tracking, inherit the way videos are captured and displayed: as successive frames processed sequentially. However, a video is an inhomogeneous 2D+time volume of pixels, named voxels, and frames are only one family of cuts through it. In this frame-based representation, static backgrounds introduce computational redundancy, and motion can only be understood across several frames. In this position paper, we study another family of cuts: spacetime images (STIs), obtained by sampling any set of pixels other than frames, for instance a line of pixels followed over time. Such images are familiar in narrow forms, from line-scan inspection and kymographs to spatiotemporal slices for motion analysis, but have received little attention as a general input representation for computer vision and are often limited to (x,t) or (y,t) cuts. We define new cuts leading to STIs in their general form, explain how to read their content, and show that a family of cuts tiles the video so that existing dense annotations transfer at no cost. We also detail how motion appears in an STI, spatially and in the frequency domain, and discuss applications such as walking pedestrians, deposited objects, and dynamic scenes. Two pilot experiments illustrate the representation. The first trains a segmentation network entirely in the spacetime domain, on transferred annotations, and performs background subtraction on CDnet 2014 close to supervised frame-based methods, without explicit temporal modeling. The second recovers the pan speed of a camera as a moving cut, identified by a simple criterion on the frequency spectrum of the resulting image, turning a temporal estimation problem into the choice of a cut. Our position is not that STIs should replace frames, but that slicing the video volume is a design variable worth studying in its own right.

</details>

Abstract: Repeated airborne LiDAR surveys are costly, while stored 3D geometry can become outdated between observations. Existing change-detection methods compare observed epochs. However, they do not quantify the validity of geometry that has not been revisited. We distinguish observation, existence and geometric validity using a probabilistic filter over five geometric transitions: persistence, modification, growth, removal and replacement. The framework learns class-specific continuous-time transition priors from interval-censored observations and combines shape residuals with LiDAR free-space evidence. Functional anchors enable prior sharing across sites with different survey frequencies. In controlled experiments with misspecified dynamics and 60% visibility, our method reduces combined error from 36.9% to 16.5% and expected calibration error (ECE) from 0.19-0.32 to 0.017. Cross-site sharing reduces ECE at two-survey sites from 0.156 to 0.029. On Urb3DCD-v2, the method achieves 9.8% combined error and 0.016 ECE, outperforming baselines at 12.6% and at least 0.070, respectively. Re-survey prioritisation improves only modestly over random selection with two observations. Code and an evaluation protocol are released at https://github.com/anwaarua/life4d.

</details>

Abstract: Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) classifies an unlabeled pool that contains both known and novel classes, given labels for the known classes only. Existing GCD methods assume that every un- labeled image is clean, but unlabeled data collected in the wild is often degraded by blur, sensor noise and compression artifacts. We formalize the setting in which an unknown fraction of the unlabeled pool is corrupted by an unknown operator as Degraded Generalized Category Discovery (D-GCD). To tackle it, we propose Robust-GCD, a framework that estimates the corruption directly from the unlabeled pool and uses the estimate to build labeled anchors for known classes and pseudo-anchors for novel classes inside the corrupted region of feature space. A trust curriculum controls how much the unlabeled pool is trusted while the anchors take hold, and a paired alignment loss keeps a clean image and its corrupted twin close in feature space. Across five datasets and multiple corruption families, Robust-GCD recovers between 43% and 96% of the All accuracy that SimGCD loses under contamination, and more than 60% on four of the five datasets, with the largest gains on the corrupted images themselves.

</details>

Abstract: Pose estimation underpins a wide range of automated monitoring workflows in animal behaviour research. Unfortunately, endangered great apes such as chimpanzees suffer a chronic shortage of the type of hand-annotated training data that has made robust pose estimation in humans and other model organisms possible. This paper introduces SynPanZ, a synthetically generated dataset for pose estimation in great apes. Our pipeline renders multi-individual scenes from rigged, behaviourally animated chimpanzee models with randomised poses, lighting conditions, environmental contexts, and camera motion, producing animated RGB videos with per-instance 2D keypoint locations without manual labelling. To test whether synthetic augmentation improves cross-dataset generalisation, four DeepLabCut models are trained under matched settings on data sourced from DeepWild with varying real-to-synthetic augmentation ratios. Models are evaluated on ChimpACT, a visually disjoint zoo dataset. We find that augmenting the DeepWild training data with approximately three times as many synthetic frames nearly doubles instance coverage (12.34% to 22.54%) while maintaining keypoint localisation accuracy on detected individuals (PCK@0.20: 49.71% to 52.25%). Synthetic-only training fails, showing that rendered data complements rather than replaces real annotated data. This work represents a step towards reliable pose estimation for great apes.

</details>

Abstract: As text-to-image diffusion models see widespread deployment, removing unsafe or undesirable concepts without retraining has become a pressing need. Spectral methods are attractive because they are training-free and reversible, yet current variants apply the same output-value (OV) circuit intervention at every denoising step, assuming concept information is temporally uniform. This wastes intervention where concept energy is low while degrading image quality, an inefficiency that compounds across concept types. Through timestep-resolved spectral analysis, concept-specific energy is found to follow a consistent temporal hierarchy: content concepts decay fastest, object concepts are intermediate, and style concepts persist longest. This paper proposes Timestep-Adaptive Spectral Nullification (TASN), which adapts suppression strength and spectral components to the concept energy profile. Cross-attention at each timestep is decomposed into concept-aligned and concept-orthogonal components, and it is proved that when the orthogonal component is approximately stable, the collateral-minimizing schedule sets suppression proportional to concept energy. TASN implements this with a timestep-adaptive spectral subset tracking only active concept directions, strictly generalizing uniform Spectral Nullification (SN). On five red-teaming benchmarks, TASN lowers attack success rate below uniform SN while recovering the majority of its quality loss; ablations confirm gains arise from temporal targeting rather than reduced suppression, generalizing across concept categories and architectures.

</details>

Abstract: Depth-aware augmented reality on phones needs dense geometry of the surrounding room. iPhones and iPads without LiDAR expose only sparse feature points and detected planes, and ARKit often takes tens of seconds to report the first wall. We describe a scan-free pipeline for these devices. A point-map network (MoGe-2) predicts metric geometry from each camera frame, the remaining depth shift is recovered from the camera intrinsics, and each frame's scale is aligned to views already in the map. A persistent voxel map with visibility-based expiry answers occlusion and collision queries. Co-located devices share one coordinate frame by solving yaw and floor-plane translation from sightings of each other. We evaluate the deployed system on 309 device-sessions from 19 device models without LiDAR, with 179 device-sessions from LiDAR phones in the same deployment as a reference population. A first dense depth map was recorded within 10 s in 80% of sessions, while ARKit had reported a wall plane by then in 25%. The median session scale was 0.98 against ARKit's wall planes and 1.00 against its camera translation. Scale changed by a median of 9% between nearby frames, against 0.4% with LiDAR, and depth points spread three times as widely around walls. In multi-device sessions, 69% of devices without LiDAR reached a converged shared frame, against 84% of LiDAR phones. The main remaining error is scale that changes from frame to frame.

</details>

Abstract: A vision transformer read at a higher resolution than it was trained at spreads each attention row over many more patches, yet every row must still hand out exactly one unit of weight. A law of softmax attention predicts that a growing crowd of bounded scores dilutes the attention on any patch or register whose score stays fixed. The same law gives a remedy that needs no training: multiply every score, the sink's included, by the ratio of the logarithms of the test and training token counts. Given sufficient margins at the training resolution, this keeps the attention on a match when one is present, and on a sink such as a register when none is, though trained models need not meet those margins. We test these predictions in public vision transformers and in controlled models read far above their training token count. Attention spreads as predicted. Registers lose their share, though production models compensate in part, and the rescaling holds the share of DINOv2's registers nearly level. Applied at test time without training, the rescaling recovers part of the lost accuracy in nearly every model, with absolute or rotary positions and with registers, and on images with native detail. The calibrated multiplier rises with the logarithmic ratio on average and varies across models. Its rise is steeper than the square-root rule used in image generators; in controlled models the log ratio is also more accurate, while in the public models the two rules' accuracy difference remains unresolved. The rescaling does not restore the attention a model learned, and why it helps, beyond countering the dilution, remains open.

</details>

Abstract: Existing multimodal gait recognition methods exploit complementary information by fusing heterogeneous representations. Whether useful complementarity can be constructed from a single representation before fusion remains less explored. We propose PolarGait, which constructs complementary views of Human Sketch through filtering and spatial reparameterization. Filter-Oriented Sketch Decomposition (FOSD) uses Gaussian smoothing to extract coherent structures and absolute residuals to capture local response differences. Gait-Oriented Polar Transformation (GOPT) combines silhouette-guided sketch normalization, a gait-oriented center, and lower-body-biased non-uniform angular sampling to construct a polar view complementary to the Cartesian representation. Silhouette provides auxiliary global body-shape information. Experiments on CCPG, SUSTech1K, CASIA-B, and Gait3D validate the framework. Under the single-Sketch setting, FOSD+GOPT improves the original Sketch baseline by 6.6% on CCPG and 4.6% on CASIA-B. The complete PolarGait achieves Rank-1 accuracies of 87.9%, 95.2%, 96.6%, and 86.4% on the four benchmarks, respectively. These results show that complementary gait information can be constructed within a single structured representation and integrated for robust gait recognition.

</details>

Abstract: Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (LMRMs) extend large language models to heterogeneous modalities, demonstrating strong potential across a wide range of open-ended tasks. Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has been widely adopted to improve reasoning performance in LMRMs; however, existing approaches overlook the critical role of visual perception uncertainty in guiding reasoning processes. We term this limitation Reasoning Allocation Misalignment, where the misalignment between visual perception uncertainty and reasoning depth leads to over-allocation of deep reasoning under uncertain visual grounding, resulting in unnecessarily long reasoning trajectories and degraded performance. To address this issue, we propose ThinkWise, a perception-aware RLVR framework that jointly models task difficulty and visual perception state to regulate reasoning depth. Specifically, we introduce an instance-adaptive visual perception modeling mechanism that enables context-aware calibration of perception uncertainty, leading to more accurate perception estimation. We further design a structured reward formulation that jointly considers perception, correctness, and task difficulty. In particular, an answer reward provides supervision for correctness, a perception- and difficulty-conditioned length reward adaptively regulates reasoning depth, and a perception reward encourages trajectories with stronger visual grounding. Extensive experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkWise consistently improves the trade-off between accuracy and reasoning efficiency across both 3B and 7B models. These results highlight the importance of perception-aware reasoning and establish a principled framework for advancing LMRMs.

</details>

Abstract: Our prior work adapted a 2D CLIP dual encoder with LoRA to a multi-source cell-microscopy corpus, reaching anomaly-scoring ROC-AUC up to 0.985. This paper advances the same contrastive-learning domain-adaptation methodology from 2D microscope images to 3D microscope volumes, pairing a 3D Swin Transformer voxel encoder (MONAI's SwinUNETR backbone) with ClinicalBERT as the finding-text encoder. We train on 3,000 single-cell 3D confocal fluorescence volumes (4 tagged organelle structures) from the Allen Institute's public hiPSC single-cell image dataset, paired with template-generated finding text, with mitotic (dividing) cells oversampled as an abnormal-like proxy class validated by ROC-AUC. Naively reusing work's freeze backbone, LoRA the attention projections recipe for the Voxel Encoder collapses voxel embeddings to near-input-invariance, since LoRA's low-rank delta cannot compensate for an otherwise-frozen, randomly initialized (not pretrained) 3D Swin backbone, even as InfoNCE loss decreases healthily. Switching the Voxel Encoder to full fine-tuning resolves this: 4-way zero-shot structure accuracy rises from 25.0% (chance) to 48.6%, and voxel anomaly-scoring ROC-AUC improves from 0.486 to a peak of 0.628 (still below the 0.70 sanity threshold), while bidirectional retrieval recall stays low (R@10 $\le$3.8%). We further introduce a nearest-neighbor diagnosis-reason retrieval evaluation, reaching 29.8% hit-rate@{1,5} at its best scale while scoring 0.0% elsewhere. The central finding of this MVP is architectural: LoRA's parameter-efficiency assumption presumes a pretrained backbone worth preserving, and breaks down when the backbone is randomly initialized.

Abstract: Three-dimensional photoacoustic computed tomography (PACT) provides volumetric contrast related to optical absorption for studying tissue anatomy and dynamics. Method development requires experimental datasets that retain measured signals and acquisition context. Public resources remain limited in their combined coverage of varied specimens, volumetric acquisition modes and reusable reconstruction information. Here we present PACTHub, a large-scale structured experimental dataset comprising 1,464 acquisition groups and 99,810 photoacoustic exposures collected over nearly four years. Animal, phantom and human measurements span single-position imaging, rotational compounding, panoramic scanning and time series. A common PAdata/JSON representation links acoustic signals to detector geometry, exposure selection, signal preparation and physical reconstruction coordinates. Public reconstruction implementations, numerical volumes and maximum-intensity projections provide practical entry points for reusing the measurements. Spatial and temporal examples demonstrate how retained acquisition relationships support panoramic reconstruction and regional signal analysis. Detector and exposure subsets enable studies of angular sampling and measurement budget. Volumetric evaluation combines PSNR, SSIM and L1 with Q, a complementary measure of local-contrast agreement within reference-defined structures. By connecting measured signals to reconstruction settings and numerical outputs, PACTHub makes its experimental scale and diversity available for reuse. It provides an experimental foundation for developing and evaluating artificial intelligence (AI) methods in volumetric PACT.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion models for image super-resolution can recover scene content while producing distracting artifacts or unbalanced colors. We study whether a large language model (LLM) can improve perceptual quality by editing a small subset of existing U-Net weights without a clean reference image. The proposed framework provides the editor with architecture code, selected weights, activation summaries, and sampling previews. It permits up to 1,020 candidate trials while limiting accepted changes to three layers and fewer than 0.1\% of model parameters. The evaluation protocol uses ResShift, SinSR, and SUPIR and compares GPT-6 Astra with a specified Qwen3-VL model under matched access. Feedback metrics guide editing, while separate quality metrics and blinded human comparisons evaluate the final weights. We also report an executed color calibration experiment using real photographs and two small residual networks. This experiment demonstrates correction of a fixed color shift and reveals that an improving feedback score can accompany worse performance on unseen scenes. A separate lightweight super-resolution experiment isolates modest changes in luminance detail while preserving color. The findings motivate restricted updates and independent evaluation of perceptual weight editing.

Abstract: Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection in vision-language models presents unique challenges due to the reliance on cross-modal similarity. Even when embeddings are well separated for in-distribution (ID) data, similarity scores can remain overconfident for OOD inputs. A common remedy is to adapt the model on ID data, but adaptation discards the pretrained geometry that made the similarity meaningful. We propose multi-modal logit fusion (MM-Log), which keeps both by pairing a gradient-free zero-shot prior with a task-adapted branch in each modality and fuses their four cross-modal similarity channels into a single OOD score. Because the zero-shot priors are never adapted, the cross-channels act as a consistency check between the pretrained and adapted geometries: an OOD input that fools the adapted branch through a shortcut must additionally agree with frozen CLIP to retain a high score. We formalize this as a margin bound showing that the fused score is provably penalized by disagreement between the zero-shot and adapted branches, a signal to which adapted-only scores are structurally blind. MM-Log requires no auxiliary OOD data, negative prompts, or test-time adaptation, and trains on few-shot ID data alone. Across Far-OOD, Near-OOD, and Hard-OOD benchmarks, MM-Log consistently outperforms strong CLIP-based baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Efficient aerial visual-language navigation (VLN) requires reducing model storage and inference costs while maintaining instruction-conditioned action prediction. We present ARC-VLN, a token-type-aware recursive computation framework built on a language backbone with shared parameters. ARC-VLN retains full-depth computation for text and action tokens while adaptively selecting exit depths for visual tokens. Representations of early-exiting visual tokens are retained, and active-token compaction shortens the sequences processed by subsequent recursive blocks. To mitigate routing collapse to a single exit depth, we design a dedicated learning strategy that encourages the model to distribute visual tokens across multiple exit depths.On OpenFly's Test-Seen and Test-Unseen subsets, ARC-VLN achieves the lowest navigation error and the highest success rate and success weighted by path length among all compared methods. Production recall is 8%, and 0 of 45 on handwriting. The backstop removes no correct medication and cuts unsafe outputs from 45 to 1. On handwriting nearly all medications never reach the model in readable form; on typed sheets the model mostly picks the wrong text. We trace much of the handwriting floor to a defect in our own crop step, then replace the pipeline: rotated-box crops, a 2B-parameter vision-language model that re-reads prescription lines and extracts the medications, and per-line numeric grounding. On the same captures recall rises from 8% to 67% (paired +59 points, 95% CI +40 to +78): typed 19% to 100%, handwritten 0 to 20 of 45, with no unsafe output. Rotated crops account for most of the reading gain and the model for most of the recall gain, and the OCR confidence gate is now the main limit. The cost is 61 s per photograph against about 12 s, and about 1.9 GB of memory. An earlier claim that better OCR lowered recall does not survive the controlled comparison, and we withdraw it. All results are in-sample, from one writer and one device.

Abstract: Fine-tuning on hard negatives is a popular way to improve the compositional reasoning of vision-language models, but when accuracy goes up it is rarely clear what the model actually learned. We ask this question for multimodal large language models. We use CompHall, 2,860 forced-choice questions (spatial, attribute, counting and part-whole) with two graded hard negatives each, three model families (LLaVA-1.5, LLaVA-NeXT and Idefics2), and controls that such studies often lack: gray-image evaluation, three seeds, option-order tests, control arms trained on different question types, and independent benchmarks. We find four things. First, CompHall is not a pure test of vision: part-whole questions can be answered from the text alone (66-95% without the image), and per-image accuracy is unrelated to an object-hallucination probe (r = -0.05), so we see no evidence that compositional failures drive hallucination. Second, the original fine-tuning method scored mostly image-placeholder tokens and hurt both LLaVA models. With a corrected loss, fine-tuning raises accuracy on the spatial-relation questions of CV-Bench in all three families, by +3.2, +23.1 and +6.0 points (averaged over both option orders). Control arms show that the gain needs spatial training questions, and for LLaVA-NeXT about two-thirds of it appears even on relation words that were only wrong options in training. Third, the gain is not general: on CompHall itself it is significant only for LLaVA-NeXT (+6.9 points) and not detectable for Idefics2, it reaches about 3 points at most on true/false statements, and LLaVA-1.5 fine-tuned on other question types learns to answer "true" too often. Fourth, both losses raise the yes-bias of LLaVA-1.5 on POPE.

</details>

Abstract: Long-running environmental camera networks produce abundant images but few reliable annotations. Using FujiView, a network of webcams around Mount Fuji, we study three linked questions: whether gamification affects annotation participation and agreement, how annotators disagree on ordinal visibility labels, and how label budget affects downstream classification. Across seven nonrandomized sessions with 22 labelers, gamification showed a more promising pattern for sustained participation than for labeling speed or agreement. In the closest remote-session comparison, the session recorded as gamified had longer active labeling time, while throughput and reference agreement remained similar. Annotation disagreement was substantial at fine granularity: crowd votes matched the single-annotator reference on 49.1% of exact visibility classes, versus 90.2% after grouping them into Good/Poor. In five-replicate label-budget experiments, models using 25% of the available label pool achieved held-out exact accuracy only 2.8 percentage points below the full-pool model on average. Together, these findings highlight the importance of designing reliable annotation targets, evaluating engagement mechanisms without assuming causal benefits, and using empirical scaling curves to plan labeling effort.

Abstract: Scenic visibility is not a single forecasting problem: planning a trip months ahead, deciding whether to go outside today, and judging whether tomorrow will be clear rely on different information. Using a year-scale multimodal archive of Mount Fuji webcams and meteorological observations, we identify a clear hierarchy across timescales. Seasonal climatology provides useful long-range guidance, webcam imagery is the strongest same-day observation (AUC 0.95), and the numerical forecast valid on the target day carries most of the day-specific signal one to three days ahead. Revealing this hierarchy requires evaluation that respects the dependence among cameras observing the same regional weather state: permissive splits substantially overstate multi-day performance, while date-grouped and forward-in-time evaluation reduce the apparent contribution of the current scene and current weather. On 6,575 camera-days, the current visual summary provides no measurable one-day gain once the target-day forecast is available. The remaining opportunity lies at shorter horizons, where the present visual representation discards cloud geometry and motion. These results recast scenic visibility prediction as a multi-timescale sensing problem rather than a single multimodal forecasting task.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) have demonstrated its effectiveness for photorealistic novel view synthesis. However, extracting clean, watertight, explicit surface meshes from 3DGS remains a critical challenge. Previous methods approximate geometry via discrete Marching Tetrahedra, where each grid vertex is assigned a single scalar signed distance value. At complex geometric boundaries and thin structures, this suboptimal parameterization forces severe compromises across shared vertices, causing gradient collisions and geometric frustration. Furthermore, existing optimization objectives neglect element shape conditioning, producing degenerate triangles. We introduce TopSDF, a framework that models the signed distance state as a multivariate Gaussian random field. By learning latent topological covariances across tetrahedral vertices, our formulation expands local degrees of freedom for SDF estimates, allowing the candidate mesh vertices to be placed flexibly along the bipolar edges in the tetrahedra as driven by such topological correlation. Concurrently, we directly integrate variational meshing energies into the joint optimization, coupled with an energy-driven Gaussian densification strategy to improve triangle mesh quality. Our experiments across Mip-NeRF 360, Tanks & Temples, and ScanNet++ show that TopSDF achieves state-of-the-art mesh-based novel view rendering (+0.86 dB PSNR on Mip-NeRF 360) and triangle-mesh quality (from 0.37 to 0.49 aspect ratio).

</details>

Abstract: Hybrid bonding is rapidly emerging as a foundational technology for next-generation advanced packaging, enabling ultra-high-density interconnects required for heterogeneous integration of chiplets, logic, memory, and specialized accelerators. As the industry pushes toward ultra-fine pitch interconnects approaching single- digit microns and below, significant technical challenges must be addressed across the process flow. These challenges include achieving extremely tight wafer and die alignment tolerances, maintaining surface planarity and cleanliness at the nanometer scale, controlling copper dishing and dielectric erosion during CMP, and ensuring defect-free oxide and copper-copper bonding interfaces. In addition, scaling hybrid bonding to ultra-fine pitch requires improvements in metrology, defect inspection, thermal management, and yield control to support high-volume manufacturing. Integration with complementary technologies such as silicon interposers, wafer-to-wafer and die-to-wafer bonding architectures, and heterogeneous material stacks further increases process complexity. This presentation reviews the key technical barriers to ultra-fine pitch hybrid bonding and discusses emerging process solutions and equipment innovations required to enable reliable, high-density interconnects for future high-performance computing, AI, and advanced system-in- package applications.

</details>

Abstract: Training generalist GUI agents requires large volumes of interaction trajectories comprising screenshot sequences paired with grounded UI actions, yet collecting such data through human demonstration is expensive and difficult to scale. Although public software tutorial videos contain rich operational demonstrations, their continuous pixel streams lack discrete action boundaries, semantic intents, and precise spatial coordinates, making them difficult to use directly for model training. To address this, we present T2T (Tutorial-to-Trajectory), a fully automated pipeline for synthesizing GUI agent trajectories at scale. T2T spans desktop, web, and mobile platforms to convert unannotated video tutorials into structured, executable interaction trajectories. To guarantee trajectory quality, T2T combines active segment filtering, keyframe identification via localized UI change, shortcut-free observation frame selection, presence-aware grounding, and two-stage action-space normalization. These refined trajectories are then converted into multi-task training views spanning navigation, planning, forward dynamics, and grounding. We fine-tune Qwen3-VL-8B on a subset of the more than one million human-annotation-free training samples generated by this pipeline. Evaluations show that the model achieves consistent gains across multiple benchmarks covering desktop, web, and mobile environments, including OSWorld-G, ScreenSpot-Pro, AndroidControl, and AgentNetBench, indicating improved core capabilities in grounding, step-level instruction execution, and trajectory-level decision-making. Notably, all gains are achieved without any in-distribution training data from target domains. Learning exclusively from trajectories extracted from public videos via the T2T pipeline, the model exhibits cross-application and cross-task generalization capabilities.

</details>

Abstract: Optical chemical structure recognition (OCSR) demands strict all-or-nothing graph fidelity, where a single misidentified bond order or formal charge makes the predicted graph differ from the reference structure. While vision-language models (VLMs) can reinspect ambiguous visual regions, unconstrained full-graph regeneration often introduces regressive errors into previously correct substructures. We present LVRE, a training-free visual agent that refines molecular graphs via targeted visual inspection and reversible transactional editing. Rather than regenerating the entire graph sequence, LVRE examines a high-resolution crop alongside the global diagram and local graph neighborhood, proposing modifications to named graph records that a deterministic controller checks. A controller executes each proposed modification as an isolated transaction, and a visual review module arbitrates which edits to commit or roll back. Across four foundation VLMs, LVRE improves graph accuracy by 8.0-8.5 percentage points over single-pass recognition and by 3.0-3.3 points over fixed local review. In a paired evaluation of 1,000 diagrams using Gemini 3.8 Flash, LVRE achieves 100 successful repairs while introducing only 15 regressive errors, in contrast to global reflection which yields 65 repairs against 30 introduced errors. On this paired set, LVRE introduces half as many errors on initially correct predictions as global reflection.

Abstract: Context Language Models let a model edit its own context, while Suffix Cache Reuse makes edits to text efficient by adjusting the positions of cached keys for surviving tokens. We extend these ideas to video language models with multimodal rotary position embeddings (M-RoPE). We represent a long video as timestamped segments. After viewing a sparsely sampled version, the model selects segments for decoding at 4x the frame rate, and the remaining segments are removed from its context. Our rebuild method reuses the KV cache of surviving segments by rotating their keys with a single offset shared by the temporal, height, and width components of Qwen2.5-VL's M-RoPE. A content-hashed cache also avoids repeating vision-encoder computation for those segments. In preliminary experiments with Qwen2.5-VL-7B on synthetic needle-in-a-video-haystack questions over public-domain films, the method reduces prefill tokens and end-to-end rebuild latency relative to full recomputation while usually producing the same answer. Densifying the segment containing the answer improves accuracy when that segment is known. When the model selects segments itself, accuracy does not improve: it rarely identifies a needle that was absent from the sparse sample. We release the code, raw logs, and scripts used to reproduce the paper's results.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have shown strong capabilities as generalist agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive environments. Yet real-time environments such as video games demand both deliberate control for exploration and split-second reactions to fast-moving hazards. Existing agents struggle to handle both: relying solely on direct VLM actions is too slow for sudden events, whereas delegating execution to fixed scripts leaves the agent blind at the moment of action, leading to frequent misfires whenever the scene shifts. Humans overcome this by pairing normal conscious control with conditioned reflexes-while the mind handles deliberate actions and shapes reflex rules, the reflex itself still relies on live vision to make the final instant decision. Emulating this mechanism, we propose ViCoR (Visual Conditioned Reflex). Alongside normal gameplay actions, the main VLM defines and updates lightweight situational guidance for recurring events, while a fast visual reflex branch (invoking the VLM in no-thinking mode) inspects live local views with motion cues to make the final sub-second action decisions. By offloading 67.8% of high-frequency reactions to the reflex branch (680 ms per check), ViCoR reduces average per-action decision latency by 2.6x (from 14.0 s to 5.4 s) and cuts token consumption by 36.9%. Across 17 diverse 2D and 3D games in the GameWorld benchmark, ViCoR achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving task success rate by +11.8% (from 52.9% to 64.7%) and average normalized progress by +13.9% (from 66.5% to 80.4%) over the strongest deliberative VLM and script baselines. In fast-paced real-time games, ViCoR refines its reflexes across episodes to boost progress by 1.67x, and even with a low-effort main planner, surpasses high-effort deliberative baselines by +8.1% at one-third of the token cost.

</details>

We explore channel-wise concatenation as the lower-cost option for a common production regime: structure-preserving edits that change appearance (sky, exposure, foliage, cleanup) but not layout, about 1,400 training pairs, a 4B non-distilled backbone, and 2K frames. It keeps the sequence at its original length, where sequence concatenation doubles it and roughly quadruples attention cost, and needs only single-scale classifier-free guidance (two forward passes per step instead of three). Starting from FLUX.2 Klein 4B base, we widen only the input projection and train both schemes with the same trainer, data, and hyperparameters. Channel-wise concatenation keeps the output pixel-aligned with the source; in our runs sequence concatenation, which must learn the correspondence between 10,880 target and 10,880 source tokens from fewer than 1,500 pairs, displaced structure, an observation we report without claiming a general ranking. On 29 held-out images the editor reaches 20.3 dB PSNR and 0.198 LPIPS against the human edit (unedited source: 15.0 dB), and counterfactual conditioning tests show it largely learned a global grade of the source rather than a selective edit, which we report as the open problem of this recipe. Abstract: Cancer diagnosis relies on histopathological evaluation, where tumor morphology guides disease classification and informs treatment decisions. Deep learning (DL) models excel at predicting diagnostic properties from histopathology images. Unlike typical medical images featuring small critical areas, histopathological images are textural, with relevant texture spanning the entire image; this makes it challenging to explain DL predictions by locating relevant image regions. Counterfactual explanations offer a promising alternative by asking "what changes in the image could have altered the model prediction." To achieve this, we propose SCALE, a framework for generating such counterfactual explanations for histopathological DL models. Across nine counterfactual directions spanning multiple cancer types, SCALE generates whole-slide counterfactual explanations that consistently shift predictions toward the intended target while maintaining biological feasibility. Blinded assessment by six board-certified pathologists showed higher morphological plausibility for SCALE (with a win rate of 91.7% over baselines) and promising counterfactual effectiveness (with a target consistency of 96.7%).

Abstract: A classifier that is usually right still makes confident mistakes, and a deployed system needs some way to decide when to answer and when to abstain. Cheap post-hoc scores such as maximum softmax probability (MSP) and predictive entropy are the standard answer. Distances in feature space are a popular alternative, and fusing several signals into one score is a natural next step. We ask whether adding a feature distance signal to MSP and entropy improves error detection, and whether it increases the share of predictions that can be accepted at a certified error rate when only a small labelled calibration set is available. We train a CIFAR-adapted ResNet-18 from scratch on CIFAR-10 (92.92% test accuracy, one seed) and compare five reliability scores: MSP, entropy, distance to the predicted-class centroid, a logistic fusion of MSP and entropy, and a logistic fusion of all three signals. Acceptance thresholds are chosen on a held out calibration set with Clopper Pearson upper bounds, Bonferroni corrected over a prespecified family of 315 rules at 95% simultaneous confidence. Confidence based scores were best. Error AUROC was 0.9230 for MSP, 0.9233 for entropy and 0.9235 for MSP plus entropy, against 0.8814 for the three signal fusion and 0.8224 for distance alone. At a 5% risk target with 1,000 calibration images, entropy accepted 84.82% of test images with 2.05% observed error, while three signal fusion accepted 80.61% with 2.21%. With 250 calibration images, neither distance nor the three-signal fusion could certify the 5% target at all. The results come from one seed, one dataset and one architecture, with no corruption experiments, so they say nothing about distribution shift detection. Within these limits, the distance signal did not earn its added complexity.

Abstract: Occupancy estimation for automated vehicles is a crucial task that builds a 3D map using vehicle sensors. This map allows the vehicle to identify drivable areas and various obstacle classes. The task involves complex subtasks such as depth estimation, object classification, and segmentation to align semantic information with the 3D grid. Moreover, producing these estimations from multiple sensors demands significant computational power and memory, as high-dimensional data fusion requires complex feature extraction and processing. Consequently, most existing methods rely on large and computationally intensive architectures that are not suitable for real-time or real-world applications. We present a real-time, lightweight architecture that leverages camera and radar sensors through a residual multi-stage head. Unlike prior methods that rely on heavy camera-only blocks, our architecture employs camera-radar fusion and lightweight components. Additionally, we introduce a task-specific data augmentation strategy that enhances data diversity and improves generalisation. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance, attaining 45.7 mean Intersection over Union (mIoU) on Occ3D and 41.9 Ray-level Intersection over Union (RayIoU) (41.9) on OpenOcc datasets, running at 17.2 frames per second (FPS) and using only 5.3 GB of memory, compared with 4.1 FPS and 7.7 GB for STCOcc.

Abstract: A robot that returns to a space must tell which objects were added, removed or moved since its last visit, while its trajectory, viewpoints and lighting differ between the two visits. Abstract: Reliable vision-language reasoning requires a control decision that conventional grounding methods leave implicit: which input is entitled to determine the answer? Auxiliary text may be a defeasible claim about an image, a binding task instruction, or compatible context. Correcting every image-text conflict toward the image therefore repairs false claims but overwrites valid task rules. We formalize this setting as mixed-authority vision-language reasoning and introduce AITHER, an Authority-Informed Text-Image Hierarchy for Evidence Routing. Its final policy, the Semantic-Frame Router, is a training-free black-box control layer that (i) compiles explicit task directives, (ii) recovers standardized spatial frames from metadata-scrubbed pixels, (iii) resolves the semantic polarity of remaining notes, and (iv) routes among frozen observable candidates with fail-closed semantics. This ordered decomposition converts authority arbitration from an unconstrained prompt preference into a reproducible inference program. Across 1,620 MATRIX predictions, AITHER reaches 95.1% accuracy, exceeding the strongest task-and-frame baseline by 9.1 points with a 95% record-cluster interval of 6.8-11.5 points and no harmed records. It further reaches 100.0% on 1,800 controlled FRAME predictions and 93.3% on GQA-MATRIX. The results show that explicit authority routing can simultaneously improve false-claim correction, preserve task-defined answers, and eliminate clean-context overcorrection.

Abstract: A satellite forecast of a typhoon should give a low-error picture of the next two days, together with an ensemble whose members are coherent fields and whose spread responds to the storm at hand. A small direct regression network already gives the lowest squared error on held-out storms, so a generative model has to earn its cost through the part of the future that the regression smooths away. We present TyphoonWorld, a discrete-token world model that generates only that part. Conditioned on tokens of the regression forecast, the latest frames and the best-track state at forecast time, it samples residual fields lead by lead, and members add these residuals to the regression center. We compare it under one protocol with per-pixel Gaussian networks, a diffusion model of the same residuals, residuals from other storms and an unconditional token world model, on 276 forecast windows from 46 held-out storms of Digital Typhoon. TyphoonWorld matches the squared error of the regression, lowers the CRPS of the area colder than 220 K, a direct measure of deep convection, by 14 to 39% against every other ensemble, and ties its spread to forecast error as closely as the Gaussian network, with a correlation of 0.78 across windows. It uses 56% of the parameters of the unconditional world model.

Abstract: Stereo Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) recovers three-component velocity fields by triangulating tracer particles observed by synchronized cameras. It requires an accurate extrinsic calibration, which is still obtained by inserting a physical target into the flow and must be repeated whenever the rig is moved. We present a target-free method that calibrates a stereo PIV rig directly from particle image sequences. Because tracer particles are visually indistinguishable, we match motion rather than appearance: building on CUT3R, each camera stream is processed by a recurrent transformer whose persistent state accumulates particle motion over time. The state is decoded into per-camera optical flow and queried by a cross-view decoder that regresses bidirectional point correspondences, from which the relative pose is recovered with a five-point RANSAC solver. We train on a new synthetic stereo PIV dataset with ground-truth poses and evaluate on standard PIV optical flow benchmarks and on 200 real rig placements. Our model achieves lower flow error than existing PIV networks, more accurate cross-view correspondences than COTR, and higher pose accuracy than the strongest detector-matcher pipelines on both synthetic and real data, without any calibration target.

Abstract: Recognizing individual events does not guarantee correct execution of compositional instructions in streaming video. For example, given the request "Notify me when the pot boils after someone turns on the stove and leaves the kitchen", existing models may recognize the individual events yet notify too early or miss the intended moment, failing to track how their temporal conditions are satisfied over time. To evaluate this gap, we introduce CoTriggerBench, which composes annotated video events into temporally constrained instructions and verifies notification timing and silence through executable event plans and source-event replay. To address this challenge, we propose a real-time VLM agent, TEMPI. TEMPI decomposes instructions into atomic event queries and temporal constraints, and uses Scenario Memory to continuously maintain task progress as event evidence arrives, allowing earlier observations to support subsequent decisions beyond the detector's visual window. Even when no notification is required, relevant events continue to update the scenario state. On CoTriggerBench, TEMPI achieves 44.4% instruction-level success, outperforming the strongest closed-source and fine-tuned open-source VLM baselines by 15.6 (Qwen3.8-Omni-Flash-Realtime) and 18.1 (MOSS-VL-Realtime) percentage points, respectively. To support efficient on-device streaming inference, we further optimize the streaming inference pipeline by overlapping partial visual encoding and LLM prefix prefill with frame arrival. On an 8 GB RTX 5060, our optimized detector implementation achieves a 4.90$\times$ speedup.

Abstract: We address the problem of reconstructing continuous three-dimensional lipid profiles of the mouse brain from lipid measurements acquired only on a sparse set of two-dimensional tissue sections. Using MALDI-MSI measurements from coronal sections of two densely sampled and six sparsely sampled mouse brains, registered to the Allen Mouse Brain Common Coordinate Framework (CCF), we seek to interpolate the measured hundreds of lipid profiles across the unobserved voxels of the reference brain. We introduce a Neural Latent-Space Gaussian Process (LGP) architecture for this task and critically evaluate its ability to reconstruct spatial lipid distributions, using held-out mouse brain sections to assess generalization.
  We propose a Gaussian Process prior for the distribution of low-dimensional lipid profiles across the mouse brain (with Matérn or a Riemann manifold kernel). This allows us to encode 3D coordinates in a small number of latent 3D volumes that are then mapped to particular lipids using a multilayer perceptron (MLP). We performed an exhaustive analysis over a range of algorithms from simple linear regression, XGBoost, MLP to state of the art models such as Neural Transcriptomic Field, graph-based SPA3D for spatial omics reconstruction and DEEPSPATIAL. We additionally evaluate GPLFR as an alternative Gaussian-process-based approach.
  Across 173 measured lipids, LGP outperforms all evaluated baselines, including recent spatial-omics models and GPLFR, achieving a mean correlation of 0.613 and demonstrating the effectiveness of our approach for sparse 3D lipid interpolation.

Abstract: Long-video reasoning requires a system to localize sparse evidence over extended temporal sequences, preserve temporal structure, and generate answers despite substantial multimodal distractors. Existing video retrieval-augmented generation methods typically build indexes from uniformly sampled video segments. In long-video settings, this design readily introduces irrelevant context and is unreliable for questions with explicit temporal constraints. We propose VTG-RAG, a framework for long-video question answering that combines query-adaptive coarse-to-fine sampling with temporal graph retrieval. First, a two-stage sampling strategy selects core segments with high mean query relevance or high intra-segment relevance variance. The framework then integrates subtitles, speech transcripts, visual descriptions, entities, and relations from these segments into a temporally ordered multimodal graph. Finally, it performs temporally filtered, two-pass retrieval and reasoning across frame, text, and graph evidence spaces. A lot of experiments have proven the effectiveness of the VTG-RAG. Specifically, we evaluate the framework on Video-MME and LongVideoBench. On LongVideoBench, the reported Qwen2.5-VL-32B and 72B results improve from 48.16% to 50.56% and from 52.13% to 53.32%, respectively, with 24 answer-input frames. These correspond to gains of 2.40 and 1.19 percentage points. The ablation experiments of VTG-RAG further support that the contributions of each component are very important.

Abstract: Surgical workflow assistance is inherently anticipatory: systems must reason about how procedures evolve, not just recognize the current frame. However, many prior approaches perform prediction in pixel space by synthesizing future frames, which primarily focuses on appearance fidelity rather than the semantic representations required for downstream reasoning. To address this limitation, we propose a feature-space semantic forecasting framework that models temporal dynamics directly within the representations of a frozen Vision Foundation Model (VFM). Specifically, we build a hierarchical target representation from multi-layer VFM features, replace future-frame feature tokens with learnable \texttt{[MASK]} tokens, and train a masked transformer to forecast the corresponding future latent features from observed context, thereby formulating procedural evolution as masked future feature prediction. Experiments on public laparoscopic benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements in phase anticipation, multi-label tool presence prediction, and remaining-time estimation over RGB-based forecasting baselines, while enabling a plug-and-play representation interface for lightweight downstream heads.

Abstract: Cellular camera traps can alert field teams within minutes, supporting anti-poaching, threatened-species protection and human-wildlife conflict mitigation. Most systems use central servers, sending images of people and sensitive species locations outside the collecting organisation and often across national borders. We ask whether this capability can run on infrastructure owned by field organisations. Trap Tracker RT is an open-source, self-hosted application that receives images by email or locally via FTP/SFTP, classifies them with a project-specific model and sends alerts through a mail server chosen by the organisation. We evaluated DeepFaune-SSA v1.2, pairing MegaDetector v6 with a DINOv3-based classifier for 33 sub-Saharan taxa, against three open alternatives on 31,131 out-of-distribution images from Kenya, Namibia and Malawi, independent of its training data. DeepFaune-SSA achieved image-level species accuracy of 0.901 (95% CI 0.894-0.908), versus 0.530 for the global SpeciesNet classifier and 0.556 and 0.547 for YOLOv10x and YOLO26x detectors trained on the same regional data. DeepFaune-SSA had the highest recall for all 26 species evaluated. The single-stage detectors missed animals in about a quarter of images; SpeciesNet assigned species labels in only 57%, usually falling back to coarser labels otherwise. In bench tests of the fully local path, alerts were issued about 6 s after upload completion, including with slowed and interrupted uploads. The two-stage pipeline processed 19 images per minute on eight CPU threads without a graphics processor. Trap Tracker RT is used by five organisations in the United Kingdom and Kenya, including for human-elephant conflict mitigation. We discuss how data paths, rather than inference location, determine data sovereignty, the responsibilities of self-hosting and the ethics of real-time monitoring.

</details>

Abstract: In recent years, machine learning has demonstrated impressive performance in many real-world tasks, especially in computer vision and natural language processing. However, to apply them in safety-critical systems one needs formal guarantees on the neural network outputs. The Reluplex tool is proposed to verify the safety of deep neural networks (DNNs), and in case the DNN fails to give a correct output, can generate adversarial examples. Since the tool can only handle DNNs, it is necessary to extend the tool to process image data. Therefore, in this paper, we propose the Conv-Reluplex framework, which is designed to verify the convolutional layer and pooling layer in convolutional neural networks (CNNs), and generate adversarial examples when classification is misguided. We conduct several experiments on MNIST to evaluate our approaches. The results show that the original CNN is improved using the adversarial examples generated by our tool, and the precision of classification can be increased significantly.

Abstract: Single-pass primitive decoders such as LocateAnything and VFlash predict all quantized coordinate tokens of a geometric primitive in one forward pass, enabling fast, System-1 visual planning. We study what matters when such a decoder serves as a System-1 world model: a fast action-conditioned predictor that ranks candidate pushes by their predicted outcome. On ManiSkill3 PushT-v1 (512 held-out test families, 8,192 rollouts) and on human demonstrations from lerobot/pusht (2,384 test windows), we compare decoders, training losses, rigid-body projections and coordinate frames, using matched checkpoints, hierarchical bootstrap confidence intervals and Holm correction. Two factors of similar size dominate. First, expressing inputs and outputs in the SE(2) frame of the object's initial pose lowers decision regret by 1.9-2.2x for each of five decoders, and the object-frame model trained on 128 families matches the world-frame model trained on 512. Second, single-pass decoding outperforms iterative decoding: in the object frame, single-pass heads reach 0.020-0.021 regret at 1.6-1.8 ms per decision, against 0.046 for 10-step DDIM diffusion and 0.059 for KV-cached autoregression at 10.3 and 12.9 ms. The choice among single-pass heads has no measurable effect on regret, and our registered hypothesis that the frame effect exceeds the decoder spread is not supported. Soft-token expectation with an end-to-end 2D Kabsch projection gives the lowest trajectory error (11.35 px on ManiSkill3, 3.78 px on lerobot/pusht), exact rigid-body consistency, and lower regret than diffusion at every budget from 4 to 50 DDIM steps, at 3-29x lower latency.

Abstract: Aerial perception systems are increasingly expected to support diverse tasks, yet existing solutions remain fundamentally tied to task-specific outputs. Detection, segmentation, and other perception objectives are usually learned either by separate models or by sharing a backbone with multiple task heads, leaving the model itself unaware of the common structure underlying these tasks. We are interested in asking: can a single perception representation support different levels of understanding without being designed for any specific task? We notice that occupancy is a promising task-agnostic representation for drone perception. By modeling the spatial existence and semantics of the environment, it naturally bridges different perception granularities, from coarse scene understanding to fine-grained object localization. Therefore, we introduce AerialOcc, a unified occupancy-based perception model that enables multiple drone perception tasks from a single inference process, without maintaining task-specific prediction heads. To facilitate this direction, we construct AerialOcc-20K, a large-scale multi-class occupancy dataset for aerial environments. Extensive experiments show that AerialOcc achieves competitive performance across diverse perception tasks while preserving a unified architecture. Our findings demonstrate that occupancy can serve as a universal perception interface, offering a new perspective toward flexible and scalable visual intelligence for autonomous drones from a common 3D scene representation. Abstract: Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MRAG) has shown promise for Agricultural Visual Question Answering (Agri-VQA), but existing methods still face challenges in retrieving relevant knowledge while avoiding redundancy and in integrating retrieved evidence beyond simple prompt concatenation. To address these issues, we propose Agri-MRAG, a multimodal retrieval-augmented framework for agricultural VQA. Specifically, we first introduce a semantic-consistency-guided clustering mechanism that combines multimodal clustering and weighted relevance scoring to select high-confidence knowledge subsets and adaptively determine the retrieval size $k$. For evaluation, we use PlantVillageVQA as a broad agricultural benchmark and construct PlantVQA, a region-specific forestry and fruit crop VQA dataset containing 7,514 images and 15,028 expert-verified question-answer pairs. Abstract: Medical image classifiers require not only high accuracy but also reliable predictions under small input perturbations. Certified robustness provides mathematical guarantees of prediction stability, but preserving these guarantees when adapting pretrained models to new tasks remains challenging. We propose a fine-tuning approach based on quantum-inspired orthogonal butterfly layers that preserves the Lipschitz bounds of pretrained certified networks. Inspired by quantum circuits of reconfigurable beam splitters, these layers preserve orthogonality by construction using only $\mathcal{O}(n\log n)$ trainable parameters. Their structured design enables efficient classical implementation and offers a path toward future quantum implementations with logarithmic circuit depth under suitable connectivity assumptions. Controlled experiments on MNIST and MedMNIST demonstrate improved certified robustness compared with conventional alternatives and, in the DermaMNIST transfer, greater resistance to the tested attacks than low-rank adaptation (LoRA). We then apply it to trichoscopic image classification for alopecia diagnosis and alopecia areata activity assessment. By adapting a certified ImageNet-pretrained network, butterfly fine-tuning achieves approximately twice the certified accuracy of conventional fine-tuning and LoRA, with comparable classification accuracy on the same backbone and data split and similar empirical resistance to the tested adversarial attacks. Larger robustness guarantees, including certificates covering certain image-processing transformations, can be obtained at the cost of reduced accuracy. These results highlight the potential of quantum-inspired butterfly layers for parameter-efficient adaptation of medical image classifiers while preserving the mathematical guarantees underlying certified robustness.

</details>

Abstract: This report presents a complete technical solution for the UrbanTwin V2X-Real Sim2Real LiDAR Challenge, covering both synthetic point cloud generation and 3D object detection. For data generation, we adopt per-axis quantile calibration. Public V2X-Real training data are used to construct the real reference distribution, and independent one-dimensional quantile mappings are estimated for the x, y, and z axes of the base synthetic point clouds. The calibrated point clouds are then resampled using a fixed random seed and clipped to the valid spatial range, producing 50 frames with 2,000 float32 XYZ points per frame. For detection, we use a two-stage Voxel R-CNN (VoxelRCNN) detector trained with RangeLDM-enhanced synthetic point-cloud data, point clouds downsampled to 50k points per frame, and reconstructed pedestrian data. Training is performed on four NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 GPUs with a global batch size of 16 for 40 epochs. Final testing uses direct single-model inference with the original VoxelRCNN model. On the official evaluation server, the normalized Realism score is 0.7905 and the normalized Detection score is 0.5583, with mAP@0.5 of 0.3908, BEV mAP@0.5 of 0.4219, and mAP@0.7 of 0.1047. According to the official weighting rule, the final combined score is 0.6 x 0.5583 + 0.4 x 0.7905 = 0.6512. This report documents the complete implementation details of data construction, model training, inference, submission formatting, and official evaluation.

On 60 NLST chest CT pairs with coverage defects whose ground truth is computed from scan geometry, a false header that Claude Haiku 5.5 resisted when shown with the image (2% of its detections overruled) overturned 91% of them when it arrived after the model's answer (Nemotron-3.5-VL 43% to 100%). With the order reversed, the image overturned 98-100% of the header's errors (Claude models): whichever source came second won. This is not generic sycophancy: a neutral "please confirm" reversed 0 of 1,072 correct refusals, and the effect persisted when the header arrived as a tool result, as in agentic pipelines. One warning sentence cut sequential reversal to at most 9%, and the pattern held on 24 real mismatched pairs. Abstract: Medical vision--language models align radiographs with free-text reports, but radiology language distinguishes explicit findings, negation, uncertainty, and omission. We introduce CARET, a parameter-efficient image-side adaptation method that retains pretrained image and text encoders and uses paired positive and negative clinical statements to define a low-dimensional semantic correction space. Abstract: A single unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) mission over an incident site produces hundreds of images, far more than a rescue commander can review under time pressure, and human screening of destruction imagery carries a documented psychological cost. One natural-language query over a mission folder returns a ranked shortlist with per-image justifications, editable SVG overlay annotations, and a GPS map of the hits. In a qualitative evaluation on German fire-brigade field data from three real deployments and a live fire exercise, a single query condenses an entire survey mission into a handful of annotated frames that a commander can review at a glance.

Abstract: Block-sparse featurizers (BSFs) use groups of learned directions to capture the multidimensional structure of visual concepts. However, grouping alone does not ensure that information about a concept is consolidated within a single group. We introduce Guided Group Featurizers (GGF), a novel object-guided extension that uses object annotations for initialization and training while preserving the BSF architecture. We assess whether each group's strongest response regions share an object type, part or material across images. Blinded reviews give GGF groups an average semantic coherence score of $94.1\pm0.9$%, versus $83.0\pm1.9$% for SLiCS and $65.3\pm2.2$% for supervised Vanilla BSF. GGF's consolidation advantage extends to unguided categories: one selected group per category covers $65.6\pm2.4$% of target-containing crops, versus $58.2\pm2.2$% for supervised Vanilla BSF, at approximately 5% false-positive rates for both. Across COCO and VOC, GGF also improves single-group object Capture and localization over Vanilla BSFs, SLiCS and Concept-SAE. Its groups produce more focused and reproducible object responses while supporting recognition and retaining associations under rotation, scale and brightness changes. GGF also uses fewer learned parameters than Concept-SAE and shorter recorded VOC training loops than both supervised alternatives. These findings support object guidance for organizing more interpretable BSF groups and show that response concentration extends to categories excluded from guidance.

Abstract: We introduce UnSPAD, an uncertainty-aware burst photography framework capable of fusing and denoising arbitrarily long sequences of binary single photon avalanche diode (SPAD) measurements. Unlike prior methods that require fixed burst lengths or rely on powerful generative priors, UnSPAD combines learned motion estimation with uncertainty-informed measurement fusion and denoising to produce temporally consistent, hallucination-free reconstructions. UnSPAD first forms an initial scene estimate by averaging flow-aligned binary frames. UnSPAD then uses per-pixel uncertainty maps to perform uncertainty-aware denoising; the denoiser is informed about which regions accumulated the fewest observations (and thus contain the most relative shot noise) due to motion, occlusion, or alignment errors. This two-step reconstruction pipeline helps mitigate hallucinations---outside of the structure implicitly introduced by the flow estimates, the initial scenes estimates are entirely prior-free. Experiments on real and simulated SPAD and quantum image sensor (QIS) benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art reconstruction quality, improved temporal consistency, and superior color fidelity relative to the state of the art.

</details>

Abstract: Small details in digital images -- a face, a street sign, a license plate -- are routinely upscaled in hopes of making them interpretable. Upscaling options now range from conventional interpolation, whose operations are spatially invariant and fully explainable, to AI-powered methods that synthesize detail potentially absent from the source. Here, we ask which of these techniques most reliably helps human readers. In two online experiments, we measured legibility of 255 US license plates ranging in resolution from 128 to 8 pixels tall, as well as their legibility after upscaling by eight different methods. Legibility of the un-upscaled plates collapsed from ceiling to floor across a narrow band of plate heights (32 to 10 pixels). Conventional upscaling techniques (bilinear, bicubic, and Lanczos interpolation) gave modest gains of about 5-9 percentage points within this range. Nearest-neighbor interpolation, however, was harmful, rendering unreadable many plates that were readable without any upscaling. Advanced methods diverged: SwinIR, an open-source AI enhancer, raised performance by up to 38 points relative to no upscaling, while an adaptive denoiser-based image prior and commercial AI upscaler tended to impair legibility. We found no strong evidence of consistent character hallucinations across methods. Decomposing the task, conventional interpolators mainly helped readers count characters, whereas SwinIR alone made the characters themselves more identifiable.

</details>

Abstract: Violation-of-expectation (VOE) benchmarks evaluate intuitive physics in video world models by testing whether physically impossible events produce greater prediction errors than matched possible events. However, conventional VOE evaluations aggregate prediction errors across space and time, making it unclear whether successful discrimination reflects model's understanding of physical violations at the locations and time where they occur. To address this limitation, we introduce localized VOE evaluation, a framework that measures prediction error differences between possible and impossible events within spatial regions and temporal intervals associated with physical violations. We compare global, temporally localized, and spatiotemporally localized evaluations of V-JEPA models pretrained on developmental egocentric videos or large-scale natural video datasets. Our results reveal that global and localized judgments can diverge in both directions: global aggregation can either obscure correct localized discrimination or produce apparent discrimination without corresponding localized evidence. During continued pretraining on developmental egocentric videos, agreement between global and localized judgments changes non-monotonically, while confidence in localized judgments increases despite modest improvements in accuracy. Together, these findings establish localized VOE evaluation as a complementary diagnostic for assessing intuitive physics in video world models and demonstrate that global prediction errors do not necessarily reflect the spatiotemporal evidence of physical violations.

</details>

Abstract: We present Role-Separated Diffusion Transformers (RoSeDiT), a new diffusion transformer architecture that isolates the global rule from the image tokens. RoSeViT showed in visual reasoning that a vanilla ViT spreads a shared rule over all $L$ tokens at an entropy of order $Ld$, whereas an enforced controller route reduces it to order $d$. In class-conditional DiT and SiT, the class and the noise level are explicitly conditioned by adaLN, so a RoSeViT-style controller channel on the ViT side is insufficient to establish the full role separation. By introducing the adaLN listening mechanism, which makes adaLN compute under the controller's command, RoSeDiT brings the entropy theorem and role separation into the latent diffusion domain. Under the same SiT training and evaluation protocol at the same level of parameter count, RoSeDiT surpasses SiT at all four model scales at 400K steps, and RoSeDiT-L/2 even surpasses SiT-XL/2's 17.2 FID with 15.34 and 32% fewer parameters. RoSeDiT-XL/2 surpasses SiT-XL/2's 7M-step FID of 8.3 at 1.4M steps with 7.98, using one-fifth as many training steps. We further observe that models with the same denoising loss can have a large FID gap, which indicates a misalignment between the denoising objective and the image manifold. We formalize this phenomenon into Deterministic Sampling Manifold Compression (DSMC), a two-stage training schedule with which RoSeDiT-XL/2 surpasses SiT + REPA's 4M-step FID of 5.9 at 2.4M steps with 5.84.
