Abstract: Hard vision benchmarks often keep images that strong classifiers get wrong. Since a classifier is also marked wrong when the label is wrong, such failure screens may collect label errors. We measure this on ImageNet's twice-relabeled validation set with 103 classifiers. Among the 1,892 images that all 66 supervised classifiers get wrong, 61% have a wrong label, versus 11.1% overall. The fewer images a screen lets through, the more of them are mislabeled, along approximately one curve. Fit on one model family, this curve predicts the screens of two held-out model families with a mean error of 2.4 percentage points, and replays of ImageNet-D's and ImageNet-Hard's screens within 0.1 and 6.3 points. On new pools, ImageNet-V2 and CIFAR-10, enrichment at the strictest screens is stronger than the curve predicts: on ImageNet-V2, by human relabeling, 67% of the images that all 66 classifiers get wrong are mislabeled, against 46% predicted. Screens that let models choose among only some of the classes, as ImageNet-A's does (200 of 1,000), fall below the curve. By ReImageNet, 32% of ImageNet-Hard's ImageNet images have no correct label. ScreenAudit, which comprises an audit tool, a script that rebuilds corrected answer sets for the 5,464 decided ImageNet-Hard images (of 5,646) from ReImageNet's gated release, and per-image audits of 1,198 benchmark images, will be released later.

Abstract: Finding effective prompts and seeds for one-step image editors requires balancing edit fidelity, source preservation, and computational cost. To address this challenge, we explore a large collection of candidate edits, where each candidate is defined by a unique prompt-seed pair, and generate corresponding images on demand. We present SearchChord, a training-free framework that leverages a large pool of such candidates, combining text-based preselection, image verification, and latent-field recomposition. Our ablation studies across pool sizes up to 200 candidates show that a pool of 100 offers a cost-effective operating point. Compared with feeding all 100 candidates into recomposition, TextSim preselects a subset of 10 candidates. Rather than uniform averaging, we incorporate image-verifier scores to weight each candidate during recomposition. Experiments on PIE-Bench and the proposed CropWeed-EditBench reveal a trade-off: candidate selection improves target-text alignment, while recomposition better preserves unmodified source content. The oracle verifier achieves the best performance in both preservation and target-text alignment. Practical verification partially recovers semantic alignment, but recomposition still compromises edit fidelity, including in agricultural image edits. These results motivate text-first preselection and stronger verification to balance preservation, fidelity, and cost. Code and data will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: Pavement crack grid localization remains challenging for thin and tiny cracks due to weak visual cues and background interference. Box detection often misses these cracks, while supervised semantic segmentation faces limited pixel-wise annotations and high labeling costs. In this paper, we propose a Frequency-Direction-Aware Multi-Task framework (FDAM) with box detection and grid classification branches. The core grid classification branch comprises Frequency-Domain Enhancement (FDE) and Directional Feature Refinement (DFR). FDE adaptively weights spectral bands and fuses reconstructed features with spatial features. DFR captures thin crack patterns through multi-scale strip convolutions, direction-sensitive modulation, and feature recalibration. Sparse positive cells and ambiguous local predictions motivate adaptive grid supervision. Adaptive Importance Reweighting emphasizes uncertain predictions and local label transitions, with focal supervision for all cells. Our contribution is to couple the box detection and grid classification branches through shared feature learning for pavement crack grid localization. We validate FDAM on our RoadDistress2026 dataset of thin and tiny cracks and further evaluate it on public datasets Crack500 and DeepCrack. The code is available at https://github.com/Lab-PANbin/FDAM

Abstract: Omnidirectional ($360^\circ$) cameras capture the full surrounding scene in every frame, providing a strong advantage for visual odometry (VO) over pinhole sensors with a limited field of view. However, their equirectangular projection (ERP) introduces latitude-dependent distortion, an artificial seam at the image border, and a sampling bias toward the poles. Existing $360^\circ$ VO methods address these challenges only partially. DEFDVO closes this gap with a patch-based deep VO method that operates directly on ERP imagery, combining spherically initialized deformable feature extraction, end-to-end seam-aware estimation, and spherical-gradient patch selection. The deformable feature extractor combines spherical geometric priors with learned sampling offsets, while seam-aware processing preserves ERP continuity, and spherical-gradient selection reduces polar sampling bias. Trained exclusively on synthetic TartanAirV2 ERP imagery, DEFDVO improves the best prior average absolute trajectory error (ATE) by $4.8\times$ (0.201 m to 0.042 m) on the TartanAirV2 evaluation set defined by 360DVO and achieves the lowest average ATE on both splits of the real-world 360DVO benchmark. In this paper, we present DecoEdit, an all-in-one image editing framework that goes beyond flat control by seamlessly integrating 1D textual, 2D spatial, and 3D volumetric inputs under a unified architecture. First, to power this unified model, we establish a data curation pipeline and curate a large-scale dataset, namely DecoDataset, containing 1.6 million samples, encompassing diverse multi-dimensional (1D/2D/3D) tasks and their complex compositions. Furthermore, we establish a comprehensive image editing benchmark named DecoBench to systematically evaluate multi-task editing performance. Finally, to address the fundamental trade-off between complex spatial transformations and identity preservation, we propose a novel decoupled pipeline: DecoEdit factorizes the editing process by utilizing structural guidance from SAM-3D to anchor the target geometry, scale, and spatial orientation, while concurrently leveraging a detail-preservation path to lock in high-fidelity textures. Abstract: Open-vocabulary remote sensing image segmentation (OVRSIS) enables the segmentation of unseen classes without additional annotation or retraining. By analyzing the predictions of existing OVRSIS methods, we find that many misclassified patches coexist with visually similar, correctly classified patches of the same class within the same image. Unfortunately, most of these methods stop after a single inference rather than using their predictions to guide further inference. Motivated by this observation, we propose \textbf{Puzzle}, a plug-and-play iterative re-inference framework that combines prediction history with a visual-similarity prior to progressively refine patch-level attention. Puzzle employs a lightweight history-conditioned controller to regulate each patch's contribution as an information source and its reliance on visually similar patches, while an adaptive stopping rule determines when to stop. It keeps the segmentation model frozen and reuses the same controller across different models and datasets without retraining. Extensive experiments demonstrate consistent performance improvements across diverse OVRSIS models and datasets, highlighting the strong transferability of Puzzle. Moreover, Puzzle can further improve the performance of SOTA methods.

</details>

Abstract: Video-based fall detection could support timely assistance when a person cannot call for help. Unfamiliar cameras and scarce local labels challenge deployment. We evaluate three vision-language models (VLMs), Qwen, LLaVA, and InternVL, and three visual baselines, CLIP-temporal, VideoMAE, and Pose-TCN. We evaluate the stored source model without target data for training or selection. For each label budget, we update a separate copy using labeled clips from the held-out target dataset. We call this target-site personalization. We compare the Source-initialized model with Target-only, which skips source training but uses identical target clips and updates. Across nine targets, source training raises mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) from frozen-model values of 0.821 to 0.912 for Qwen, 0.883 to 0.947 for LLaVA, and 0.939 to 0.962 for InternVL. With five target examples per class and 26 updates per run, 95% confidence intervals for the AUROC benefit over Target-only lie above zero for Qwen, CLIP-temporal, VideoMAE, and Pose-TCN. With full-target training, they lie above zero only for Qwen and LLaVA. Separate VLM analyses reveal threshold sensitivity and post-fall false detections. The benefit varies by model and label budget.

Abstract: Logical anomalies arise when every component looks normal but their combination violates the scene rules. In practical inspection, these rules must hold under varying backgrounds, illumination, and imaging quality, with only a few dozen normal images per scene, whereas existing methods couple normality with appearance and merge different constraints into one reference. We model logical normality as decomposable constraints and propose EviRef, a training-free method that builds a separate reference memory for each family of evidence. Component prototypes built once per scene parse images under any capture condition into one label space, on which occupancy and relation constraints are verified. Semantic constraints are verified with whole-image embeddings independent of parsing, and additional evidence such as text is admitted through a task-level gate without modifying existing memories. On VID-AD, EviRef attains a mean image-level area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 92.1% (88.9% with visual evidence alone), the highest among the compared methods under each of five capture conditions, with a standard deviation of 0.5. Abstract: JEPA-based world models predict action consequences in latent space, but representative planning approaches rely on candidate-action search, requiring repeated evaluation of action sequences and incurring substantial online computational cost. Direct action generation avoids this search, yet how to leverage latent predictions to guide action generation given task goals remains a key challenge in translating learned physical dynamics into effective decisions. We introduce WA-JEPA, a world-action model that couples action generation, action-conditioned latent prediction, and goal-aware feedback. The model first generates an action proposal and predicts the resulting future scenes and proprioceptive states, then refines the proposal through two complementary feedback paths, using the task goal as a reference. Goal-discrepancy residual feedback (GDRF) uses differences between the latent representations of predicted scenes and the task goal to guide chunk-wise action refinement. Future-scene context feedback (FSCF) preserves fine-grained predicted scene representations to guide subsequent action refinement. Jointly learned from offline demonstrations, this process iteratively updates the same plan before execution, making future predictions directly participate in action generation without candidate-action search. We evaluate short-horizon goal reaching, long-horizon tasks, and full-task execution. The results show that goal-aware predictive feedback supports competitive task performance while maintaining a compact model and low online computational cost. Code is available at https://github.com/Dovisya/WA-JEPA.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly being evaluated for autonomous driving through question-answering benchmarks, yet high benchmark accuracy does not necessarily reflect visually grounded reasoning. We present a diagnostic evaluation framework that systematically probes whether VLMs base safety-critical driving decisions on visual evidence or instead rely on language priors, answer biases, and dataset regularities. Our benchmark comprises two task categories, Adverse Weather and Junctions \& Intersections, with a nuScenes spatial reasoning extension, and evaluates open-source (general, driving, and spatial-reasoning) VLMs along four diagnostic protocols: image versus no-image evaluation, counterfactual weather sensitivity, linguistic robustness under paraphrasing and negation, and spatial reasoning. All ground-truth labels are derived programmatically from BDD100K and DriveLM annotations, enabling reproducible evaluation without manual labeling. Across all models, we find that competitive overall accuracy conceals systematic failure modes, including strong affirmation bias, limited sensitivity to counterfactual scene changes, near-chance performance on negated questions despite high paraphrase consistency, and confident driving decisions even without visual input. These results demonstrate that current open-source VLMs remain insufficiently grounded for safety-critical autonomous driving and underscore the need for diagnostic evaluation beyond aggregate accuracy.

Abstract: Self-supervised monocular depth networks can predict a per-pixel uncertainty, which is mainly evaluated by how well it ranks pixels according to their error. Such metrics are insensitive to the magnitude of the uncertainty, so an uncertainty half as large as the actual error obtains the same score. In this paper, we investigate whether the predicted uncertainty is as large as the depth error, both on average (scale) and across confident and uncertain, near and far pixels (shape). On KITTI, the scale is miscalibrated for every uncertainty estimate we evaluate, and the error of MonoProb is nearly twice its predicted uncertainty. We propose MonoCal, which corrects both the scale and the shape without ground-truth depth. Scale calibration multiplies the uncertainty by a single constant measured from stereo pairs, requiring no retraining, and this constant closely matches the one fitted with ground truth. Since a constant cannot change the shape, we further retrain the uncertainty to predict where the depth of the network disagrees with that of a second self-supervised network. Experiments on KITTI show that MonoCal achieves better calibration across uncertainty levels and depth ranges than rescaling with ground truth, while still using a single image at inference. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: Quantitative susceptibility mapping (QSM) is a novel quantitative MRI contrast for studying tissue susceptibility changes in brain disorders, including Parkinson's disease (PD) and multiple sclerosis (MS). However, small cohorts and the lack of local annotations make developing deep learning models for QSM-based diagnosis challenging. In this work, we propose PoRef-MIL, a weakly supervised multiple instance learning (MIL) framework combining iterative slice relabeling and network retraining with a plug-and-play posterior refinement module (PRM). During training, a 2D dual-branch diagnosis network and a subject-level classifier were alternately trained for five rounds. The classifier was used to update slice pseudo-labels for retraining the diagnosis network. The PRM was applied during inference to refine slice probabilities through bidirectional posterior inference and calculate entropy-derived confidence from the refined probabilities, which was then used in sliding-window scoring to obtain subject-level predictions. PoRef-MIL was evaluated on an in-house PD cohort of 287 participants and a public MS cohort of 150 participants. Compared with eight classification methods, PoRef-MIL achieved the highest mean areas under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.942 and 0.872, and accuracies of 0.926 and 0.833, for PD and MS classification, respectively. Ablation experiments were conducted to validate the effectiveness of iterative relabeling and PRM, and the plug-and-play feature of the proposed PRM was also evaluated using four state-of-the-art MIL models. Overall, PoRef-MIL offers a promising approach to QSM-based disease classification in small cohorts without manual slice annotations.

Abstract: Recent pre-trained vision-language models often encounter a Multi-Domain Task-Incremental Learning (MTIL) scenario in practice, where a set of classes of multi-modal tasks arrive incrementally. Due to privacy concerns and memory constraints, MTIL with pre-trained models encounters forgetting of knowledge from old tasks, degradation of zero-shot transfer capability, and underfitting of new-task knowledge. To overcome these challenges, previous MTIL methods attempt to learn a discriminative cross-task identification (CTI) module and an effective new-task adaptation (NTA) module. However, current CTI modules suffer from severe task confusion between seen and unseen tasks, and NTA modules cannot adaptively balance the performance and parameter cost while incorporating task-specific knowledge. To alleviate the above dilemmas, we propose an effective and efficient TSP-DLoRA method for MTIL, which consists of Task Subspace Projection (TSP) and Dynamic Low Rank Adapter (DLoRA) modules. Specifically, our TSP module includes a task identifier classifier based on task-specific subspaces and a feature projection strategy that can determine the identifier associated with samples from both seen and unseen tasks. Our DLoRA improves the knowledge adaptation from new tasks by dynamically assigning Low Rank Adapter (LoRA) across transformer layers based on the task distributions. Experimental evaluations across 11 datasets, using three performance metrics, demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed method. Code is available at https://github.com/FerdinandZJU/TSP-DLoRA.

Abstract: Visual brain decoding translates neural activity into human-interpretable images. While recent EEG-to-image reconstruction methods rely on aligning EEG signals with pretrained visual embeddings to condition an image generator, we empirically demonstrate that stronger cross-modal alignment do not translate into comparable gains in reconstruction quality. This indicates that improving the input condition alone is insufficient; the generative process itself requires explicit supervision. Although Representation Alignment (REPA) offers a solution by guiding diffusion transformers with pretrained vision encoder features, we find that EEG-conditioned generators struggle to follow these fixed targets. To address this mismatch, we introduce generation-derived supervision (GDS): an image-conditioned teacher that translates static image-encoder features into the internal representation space of a diffusion transformer. Our EEG-conditioned student generator closely tracks these dynamic targets during denoising, discarding the teacher at inference. Evaluated on THINGS-EEG and THINGS-MEG, our approach consistently outperforms conditioning-centric baselines, achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction fidelity.

Abstract: For more than half a century, video games have pursued better graphics, more natural interaction, and more engaging gameplay. Generative video models (VMs) now bear on all three. Neural networks already draw most of the pixels in shipped games, and recent world models let a lightweight game engine keep the world state while a VM turns the engine's coarse proxy video into final frames. These systems, however, treat the VM as a renderer that only restyles motion the engine has already authored, which leaves most of its generative capacity unused and limits the diversity a game can offer. We present GameWM, a design for next-generation games built on a clear division of labor. The GameWM-engine keeps what must be exact: rules, state, camera, and the layout of the world, which also spares the video model from having to remember it. The GameWM-VM generates what is expensive to author by hand: the look of the world and the detail of the player's interactions. One game mechanic can then be realized in many visually and physically distinct ways, while its outcome stays exactly as the engine computed it. To realize this division of labor, we build GameWM as a complete system: a data engine centered on interactions rather than exploration, an interface through which authored character sheets, scene art, and skill descriptions enter the model, and a model family from 5B to 33B parameters with a real-time causal variant for play. Together they give every player a fresh version of the same game: each player's own character, world, and skills turn the same engine mechanics into a new experience, while every event keeps the timing and outcome the GameWM-engine computed.

</details>

Abstract: Hybrid vision backbones interleave Mamba state-space mixers with self-attention, and a block's role is usually probed by removing it and measuring the damage. We test what that measurement means in MambaVision. On all 50,000 ImageNet validation images and three model sizes, every block ablation is paired with a size-matched null, the same perturbation with its channels shuffled or their signs flipped. In top-1 accuracy and in true-class rank, real and null damage are nearly indistinguishable in absolute terms: pooled over the blocks of MambaVision-T, the content effect lies within [-0.044,+0.016] percentage points (90% interval). Two attention-only controls show that the test detects content, with real ablations worse than their nulls by up to 4.2 points in DeiT-Small and 1.0 point in Swin-T. Part of MambaVision's near-equivalence reflects how little damage its blocks do (0.11--0.34 points on average, against 1.7 and 2.1). Scaled by damage, its content share is comparable to Swin-T's in accuracy, lower in rank, and far below DeiT-Small's: single-block damage in MambaVision is small and mostly positional. Ablation also hides what blocks carry together: removing all blocks of one type compounds damage to 3.7--8.4 times the sum of single effects, far more than size-matched nulls, including nulls that keep the alignment between the blocks' perturbations; for adjacent pairs, aligned nulls reproduce most of it. Freezing input-dependent selection in all Mamba mixers shows that it accounts for 20--32% of the Mamba contribution, mainly through the B and C projections. We turn these results into a protocol for causal attribution in hybrid networks.

Abstract: Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) is essential to numerous vision applications operating under adverse illumination. Despite substantial advances, real-world nighttime enhancement remains challenging due to the scarcity of strictly aligned day-night pairs and complex degradations involving extreme darkness and heterogeneous artificial light sources, leading to fragile generalization. To overcome these challenges, we propose MrLIE (MLLM-empowered reference Low-light Image Enhancement), a novel reference-based unsupervised LLIE framework that synergizes the generative and reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with conditional diffusion. MrLIE employs the MLLM as a high-fidelity reference synthesizer and diagnostic critic, iteratively generating, diagnosing, and correcting normal-light references directly from degraded nighttime observations. Based on this, our method governs the enhancement process through two complementary mechanisms, in which a confidence offset module rectifies spatial-geometric inconsistencies for reference reliability and a structure-anchored injection scheme harmonizes aligned reference cues, low-light structural constraints, and ControlNet additive anchoring during the denoising process. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that MrLIE achieves superior perceptual quality, natural appearance, and robust generalization.

Abstract: Generative models increasingly write a chain of thought before drawing, and report large gains from doing so. We ask what that text contributes, by substitution rather than ablation: holding the generator, sampler and seeds fixed, we replace the think block with controlled alternatives and measure the image. Across a unified model with a think mode, a reinforcement-learned reasoning generator, and a planner driving a frozen diffusion model, the same picture emerges. Content-free prose of matched length is worth nothing or less ($-0.05$ to $-0.15$). One sentence from an unrelated 8B model, naming the subject and a few identifying details, matches the generator's own plan in three to six times fewer conditioning tokens. The value sits where the referent is in doubt: writing down a name is worth $+0.33$ when it is right and $+0.05$ when it is wrong, and where the generator already succeeds the whole effect is $-0.07 \pm 0.07$. Cutting a plan at the text encoder's window leaves 272 of 286 images byte-identical, and a plan rewritten to fit that window ties a bare identification ($+0.003 \pm 0.044$), so a plan contains nothing the identification does not already supply. Field-level interventions find only colour executed reliably, and a control writing the same sentences into the user prompt shows that limit belongs to the generator rather than to the planning channel. Two judges and two annotators replicate the central contrasts. A think block earns its keep as a specification of what to draw, not as a plan that is carried out.

Abstract: Robust 3D lane detection requires accurate metric road geometry recovery from visual inputs, yet conventional RGB-based approaches suffer from fragile lane evidence under adverse illumination, motion blur, and long-range perspective compression. Event cameras offer complementary high-temporal-resolution, high dynamic-range structural cues that are robust to these challenges, but their sparse, motion-dependent responses cannot be directly fused into consistent 3D geometry. To enable systematic research, we first build two multimodal benchmarks with metric 3D lane annotations: DSEC-3DLD (real-world sequences) and Ev-OpenLane (large-scale simulated sequences). We further propose RETR, a structure preserving transformer that converts complementary RGB and event observations into coherent 3D lane geometry. RETR first aligns modality consistent evidence through Reciprocal Context Flow Fusion, then preserves thin and uncertain lane structures with Uncertainty Aware Structural Consolidation, and finally decodes ordered lane hypotheses using a Geometry State Decoder with proposal conditioned initialization, reference conditioned query evolution, and diversified geometric inquiry. Abstract: Modern autonomous driving and driver assistance systems rely predominantly on cameras, radars and LiDARs for environmental perception. However, these modalities face different environmental limitations, including poor illumination for cameras and adverse weather for LiDAR. Acoustic sensing, particularly using microphone arrays, could provide complementary perception capabilities for autonomous driving, but has remained largely underexplored. In this work, we design a horizontally arranged microphone array that functions as the "ears" of a vehicle, enabling localization of nearby moving vehicles in a bird's-eye view (BEV) representation that captures both their relative direction and distance. Using this array, we collect a real-world driving dataset, AcousticScenes, which contains 2,117 static and 2,863 dynamic samples with synchronized spatial acoustic signals, LiDAR point clouds and 3D bounding box annotations. Building on this dataset, we propose AcousticBEV, a learning-based method for acoustic BEV object localization, which estimates the center locations of moving traffic participants in BEV from the microphone-array signals, conditioned on the ego vehicle's own speed. Experimental results demonstrate that AcousticBEV outperforms both classical signal-processing baselines and other learning-based methods, achieving F1 scores of $47.7 \pm 1.2$% on "Easy" scenarios and $45.1 \pm 1.4$% on "Difficult" cases. Our findings demonstrate that acoustic signals from a low-cost, vehicle-mounted microphone array can localize nearby moving vehicles in both azimuth and distance.

Abstract: Diffusion models can memorize and reproduce training samples during inference, raising serious privacy and copyright concerns. Existing inference-time mitigation methods often suppress conditional information globally, which may reduce memorization but can also harm semantic alignment and image quality. In this paper, we revisit memorization from the perspective of cross-attention heads. Through systematic analysis and targeted ablation, we find that memorization is mainly driven by a small subset of shallow-layer attention heads with abnormally strong semantic control, rather than by the entire cross-attention mechanism. Motivated by this finding, we propose Projection-based Top-k Attention Head Suppression, a targeted inference-time mitigation method. Our method first identifies the top-k attention heads most related to memorization using a semantic-control score, and then removes their condition-specific components through directional projection between conditional and unconditional representations. This design selectively weakens memorization-related features while preserving useful semantic information. Extensive experiments on different diffusion models and datasets show that our method effectively reduces memorization and achieves a better trade-off between memorization mitigation and image quality than existing approaches.

</details>

Abstract: Image steering aims to provide fine-grained control over concepts in generated images, enabling targeted and continuous manipulation without broadly altering the generation process. Recent steering methods for diffusion models primarily focus on the text-conditioning space or rely on costly auxiliary training. Deviating from that, we here propose a novel steering approach that extracts visual concept directions directly from the image-side activations of Diffusion Transformers. Specifically, we showcase few-shot concept extraction in the activation space using a simple difference-of-means estimator over intermediate residuals. We observe (a) scene-level concepts such as lighting, weather, and style are distributed across image tokens, as well as (b) local concepts such as object colors, textures, materials, and facial attributes are concentrated in specific regions. Towards controlling both concepts, we introduce a new unified training-free framework that uses global difference-of-means for (a) distributed concepts and masked difference-of-means for (b) local concepts, preventing local signals from being diluted by global averaging. Notably, using only a handful of positive and negative prompt pairs, our method identifies and extracts reusable concept directions that can be injected at inference for continuous steering. Quantitatively, our method outperforms text-encoder steering and training-free activation-editing baselines, as well as is competitive with specialized editing models. Our results suggest that as concept directions can be applied at patch level, our method enables precise targeted and compositional steering, manipulating multiple concepts in the same image.

Abstract: Hyperspectral models of fish freshness rarely transfer between species, and labelling a new species is slow and destructive. Freshness is also ordinal, and standard domain alignment can erase that order. We present InvarOrd, which adapts to a new species from 30 labelled cubes (5 per rank) in three stages: self-supervised spectral pretraining on unlabelled source cubes, cross-species alignment by a shrinkage covariance discrepancy computed only within matched ranks, and ordinal distillation into a compact student. The alignment term is provably invariant to the rank means, so it gives no incentive to collapse the ordinal axis. On paired salmon and cod data (800 cubes each, 16 days), a source-trained teacher reaches 1.6 to 1.9 days mean absolute error (MAE) on the other species, with quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) below zero. The 423K-parameter student, 2.54 times smaller than its teacher, attains the lowest MAE (1.476 days) and highest within 1-day accuracy (56.1%) among 14 configurations, matching the best of six ImageNet-pretrained backbones (ConvNeXt-T, 1.503 days) at 66 times fewer parameters, 1.44ms per cube on a GPU and 5s re-adaptation. InvarOrd beats target-only training by 0.29 days (p = 0.031). Among alignment objectives, rank-invariant alignment retains the most ordinal structure (embedding rank correlation 0.183, QWK 0.112), whereas global discrepancy lowers the rank correlation to 0.049 and QWK to zero at only 0.014 days higher MAE.

Abstract: 3D object detection is critical for intelligent transportation systems. LiDAR has been widely adopted for accurate geometric measurements; however, purely geometric representations remain ambiguous under sparse observations and occlusions, while cross-sensor multimodal fusion suffers from calibration and synchronization errors. To overcome these challenges, we leverage an Ouster LiDAR to construct PALIN (Pixel-Aligned LiDAR-NIR), a large-scale intrinsically pixel-aligned multimodal dataset jointly capturing geometry and NIR intensity, comprising approximately 8,000 annotated frames from static urban intersections and diverse mobile suburban scenes. Building upon this dataset, we propose CMambaFusion, a novel fusion framework consisting of a four-directional Cross-Mamba module for bidirectional cross-modal interaction and a Gated Concat-Mamba module for joint feature refinement. Abstract: RGB and thermal infrared (TIR) sensors provide complementary observations, yet their distinct sensing characteristics make effective cross-modal coordination challenging. Although existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can process paired RGB-T inputs, they often fail to translate cross-modal relations into effective task-specific decisions, leaving the potential of these relations to improve task performance and robustness largely untapped. Motivated by this observation, we introduce RelMLLM, an RGB-T MLLM that explicitly learns to exploit cross-modal relations in task-specific decision making. It represents modality reliability, the cross-modal relation, and the dominant modality as a verifiable decision state. We derive task-grounded supervision for this state by comparing RGB-only, TIR-only, and paired RGB-T task outcomes. A hierarchical relation-guided reward supervises modality-specific evidence, relation-state prediction, and the final task output, while encouraging consistency across evidence, relation, and decision. We evaluate RelMLLM on natural RGB-T grounding, RGB-T visual question answering, and held-out sensor degradation. RelMLLM improves grounding accuracy and degradation robustness over its Qwen3-VL baselines while preserving performance on general RGB-T reasoning benchmarks. These results demonstrate that effectively exploiting cross-modal relations leads to more accurate and robust RGB-T decisions. Code and model weights are publicly available.

Abstract: Automated Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) image classification provides a crucial non-invasive approach for neurological diagnosis, yet it is fundamentally challenged by inherent acquisition noise and high inter-subject structural similarity, which obscure subtle pathological variations, thereby limiting the performance of deep neural networks. While contrastive learning (CL) offers a promising mitigation strategy, existing paradigms rely on rigid binary partitions that ignore negative sample heterogeneity, and they fail to capture the cross-view structural consistency vital for fine-grained anatomical alignment. To address these barriers, we propose Dual-Branch Hierarchical Contrastive Learning (DBHiCo), a novel framework explicitly designed to collaboratively enforce structural consistency and semantic discrimination. Specifically, DBHiCo leverages a natural anatomical multi-view pair (original and skull-stripped MRI scans), processing them through a dual-branch architecture to seamlessly integrate holistic structural robustness with localized intracranial details. Crucially, we introduce a hierarchical contrastive loss that discards conventional binary constraints in favor of a margin-ranked similarity hierarchy. This mechanism explicitly orders positive and negative pairs based on their structural and semantic difficulty, thereby amplifying the fine-grained semantic discriminability of the learned representations. Extensive experiments across three diverse neurological datasets demonstrate that DBHiCo achieves state-of-the-art performance, consistently outperforming existing MRI multi-view representation learning and advanced contrastive fine-tuning methods.

</details>

Abstract: Analyzing whole-slide images (WSIs) requires combining evidence across tissue regions and spatial scales. Multi-agent systems offer a promising approach to this task. However, existing systems make limited use of molecular evidence, often select visually prominent but less representative regions while overlooking global context, and struggle to reuse experience from previous cases. To address these challenges, we present PathLoop, a self-evolving multi-agent framework that integrates omics data for evidence-grounded WSI analysis. Specifically, PathLoop coordinates visual and omics interpreters across slide, patch, and pixel levels, using molecular signals to guide region selection and combining local tissue details with global slide features. A judgment agent then checks the consistency of the collected evidence against Pathology-Wiki, a knowledge base built from 48 entries across 24 organ systems, and triggers re-examination when the evidence is inconsistent. To reuse past experience, a four-level memory stores reasoning trajectories, practical tips, skills, and tools to guide future cases, forming a loop of evidence collection, review, and memory update. We evaluate PathLoop on four TCGA cohorts with biomarker prediction, survival prediction, and subtype classification. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of PathLoop across these tasks and support its use for evidence-grounded WSI analysis.

We propose the Consensus-Calibrated Explanation Map (CCEM), which fuses Grad-CAM++, Ada-SISE, and SmoothGrad Integrated Gradients (SG-IG) using image-specific weights derived from annotation-free reliability cues: deletion sensitivity, inter-method agreement, compactness, retinal containment, and peak structure. On the 193 images with annotated lesions, CCEM obtains the highest Soft Pointing Game score (69.61%; 95% CI 63.7-75.3), defined as the ratio of the strongest lesion-supported saliency to the global map maximum rather than a lesion detection rate, together with the lowest deletion AUC (0.570) and the best deletion-insertion balance (0.115); each gain is significant against the strongest single explainer on paired tests. Annotation-free, per-image reliability weighting therefore turns three disagreeing explainers into one map whose peak evidence is more lesion-supported and more faithful to the model than that of any single explainer.

In this paper, we propose a novel multi-level map generation framework that can automatically generate accurate and consistent maps with multiple zoom levels from aerial images. It consists of a level-aware map generator and a consistency-aware map generator. The level-aware map generator is able to generate accurate initial maps with realistic details for each zoom level. The consistency-aware map generator regards initial maps at each zoom level as a sequence and builds the connection between them, so as to guarantee content consistency between maps at different zoom levels. Abstract: Purpose: Surgical training benefits from explicit comparison between cases: in patient condition, in technique and in skill proficiency. Surgical vision-language models (VLMs) offer a promising and flexible interface for this purpose. Yet current VLMs typically see one video at a time, and they often fall short when asked to compare videos. Methods: We propose EndoComparer, a VLM for comparative surgical video question answering with two components. (a) A Pseudo-Siamese Visual LoRA (PSVL) gives each video slot its own low-rank adapter over a shared frozen vision encoder, separating the two videos in the visual pathway at the cost of a single encoder pass. (b) A Perceive-then-Compare chain of thought (PtC-CoT) trains the model to first state what each video shows, then derive the answer from these statements. We further curate EndoDiffVQA, a benchmark of nearly 11K multiple-choice questions on video-pair over cholecystectomy and gastric bypass procedures, covering anatomy, tool, action and skill. Results: EndoComparer outperforms strong generalist and surgical VLMs on detecting and specifying difference between videos. Ablations show that two proposed components contribute complimentarily, and the model remains more robust under a video-swap control that exposes confusion between the two videos. Conclusion: Providing a dedicated visual reasoning path through PSVL and PtC-CoT makes cross-case comparison of surgical videos more robust for VLMs. EndoDiffVQA and EndoComparer provide a benchmark and a strong baseline for this task. Abstract: Effective planning requires representations that preserve task-relevant geometry and object identity while ignoring visual variation that does not alter the task. Existing evaluations of visual generalization often change several factors simultaneously, making the source of a failure difficult to identify. We introduce a controlled evaluation of object-goal navigation (ObjectNav), the task of reaching an instance of a named object category, in procedurally generated ProcTHOR houses. Training and evaluation houses share floor plans, object locations, goals, and start states, while visual factors are changed individually or in combination. Across model-free reinforcement learning (RL), model-based RL, and agents using frozen pretrained visual representations, our results show that appearance changes impair navigation, but their effects differ sharply by factor. Surface material changes account for most of the average change in success, whereas clutter and lighting have little effect. The magnitude of a global image statistics shift does not reliably predict performance: changing only the goal object's appearance can substantially impair an end-to-end agent despite a small image-level change. We found that a model based agent that plans over frozen features from the pretrained DINOv2 image encoder achieves the highest success (0.83) under the full shift, but remains sensitive to surface appearance. Our experiments reveal an important requirement for planning representations: they must separate invariant spatial task structure from appearance.

</details>

Abstract: Vision models are trained on 2D images of a 3D world. Do their hidden features encode the direction from which the camera views a scene, and if so, in what form? We study the relative viewing direction $α$ of two objects in rendered scenes where both object centres stay at fixed pixels while the camera moves, so that perspective, shading and shadows change but the projected layout does not. In eight pretrained models, from diffusion models to self-supervised and image-text encoders, $α$ is encoded in a strikingly simple form: a single two-dimensional plane in the hidden space, shared across scenes, in which the difference between the two objects' features traces $(\cosα, \sinα)$. The plane keeps its orientation when lighting, colours, floors and object shapes change. This encoding is more than a summary of a few visible measurements: readouts fitted on one set of camera paths stay accurate at camera positions outside their range in seven of the eight models, whereas decoders of the measured object and shadow geometry, equally accurate within that range, fail there. In the diffusion transformer PixArt-$α$, the plane forms abruptly at a single block and carries direction information to later blocks, yet editing it changes the objects rather than the generated viewpoint. We also examine how this encoding carries over to scanned real objects.

Abstract: Conventional experience replay typically applies fixed preservation constraints to historical exemplars, overlooking variations in how learned representations, predictions, and anatomical structures change during sequential training. To address this limitation, we propose Drift-Conditioned Structural Replay Loss (DCSRL), an exemplar-adaptive supervision mechanism that regulates anatomical preservation according to source-referenced knowledge drift. DCSRL operates within a reliability-guided replay framework that selects informative historical exemplars using anatomical content and boundary characteristics while adaptively regulating replay during training. For each retained exemplar, DCSRL constructs a frozen source-task reference signature and subsequently measures changes in feature representations, class probabilities, boundary responses, and predicted anatomical structures. These complementary drift measurements are aggregated and normalized to generate bounded, exemplar-specific coefficients that dynamically adjust boundary and structural replay losses without modifying the underlying memory-selection policy. We evaluate DCSRL under sequential learning across CAMUS, CardiacNet, and EchoNet-Dynamic, comparing its performance with regularization-, distillation-, and replay-based continual-learning approaches. Experimental findings indicate that DCSRL improves overall final segmentation performance and anatomical boundary preservation, particularly under heterogeneous imaging conditions, although trade-offs remain in knowledge transfer across tasks. These findings suggest that source-referenced multidimensional drift conditioning offers a promising direction for adaptive anatomical knowledge preservation in continual cardiac ultrasound segmentation.

</details>

Abstract: Auroras provide remote optical observations of energetic electrons precipitating into the upper atmosphere, and whose energy distribution encodes key information about large scale magnetospheric processes. Recovering the underlying electron precipitation field from ground-based camera images of auroras remains a challenging inverse problem. Existing auroral tomography techniques are often limited to simplified static settings, discrete spatial and energy grids, or strong assumptions on the electron energy spectrum. We propose to model the dynamic auroral electron precipitation field via a temporally conditioned neural network. The state of the precipitation field at each frame is represented by a per-frame latent code driven by learned discrete dynamics. A shared decoder maps horizontal positions and latent states to electron energy spectra. The predicted flux is converted to multi-wavelength volume emission rates and rendered into camera images accounting for delayed emissions. Abstract: Video world models promise to predict the future of a scene from its present. Do current models keep this promise? We probe this question counterfactually. When a small change to the present flips the future, such as a box resting a few millimeters before or past a table edge, a model that tracks the state of the world must flip its prediction too. We build FlipTest, a three-level hierarchy of increasingly strict tests over 1,680 initial frames from 10 simulated and 15 real-world tasks. At the base, visual minimal pairs, two nearly identical frames whose true futures differ, test whether predictions change across the boundary; matched controls go further and test whether they change only where the future does; and dense sweeps, the strictest level, locate exactly where they change. All 11 video world models we evaluate fall short. The best averages 18.3% pair accuracy over real and simulated scenes, below the 25% of random guessing. Models flip as often on steps that leave the future unchanged as on steps that change it, and their response stays flat across the sweeps. On the same frames, a vision-language model is selective and traces a near S-shaped curve near the boundary, and a coding agent that measures the image comes close to the true boundary. This implies that the deciding information is in the frame, and video models just miss it. We therefore urge the community to rethink world modeling as pure video prediction. Next-frame prediction must do more than draw plausible pixels; it must also identify the causal states that control future dynamics.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models map visual observations, robot states, and natural-language instructions to robotic actions, but their multimodal interfaces introduce new security risks. Existing VLA backdoor attacks primarily study trigger-based activation and stealthy action manipulation, leaving conditional suppression of malicious behavior underexplored. We investigate suppression-aware backdoors, which combine conjunctive trigger activation with instruction-conditioned suppression. We instantiate this design through three variants: Cross-Modal Trigger, combining robot-state and spatially separated visual triggers; Self-Suppressing Trigger, requiring a state trigger and a [DISRUPT] instruction token for activation, with [RESTORE] token suppressing the attack; and Multi-Trigger, additionally requiring a visual trigger while retaining suppression even when physical triggers persist. We evaluate these attacks using SmolVLA on two SO-100 manipulation datasets against four baselines: State Backdoor, BadVLA, TabVLA, and SilentDrift. Self-Suppressing and Multi-Trigger reduce Isolation Forest detection from 96--98% to 1--18% and action-space l divergence by 10--14X, achieving 63--71% attack success rates with perfect clean-task performance. Experiments with OpenVLA on LIBERO further show that the attacks transfer to a 7B-parameter policy, evading STRIP and action-distribution-shift detection. Our findings establish conditional suppression as an important dimension for evaluating backdoor threats in VLA policies.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) and World-Action Models (WAMs) have emerged in autonomous driving with the promise of strong generalization to long-tail scenarios. Although these models achieve state-of-the-art results on autonomous driving benchmarks, most existing benchmarks are dominated by nominal scenarios and therefore cannot test this promise. To test one such long-tail capability that is critical for safety, we introduce ReactBench-AD, a benchmark that measures how driving models react to objects that appear in the ego vehicle's path. Built on real NAVSIM and PhysicalAI-Autonomous-Vehicles scenes, ReactBench-AD composites a crossing pedestrian, a standing pedestrian, or a stationary car consistently across all camera views and history frames. Each edited scene thus forms an exact, pixel-level counterfactual pair with its original, which allows us to isolate the causal effect of the inserted object on the predicted trajectory. Evaluating four VLAs and two WAMs, we find that they react weakly, often barely at all. Finally, we test how easily one of the evaluated VLAs, ReCogDrive, can be taught to brake hard for imminent hazards: a short fine-tuning stage on edited scenes substantially improves its reactions, with the learned behavior generalizing to unseen objects and fully real scenes, at minimal cost to nominal driving. These results call into question the out-of-the-box long-tail capabilities of these models, but suggest that they can be a strong foundation for acquiring them with little additional training, highlighting the value of real or synthetic data that demonstrates the long-tail behaviors nominal datasets rarely contain. Code and data will be made publicly available.

Abstract: In subject-driven text-to-image generation, quantitatively evaluating localized visual consistency across varying poses and environments remains a major challenge. Existing metrics either rely on global image embeddings (CLIP, DINO) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) that pool spatial representations and miss fine-grained textural artifacts, or depend on scalar threshold-gated dense metrics such as Visual-Semantic Matching (VSM) that suffer from point-alignment dropouts, severe threshold sensitivity, and score instability. To overcome these limitations, we propose TF-VSM, a fully continuous, threshold-free visual-consistency metric paired with an extended feature-disentanglement retraining schedule for dual-branch ResNet feature-aggregation networks. Rather than enforcing rigid binary cutoffs $(T_s,T_v)$, TF-VSM weights every object point by a non-linear semantic match confidence $w_j=ReLU(\hat{s}_j)^2$ and continuously integrates visual similarity over all consistency levels $r\in[0,1]$, yielding a closed-form confidence-weighted score without any hand-tuned hyperparameters. On a controlled 100-pair benchmark with ground-truth inpainting oracles, TF-VSM attains Pearson $0.638$ and Spearman $0.674$ oracle correlation, improving over the pretrained VSM baseline ($0.454/0.589$) by $+0.171/+0.116$ after retraining, and drastically outperforming CLIP ($-0.052/-0.004$), DINO ($0.087/0.121$), and VLMs ($0.096/0.003$). Six diagnostic ablations confirm that smooth confidence weighting is the right mechanism, that hard semantic gating is brittle (dropping to $0.533$ Pearson at $T_s{=}0.8$), and that the threshold-free metric is essentially invariant to the training-time loss balance $α$ that the thresholded metric still depends on.

Abstract: Vision-based 3D occupancy prediction provides a dense semantic representation of scenes for autonomous driving and embodied perception, yet remains vulnerable to occlusion, depth ambiguity, and projection errors that induce spatially varying reliability in voxel features. Existing methods typically map voxel representations to semantic predictions without explicitly modeling such observation-induced feature variations. To address this limitation, we propose VoxSAOR, a semantic-aware occupancy robustification framework that models aleatoric uncertainty through feature-space proxies. It first quantifies Local Semantic Consistency (LSC) to derive a voxel-wise structural stability prior and uses cross-view semantic consensus to filter unreliable semantic correspondences. Within each semantic category, spatially matched stable and ambiguous voxels form stable-to-ambiguous feature transitions, which are modeled by a class-conditional diagonal Gaussian distribution, capturing both feature displacement and dispersion. Guided by these proxy distributions, Aleatoric Regime Ensemble Learning (AREL) trains independently parameterized prediction heads using feature perturbations at progressively increasing scales, with perturbation strength modulated by structural reliability. At inference, proxy modeling and sampling are disabled, and all heads process the same observed features; their output probabilities are then averaged to produce a deterministic consensus prediction without requiring sampling. Experiments across multiple datasets and occupancy backbones demonstrate the effectiveness of VoxSAOR in improving semantic occupancy prediction, long-tail recognition, and predictive calibration. The source code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/MengfeiD/VoxSAOR.

Abstract: Dynamic spatial reasoning from monocular video enables vision-language models (VLMs) to understand how 3D environments evolve over time, supporting physical world understanding and embodied intelligence. Existing efforts primarily address observed-dynamics understanding, whereas agents in dynamic environments also require future-dynamics prediction beyond the observation boundary. Moreover, external geometric processing and textual descriptions can increase inference complexity or lose information when representing continuous dynamics. These limitations motivate a shared internal state that preserves observed dynamics and remains predictive beyond the input video. Accordingly, we present Pros4D, a framework for modeling evolving 4D worlds in latent space that unifies retrospective understanding of observed-dynamics with prospective reasoning for future-dynamics prediction. Specifically, Pros4D-Engine derives geometry-grounded supervision and constructs paired examples for both tasks, while Pros4D-Bench evaluates them across eight spatial task categories spanning dynamic cameras, objects, and scenes. At the model level, Pros4D compresses observed dynamics into latent observed states and autoregressively rolls them forward into latent future states. 4D Latent Alignment (4DLA) grounds these states through complementary visual-semantic and geometric supervision, while joint language supervision connects them to task reasoning. Experiments across benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong baselines in both capabilities.

From the same state, TRR produces a paired six-task benchmark of 2,224 static--dynamic pairs, that is, 4,448 trajectories with synchronized dual-view observations, states and actions. We demonstrate RGBT observation adapters on MuJoCo and Isaac Sim, report an engineering inventory of ten prepared robot configurations and six demonstrated camera viewpoints, and describe a developer workflow that carries an existing robot scene to exported RGBT records. Abstract: We ask whether a compact universal multimodal embedding models can produce better search embeddings by running the same layers again. We study eleven loop designs and training variants on three checkpoints of one vision-language architecture. Our protocol distinguishes whether a model uses its extra pass, whether its image states respond to the query, and whether those updates improve retrieval. Abstract: Compositional Zero-Shot Learning (CZSL) recognizes unseen attribute--object pairs by recombining primitives from seen compositions. Existing methods stop sharing evidence at deployment: fixed prototypes cannot exploit unlabeled test streams, while composition-wise refinement cannot propagate corrections across pairs sharing a primitive. We introduce PRISM, an online CZSL framework that extends primitive sharing to test-time adaptation. Its Hierarchical Knowledge Accumulation Module (HiKAM) factorizes prototype migration into composition-, attribute-, and object-level residual banks. A Priority Visual Memory Queue (PVMQ), initialized by Prototype Queue Initialization (PQI), grounds text updates with bounded visual evidence. The same adaptive core supports exhaustive Full PRISM and recall-calibrated Adaptive PRISM-Lite, which selects candidate budgets from held-out ranks. Comprehensive closed- and open-world experiments on UT-Zappos, MIT-States, and C-GQA demonstrate the state of the art, with Full PRISM achieving the best open-world AUC and harmonic mean on all three benchmarks and the best closed-world results on UT-Zappos and C-GQA. Fine-grained long-tail and non-stationary primitive-block evaluations reveal gains on under-supported concepts and stable adaptation under prolonged skew. Adaptive PRISM-Lite further delivers substantial efficiency gains in the full open-world space with only a small accuracy cost. PRISM thus combines primitive-shared online adaptation, multimodal grounding, and candidate-aware inference.

Abstract: Feature pyramids provide dense predictors with features at several spatial resolutions. However, it remains largely unexplored which pyramid levels each output location should aggregate information from. We investigate this question by allowing each output location to attend to features from every pyramid level and measuring how attention is distributed across scales. Surprisingly, locations on the finest output grid allocate approximately 75\% of their attention to features two levels coarser, rather than to the current or adjacent levels. Even on the same output grid, the balance shifts toward coarser features as object size increases.
  These observations motivate Asymmetric Global Pyramid Fusion (AGPF), a neck that preserves the original output grids while allowing each location to dynamically select its contextual sources across scales. Each location interacts with a multiscale memory whose features are aligned in image coordinates, enabling flexible cross-scale aggregation without altering prediction resolutions. Compared with FPN, AGPF improves all 19 object detection and all seven instance segmentation configurations evaluated on COCO. A single fusion block improves Cascade Mask R-CNN by 1.7 box AP and 2.3 mask AP, and the gains consistently transfer to semantic and panoptic segmentation.

Abstract: Whether a sidewalk is passable depends on who is trying to pass: people who use the same mobility aid disagree about the same image, so one label per scene, or per aid, is wrong for many of them. We introduce few-shot personal accessibility perception: from K answers of a person never seen before, predict their judgment of sidewalk scenes never seen before. PassCal separates a shared description of the scene from the person's interpretation of it. The description comes from the internal state of a frozen vision-language model (VLM) and from barrier type, severity and context properties predicted by a detector trained on 14.9k public audits; the interpretation, a calibration of the person's response style and a weighting of the properties, is fit from their answers. The same VLM prompted with those answers reaches 0.562, and the same calibration without the scene 0.521. Abstract: Driving world models usually generate videos from six pinhole cameras with separate optical centres and small overlaps, and must learn to keep these views consistent. We present PanDWM, which stitches them into one $360^\circ$ panoramic band. Camera boundaries then fall inside one image, so keeping the views consistent means keeping the image continuous, and each pixel still records its camera ray. Conditioned on layouts, a causal student generates the band window by window in four steps; it is distilled from a causal teacher and trained on its own rollouts. A feed-forward decoder lifts each frame along these rays into 3D Gaussians, and static Gaussians from neighbouring frames complete them into 4D scenes. On nuScenes, scored in one stitched $360^\circ$ view, PanDWM has lower FID and FVD than the multi-camera baselines at $8$-$46\times$ their speed, and keeps its 50-step teacher's FID while lowering FVD by 22-25%.

Abstract: Attribution methods are commonly validated through convergence across methods and consistency with domain knowledge, yet neither criterion establishes whether identified features are necessary for a task. This limitation is particularly relevant in multispectral remote sensing, where overlapping spectral information makes individual bands potentially substitutable. All three methods identify vegetation-sensitive spectral regions consistent with domain knowledge, while Shapley and Occlusion exhibit strong ordering sensitivity in deletion and insertion tests. However, these properties do not reliably predict retraining performance. Aggregate attribution consensus masks substantial per-class disagreement, with pairwise mean Spearman correlations ranging from 0.09 to 0.79. Models retrained from scratch using the five lowest-ranked bands perform comparably to or better than those using the five highest-ranked bands. Furthermore, attribution rankings from an independently trained model with identical configuration and seed identify a different top-five subset that yields better retraining performance. Analysis of all 252 five-band subsets reveals consistently low effective dimensionality (1.35 to 2.62), suggesting substantial spectral redundancy. These findings demonstrate that plausible and apparently faithful attribution rankings do not necessarily identify task-relevant bands for improved retrained performance, highlighting the limitations of band-level feature attribution in spectrally redundant input spaces.

Abstract: Existing feedback-based methods for online vision-and-language navigation (VLN) primarily learn from current episode outcomes, leaving previously acquired feedback underutilized. Inspired by human episodic memory, we propose R2A, an online VLN adaptation framework that continually reuses successful and failed navigation experiences for policy learning. Specifically, Success-Failure Memory (SFM) retains successful and failed navigation episodes with acquired feedback and retrieves task-relevant experiences based on incoming instructions, enabling historical feedback to guide subsequent navigation. Prior-Anchored Adaptation (PAA) learns from these experiences by reinforcing successful trajectories and suppressing failed ones relative to a frozen pretrained policy, while regularizing behavioral drift. Together, these modules enable continual feedback reuse and stable policy adaptation without source-domain data or expert supervision. Abstract: Hematoxylin and eosin (H&E)-to-multiplex immunofluorescence (mIF) generators predict spatial protein expression from routine histology, yet pixel-level marker reconstruction does not establish downstream utility. HEmIF-Bench decouples internal encoder features from generated outputs, benchmarking both against the pathology foundation reference UNI2. Mirroring the clinical workflow where pathologists rely on mIF to define cell phenotypes, delineate tertiary lymphoid structures (TLSs) and germinal centers (GCs), and stratify patient risk, we evaluate external CD3 recovery, single-cell classification, dense nuclear segmentation, TLS/GC segmentation, and patient survival prediction. Across the benchmark, foundation-model-based MIPHEI-ViT leads among the generators, while non-foundation models show task-specific strengths. Comparing the two routes reveals that internal encoder features generally outperform generated outputs, and larger output marker panels do not guarantee stronger downstream utility across datasets. Moreover, neither internal encoder features nor generated outputs consistently surpass frozen UNI2. These findings show that pixel-level stain fidelity does not equate to downstream utility, and that generators trained at current paired-data scales have yet to surpass large-scale pure-H&E foundation models. With the expansion of paired translation cohorts and the advancement of foundation-based generative architectures, HEmIF-Bench establishes a definitive benchmark for tracking when H&E-to-mIF generation crosses the threshold to deliver genuine value beyond routine histology for tumor microenvironment analysis and precision oncology.

Abstract: Knowledge-based visual question answering (KB-VQA) aims to combine visual information with external knowledge to answer user questions. Existing methods face two main bottlenecks: visual perception errors can propagate into hallucinations, and generalization across scenarios remains limited. To address these issues, we propose SAGE-VQA (Self-evolving Agent framework with Grounded Evidence), a training-free agentic framework that combines evidence verification with experience evolution. Our key insight is to explore an Evidence-Driven Adaptive Reasoning (EDAR) strategy. It accurately acquires and verifies question-relevant fine-grained visual information. It also extracts and refines reusable experience to support robust reasoning across tasks. Specifically, we design a Co-verified Visual Grounding module that verifies question-relevant visual information and provides grounded evidence for subsequent reasoning. We further introduce a Self-evolving Hierarchical Memory (SHM) mechanism. SHM organizes experiences into declarative and procedural memories based on their nature. These memories can be reused in subsequent reasoning and continually refined through feedback, without updating model parameters. Experimental results show that our method outperforms competing methods based on lightweight open-source vision-language models (VLMs) as well as those based on closed-source VLMs. Moreover, our method can serve as a plug-and-play reasoning framework for existing VLMs without modifying their architectures.

Abstract: Person re-identification (ReID) in real-world scenarios involves three clothes conditions: the same clothes (SC), changed clothes (CC), and the unknown (GEN) scenario where clothes changes are not specified. Existing methods typically train separate SC- or CC-specific models, which limits generalization across all conditions. Meanwhile, pre-trained visual-language models (VLMs) such as CLIP have shown strong generalization ability in various vision tasks, motivating recent ReID methods to incorporate textual prompts. However, to distinguish SC and CC, prior approaches rely on fixed predefined instructions, which lack learnable context embeddings in prompts and cannot effectively handle the GEN scenario. To overcome these limitations, we propose Two-Stage Prompt Learning (TSPL), a unified framework that integrates clothes-identity decoupling and scenario-aware task prompts within VLMs. In Stage 1, appearance-decomposed prompt learning disentangles clothes and identity information under frozen encoders, producing stable embeddings where clothes cues can be selectively preserved or suppressed. In Stage 2, task-aware prompt learning trains the image encoder with learnable context embeddings guided by Stage-1 representations, enabling robust adaptation across SC, CC, and GEN conditions. Extensive experiments on five benchmarks show that TSPL achieves state-of-the-art performance under all scenarios, improving mAP by up to 9.2% on LTCC (SC) and by 6.4% on VC-Clothes (GEN), respectively. The code will be released at https://github.com/YuSuen/TSPL.

Abstract: Financial narratives contain rich information about firms' performance and business conditions. Conventional text analysis often summarizes this information into numerical representations, which may not fully preserve the dynamic structure described in the narrative. In this study, we develop FinVision, a domain-specific multimodal generative model that translates financial narratives into visual representations of contemporaneous market dynamics. Using 87,940 paired observations of Management Discussion and Analysis (MD&A) disclosures and stock price charts, FinVision learns the average relationship between a firm's narrative account of a fiscal year and its stock-price dynamics over the same period. Given an MD&A alone, the trained model generates a text-implied stock chart, extending financial text analysis beyond numerical representations toward text-to-visual generation. We validate FinVision in two ways. First, held-out tests show that the model generates stock-chart representations that closely resemble observed charts and outperform direct LLM prompting, general-purpose text-to-image models, and the same architecture without financial-domain adaptation. Second, we examine the economic validity of the generated representations by comparing text-implied and observed stock charts. The resulting discrepancy is systematically associated with weaker investor responses around subsequent 10-K filings and with worse subsequent market and operating outcomes. Overall, our findings show how domain-adapted multimodal generative AI can create a new visual representation of financial text and provide an additional modality for studying business communication.

Abstract: The emergence of foundation models has marked a transformative shift in AI, enabling robust generalization across diverse downstream tasks through putative zero-shot learning. Large Language Models and Vision Language Models have demonstrated strong capabilities in tasks such as image interpretation, report generation, and question answering by effectively learning from multimodal data-images paired with associated text often with minimal supervision. In the healthcare domain, this ability to align visual and textual information reduces the reliance on extensive manual annotations, as models can leverage existing clinical reports and imaging data to learn meaningful representations. This integration holds promise for improving diagnostic support, treatment planning, and overall patient care, even in data constrained settings. In this review, we provide a definitive taxonomy of the medical VLM landscape, tracing the evolution from early Contrastive Alignment and Generative MLLMs to the cutting edge frontiers of Dense Pixel Grounding, Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE), and Reasoning Incentivized (RL) architectures. We critically examine the "medical bottleneck" identifying the persistent challenges of data scarcity, the "hallucination" risks in generative diagnostics, the computational strain of 3D volumetric processing, and the lack of standardized, clinically grounded evaluation metrics.

Abstract: Video creative plagiarism refers to the unauthorized reproduction of creative ideas across videos despite substantial differences in visual appearance, posing a new challenge to video copyright protection. However, existing video copy detection methods primarily focus on visual content similarity, struggling to capture abstract creative that are entangled with specific visual realizations. To address this issue, we introduce a novel task, named Video Creative Plagiarism Detection (VCPD), and propose an iterative creative evidence tracing framework, termed CreaTrace, to verify creative similarity and accurately localize the corresponding evidence segments. Specifically, we design a Multimodal Semantic Event Segmentation strategy to divide similar long videos into coherent narrative events, facilitating event-level dataset construction and retrieving similar segments. Based on these events, CreaTrace employs Coarse Creative Matching to identify candidate event pairs through narrative semantics and camera language. Then CreaTrace presents Creative Decoupling to disentangle abstract creative patterns from specific visual realizations. Meanwhile, Creative Swap Contrastive Learning is devised to enhance creative consistency through cross-realization feature swapping. Abstract: Automated tunnel-lining inspection must segment different defect geometries: cracks are thin, elongated, and connectivity-sensitive, whereas leakage, lining falling off, and segment damage are spatially extensive and context-dependent. Existing approaches generally apply a common decoder across defect classes or introduce boundary, topology, and directional mechanisms as isolated enhancements. This paper introduces Morphology-Decoupled Segmentation (MDS), in which defect morphology becomes the organising principle for feature routing, decoding, and interaction. A supervised routing module partitions multiscale encoder features into complementary linear and areal streams. The linear stream employs Deformable Strip Attention with image-conditioned direction hypotheses to preserve crack continuity without assuming fixed horizontal or vertical orientations, while the areal stream combines Pyramid Pooling with feature-pyramid fusion to capture diffuse boundaries and regional context. The representations are coupled through gated cross-morphology attention, allowing complementary information exchange while retaining morphology-specific features. A resolution-adaptive tiling and probability-blending pipeline preserves fine defects across variable-resolution imagery. MDS achieves 81.17% mIoU on the internal validation set and improves foreground image-level recognition over a backbone-matched SegDINO model, primarily for areal defects. Controlled ablations demonstrate the complementary contributions of routing supervision and cross-stream interaction while revealing a trade-off between areal-class performance and crack specialisation. The submitted model achieved 67.55% mIoU on an organiser-controlled hidden test, and the team placed second. Selective dynamic INT8 quantisation reduced model storage by 2.83 times with negligible accuracy loss.

Abstract: The Platonic Representation Hypothesis posits that models trained on different modalities converge toward a shared representation. Related work aligns frozen image and text encoders on natural images without paired data. We test zero-pair alignment on five datasets across four modalities: natural images, environmental audio, chest radiographs, and 3D computed tomography (CT). It succeeds only on COCO and fails on the other four. We analyze the failures and find that alignment must first locate the subspace where the modalities co-vary and then orient the mapping within it. Consistent with previous studies, medical imaging is the hardest case: its shared subspace carries the smallest fraction of the variance. When this fraction is small, alignment needs image-text pairs, and random-matrix theory predicts how many pairs are needed. These findings motivate LightAlign: a few pairs let a ridge regression between two frozen encoders locate and orient the mapping simultaneously, and a classifier fitted only on report text then predicts findings from the mapped images. On 3D CT, LightAlign surpasses models trained end to end on image-report pairs without finding-level labels: with only 200 pairs, LightAlign outperforms CT-CLIP on CT-RATE (0.754 vs. 0.733 AUROC), and with only 100 CT-RATE pairs, it does so on RAD-ChestCT, an out-of-distribution dataset from a different hospital. On CheXpert, it reaches 0.862 mean AUROC with only about 5% as many pairs as models trained on MIMIC-CXR.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) encode a high-resolution image into thousands of visual tokens, which dominate the cost of the prefill stage. Visual token reduction removes most of these tokens, but two problems remain at extreme budgets. The vision encoder still processes every patch of the image, and the few surviving tokens often miss the small regions that hold the answer. In this paper, we propose Glance-ETD, a two-stage token reduction framework guided by a lightweight question-conditioned scorer. In the first stage, a glance encoder views the image at low resolution and re-encodes only the question-relevant regions at native resolution. In the second stage, Evidence-Tempered Diversity (ETD) selects the final tokens by combining diversity with the evidence predicted by the scorer, so that the kept tokens cover the scene and retain the answer. A single-rollout self-distillation recipe trains the MLLM with the glance encoder. With only 5% of the visual tokens, Glance-ETD retains 91.6% of the full-token performance of Qwen3.5-4B across fourteen benchmarks and outperforms the strongest prior method, LT-OPD, by 17.3 points and the same training recipe with DivPrune by 6.0. It also speeds up the time to first token on 4K images by 3.1x and generalizes to three other MLLMs and to high-resolution document benchmarks, with the largest gains on text-rich tasks.
