Abstract: Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) generate an image by repeatedly re-predicting and refining every token of a discrete latent. They reach high fidelity, but need dozens of Markov steps. We therefore train few-step GRNs on the distribution of their own samples. We use its own uniform-state corruption to perform distribution matching on discrete tokens, which lets a discrete generator learn by backpropagation, and we pair noise with samples in the manner of rectified flow. We find that the steps of a few-step GRN do not carry the same amount of information or the same repair load. We therefore exploit its discrete refinement during training and build few-step GRNs in three parts. Oversampling samples the student with more steps than it was trained for and refines its judge on these samples. Non-uniform sampling trains only the last step and hands it most of the information at sampling time. Refinement confidence keeping holds on to the tokens the student is already sure of. We train a fast class-conditional generator on ImageNet 256 and, with the same method, a 2B text-to-image model. On ImageNet 256, the 3-step FastGRN beats the FID of GRN. On a 2B binary-token text-to-image GRN, a 4-step student also nearly matches the GenEval of GRN-T2I 2B.

</details>

Abstract: The localization of acupuncture points (acupoints) in Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) presents unique challenges since they are defined by abstract principles rather than distinct anatomical landmarks. Existing approaches are typically constrained to single view or rely on indirect calculations of relative positions with respect to other landmarks, thereby overlooking human anatomical variations. Furthermore, acupoint visibility assessment, which determins whether points are occluded by human itself, hair, clothing, or other objects, has received limited attention in practical applications. Acupoint localization itself does not require 3D reconstruction, but inferring their occlusion relationships with anatomical surfaces does, which adds computational cost and limits real-time inference. In this work, we introduce acuSimNet, an efficient hierarchical multi-task learning architecture for multi-view, self-occlusion-aware visibility prediction of acupoints, achieving 99.97% accuracy on the validation set. Our approach also addresses the challenges of high-dimensional classification (174 acupoints in cervicocranial region), negative convergence issues for visible acupoints, and inter-task scheduling optimization, resulting in substantially accelerated convergence. We improved the training efficiency from the exisiting methods of 3000 epochs to achieve 99% validation accuracy, to our optimized framework achieving 90% accuracy in 39 epochs and 99% accuracy in 86 epochs. This architecture overcomes the limitations of existing methods, could enable practical applications in acupoints detection and visualization, advancing the automation of TCM.

Abstract: Despite recent progress in SAR maritime monitoring, ship classification remains challenging due to limited labeled data, class imbalance, large intra-class variability, fine-grained inter-class similarity, and variations in target scale, orientation, and backscattering structure. To address these challenges, this paper proposes MARLIN, a class-frequency-aware framework with directional sparse and hierarchical multi-scale feature extraction for ship type classification in SAR images. The proposed architecture first constructs two complementary representations of each input image, where each views are processed by parallel encoder branches. Each branch begins with a directional sparse convolutional stem designed to capture elongated and orientation-dependent ship scattering patterns, followed by customized hierarchical multiscale feature extraction blocks. To improve learning under imbalanced class distributions, we introduce a class-frequency aware objective. The proposed objective combines margin-based classification with class-frequency-weighted prototype compactness and class-pair frequency weighted margin separation. This formulation shapes the embedding space by encouraging samples from the same class to form compact clusters while enforcing stronger separation for class pairs involving underrepresented ship categories. Extensive experiments on three complementary datasets demonstrate that in addition to being lightweight and parameter efficient, MARLIN improves both overall accuracy and class-balanced metrics, confirming its effectiveness for robust SAR ship-type classification under limited and imbalanced training conditions. The codes are available at github.com/benyaminhosseiny/marlin.

Abstract: Human motion prediction (HMP) aims to forecast future movement from an observed motion sequence, and recent methods have incorporated 3D scene geometry and object semantics to improve long-term plausibility. However, the destination of future motion remains ambiguous in cluttered scenes: the same observed motion can lead to different interaction targets or navigation modes. Existing methods either handle this ambiguity implicitly or rely on auxiliary signals like gaze that are hard to obtain in practice. To address this, we propose SGIP, which explicitly predicts where in the scene future motion will be grounded across both objects and open navigation regions, and uses it to condition future trajectory and full-body pose prediction. This scene-grounded interaction prior is learned via intent distillation: at training time only, a teacher model scores candidate scene regions by combining an LLM prompted with a textual description of the person's intent with geometric motion-scene features. A student model learns to reproduce these scores from only the observed motion and 3D scene, making intent and LLM unnecessary at test time. Across multiple benchmark datasets, SGIP outperforms prior scene-aware baselines on both trajectory and pose accuracy in a realistic deployment setting where neither gaze nor intent is available at inference, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The project page is available at https://sgip2026.github.io.

</details>

Abstract: Galaxies evolve over hundreds of millions of years, and no telescope has ever watched one move. Cosmological simulations make this evolution visible as a sequence of multi-view images, inviting the question of whether a galaxy can be recovered in four dimensions from its images. This setting defeats existing dynamic scene methods, since millions of stars span only a few pixels each, faint populations blend into diffuse light, and free deformations reproduce the images without obeying gravity. We present Cosmos4D, which rests on two components. Gravitational 4D Gaussian Splatting represents each star as a self-luminous Gaussian whose motion is integrated through a gravitational potential and a low-dimensional force field shared by all stars. Luminosity-stratified dynamics give bright stars individual orbits supervised across image scales and carry faint populations with a velocity field estimated from optical flow. We further build a 4D dataset of eight galaxies from four cosmological simulations, on which Cosmos4D outperforms dynamic Gaussian baselines in both photometric quality and physical accuracy. With these advancements, we glimpse eight hundred million years of a galaxy's life in ninety-six frames.

</details>

Abstract: Masked modeling is the dominant self-supervised paradigm for point-cloud videos. However, it leaks the information it is meant to predict, whether it reconstructs masked points as in MAE-style methods or predicts their latent features. Because point-cloud tokens are anchored at points sampled from each frame, querying a target at its own position reveals where its geometry has moved, in reconstruction and latent prediction alike. For latent predictors, random spatio-temporal masks introduce a further shortcut, as visible frames on both sides of each target allow interpolation rather than forecasting. The pretext task thus reduces to completion. Once the target positions are withheld, a masked latent predictor performs only slightly better than copying the last frame. To remove this leakage, we propose PointWM, a latent world model that turns completion into forecasting by predicting the features and geometry of future frames from strictly earlier context. Each target is queried only with its last-seen position and timestamp, while a latent action learned without annotations is meant to capture the part of the future that the past leaves undetermined. On HOI4D action segmentation with a P4Transformer backbone, PointWM improves F1@50 over training from scratch by 24.9 offline and 29.1 under strictly causal inference. These results show the promise of latent world models as a leakage-free pretraining paradigm for point-cloud videos.Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/InitalZ/PointWM.

</details>

Abstract: Turning a 3D shape into a Computer Numerical Control (CNC) program is still largely manual. For every part, a machinist chooses setups, tools, step-downs, and toolpath patterns. We present Geo2Gcode, which converts a target mesh directly into standard G-code through structured machining primitives. Each primitive couples an indexed machining direction, a closed per-view reconstruction of the target, and explicit tool-radius, layer-height, and clearing-pattern parameters, and compiles deterministically into layerwise toolpaths via signed-distance queries. Most of the fidelity comes from this compiler. A lightweight selector trained with a voxel stock simulator fills in primitive parameters without per-part manual settings, approaching the finest fixed setting at about a quarter of its toolpath length. In simulation on ABC parts, Geo2Gcode reaches roughing fidelity comparable to an expert-tuned commercial CAM setup with a moderately longer toolpath, and clearly improves on a default CAM configuration. Generated programs run in a commercial G-code simulator, and one part is machined on a physical CNC mill. These results demonstrate that roughing programs can be generated directly from geometry without per-part CAM tuning.

Abstract: MambaVision, a hybrid state-space and attention backbone, places most of its parameters in its widest, fourth stage. We quantified what that stage contributes to multi-label chest radiograph classification. The fourth stage was replaced with lighter single-scan state-space blocks adapted from Spatial-Mamba, with multi-scale dilated depthwise convolutions applied to the scan output; the first three stages were retained. Tiny, Small, and Base models were evaluated across nine dataset-scale combinations drawn from two source cohorts: the National Institutes of Health ChestX-ray14 archive (full ChestMNIST and a high-resolution subset) and Indiana University Open-i. At the Tiny scale, replacement reduced parameter count by 35.7% and increased graphics processing unit throughput by 19.3%; the savings came from the stage's dense projections, not from removing quadratic attention. Without distillation, the mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve on ChestMNIST was 0.004 lower than the baseline (95% bootstrap confidence interval, 0.002 to 0.006), and 0.003 to 0.006 lower across all nine combinations. Knowledge distillation from an unreplaced teacher narrowed the gap to the undistilled baseline to 0.001-0.002 and met the equivalence criterion on ChestMNIST, but distilling the baseline with the same recipe improved it by 0.001-0.003, so a gap of 0.002-0.005 persisted under symmetric training. At the Base scale, the unreplaced Small model was both smaller and more accurate. Fourth-stage replacement is a useful trade-off only for Tiny and Small models. These measurements let developers of chest radiograph screening tools weigh model size and speed against a quantified loss of diagnostic discrimination.

</details>

Abstract: Recent methods try to inspect and revise masks to mitigate referential ambiguity and localization errors caused by complex spatial relations in language-guided remote sensing segmentation. However, these solutions often lead to Revision Degradation: revising missing regions may introduce errors into previously correct ones. Even a globally better mask can omit correct regions found in inferior masks. We term this Evidence Fragmentation: correct target evidence is scattered across imperfect masks. All masks generated by the model at this time are suboptimal. We propose GeoReVis, which unifies revision guided by execution feedback, Candidate Selection, and Cross-Mask Evidence Consolidation. It learns fine-grained revision from real actions that improve segmentation when executed and learns to select a better mask through quality comparisons between actual candidates, reducing the risk of degradation during revision. The selected whole mask can still discard useful regions. GeoReVis therefore retains all candidates across rounds, partitions their spatial disagreements into composable regions, and jointly reconstructs regions conditioned on the full instruction and context. This makes complementary and fragmentary evidence reusable regardless of its source candidate's overall quality, yielding improved segmentation. We further build a data pipeline to generate fine-grained multi-round segmentation trajectories and derive supervision for revision actions, candidate selection, and region composition from the model's own outputs, extending training beyond whole-mask IoU supervision. We also propose ReVis-Eval, a benchmark protocol evaluating multi-round revision and cross-round evidence consolidation accuracy. Experiments on ReVis-Eval and 5 benchmarks for remote sensing reasoning and referring segmentation demonstrate the effectiveness of GeoReVis.

</details>

Abstract: Scene text synthesis requires stroke-accurate characters rendered in a specified layout, as well as visual coherence with the surrounding scene. To achieve this, existing methods commonly pre-render the target text at its intended locations to form a glyph image that jointly specifies layout and glyph structure. However, this representation ties the resolution of each glyph to its target region, limiting fine-grained stroke guidance for small, densely packed, or structurally complex text. To tackle this, we propose GlyphAtlas, a scene text synthesis framework that retains the glyph image as a spatial anchor and supplies fine-grained stroke references through a dedicated glyph atlas. The atlas renders and encodes each character or word in a separate cell, decoupling reference resolution from target-region size. We further introduce atlas-anchored attention, which lets each text region retrieve stroke detail only from its corresponding atlas cells to refine the coarse glyph structure provided by the glyph image. This coarse-to-fine conditioning simplifies stroke rendering while preserving explicit layout correspondence. We optimize GlyphAtlas with a mask-weighted flow matching objective and a timestep-weighted text perceptual loss for writing accuracy and background consistency. Random resolution augmentation of atlas cells supports adjustable atlas budgets at inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GlyphAtlas achieves stroke-accurate synthesis of small, dense, and structurally complex text, zero-shot transfer to languages unseen during task-specific training, and leading performance in scenes with many text regions. project page: https://fr0zencrane.github.io/project-glyphatlas/

Abstract: Continuous and accurate estimation of human hand kinematics is important not only for intuitive human-machine interaction, but also for capturing fine-grained human manipulation demonstrations that can provide action supervision for embodied-intelligence datasets and dexterous robot learning. However, individual sensing modalities may be insufficient for reliable estimation under varying operating conditions. We present a finger-unencumbered multimodal framework for continuous regression of 20 hand joint angles from egocentric RGB images and 128-channel forearm high-density surface electromyography (HD-sEMG). The framework combines a Vision Transformer-based visual encoder, a Transformer-based EMG encoder, and a degree of freedom (DoF) query Transformer fusion decoder. To account for the varying informativeness of visual and EMG cues across joint DoFs and input conditions, the decoder assigns a separate learnable query to each DoF, enabling DoF-specific and context-adaptive fusion of the two modalities. The framework was evaluated on synchronized multimodal data from 10 participants. Averaged across participants, the proposed fusion model achieved a mean coefficient of determination ($R^2$) of $0.88$ and a mean absolute error (MAE) of $2.80^\circ$, compared with $0.82$ and $3.21^\circ$ for the vision-only model and $0.68$ and $5.13^\circ$ for the EMG-only model. It also outperformed the evaluated representative unimodal baselines and alternative fusion designs. DoF-wise analyses showed larger fusion gains for visually ambiguous joints and viewing conditions, while synthetic visual occlusion demonstrated greater robustness than vision-only estimation. These results highlight the complementary value of egocentric vision and forearm HD-sEMG for capturing fine-grained human hand kinematics, with potential applications in human demonstration acquisition and dexterous robot learning.

Abstract: Automotive radar provides long-range sensing and direct Doppler velocity measurements, but its sparse and non-uniformly sampled point clouds pose significant challenges for panoptic segmentation. Dynamic objects often yield only a few detections amid a dominant static background, providing limiting the geometric cues for separating object instances. We propose \emph{Radar Panoptic Transformer (RPT)}, an end-to-end point-based network that incorporates radar-adaptive feature extraction with semantic-guided mask prediction. Its backbone aggregates pose-aligned temporal context through grouped vector attention and learns per-point neighborhood radii conditioned on range, azimuth, and radar cross section (RCS). Point-wise semantic predictions initialize queries at likely foreground detections and guide masked cross-attention through query-point class agreement, producing instance masks without geometric clustering. An auxiliary RCS-consistency loss matches instance-level statistics between predicted and annotated instances. RPT achieves state-of-the-art performance on RadarScenes with $71.6\%$ mIoU and $82.2\%$ PQ. We further evaluate RPT on MAN TruckScenes, where it outperforms the evaluated baselines in a long-range, full-surround radar setting with sensing ranges up to $230\,\mathrm{m}$. RPT achieves real-time end-to-end inference at $31$\,Hz on RadarScenes, exceeding the sensor frame rate.

  We investigate resolution-specific processing and parameter sharing using three AdaFace-based architecture families: fixed-resolution specialists; Independent Backbones with Shared Heads (IBSH); and models with resolution-specific front-ends for 40x40, 80x80, and 160x160 inputs, followed by a shared identity trunk and embedding projection. We evaluate each architecture with route-specific training subsets and with a full-pool strategy, in which every specialist uses the complete selected training set.
  Results reveal a trade-off among same-resolution specialization, cross-resolution interoperability, and common-route re-extraction. Route-specific training produces distinct performance profiles across routes, whereas full-pool training makes route performance more uniform and primarily benefits the 160x160 route. Thus, adding training data does not improve all resolution routes equally.

These improvements also persist with a stronger backbone, demonstrating the effectiveness of visual marking across different model capabilities. Overall, SelfMark provides a unified approach to visual reasoning by integrating spatially grounded marks directly into the visual feature space of MLLMs.

Abstract: We present a method for inferring the interior temperature distribution of a solid object from registered multi-view visible-spectrum and thermal images, even when the thermal views cover only part of its surface. We formulate steady-state heat conduction as a Laplace boundary value problem and solve it with a direct boundary integral equation discretized over a point cloud: a Gaussian reconstruction of the visible-spectrum images supplies the boundary geometry, and the thermal images supply the boundary data. We do not build surface or volume meshes at any stage in our method. Instead, we sample collocation points from rendered depth without the pixels at depth discontinuities, and use a density subtraction that keeps the interior evaluation accurate up to the surface. The whole chain is differentiable, and we use it to recover the pose and depth of heating pipes, insulation defects, and the shape of cracks from thermal images. We validate our method through experiments on boundary value problems with Dirichlet-only and mixed Dirichlet and Robin boundaries, using synthetic and real captured data on scenes with challenging topology. These experiments show that our method matches analytical solutions and has better accuracy than FEM solvers.

Abstract: Video diffusion transformers rely on 3D full attention over tens of thousands of spatiotemporal tokens, and its quadratic cost dominates inference latency. Sparse attention reduces this cost, but existing methods face a dilemma: static methods apply predefined masks and cannot capture input-dependent attention, whereas dynamic methods reconstruct the sparse mask repeatedly, paying online to rediscover positional structure that is largely stable across inputs. To this end, we propose PoCoAttn, a training-free sparse attention framework that separates input-stable from input-dependent attention and resolves the former offline and the latter online. PoCoAttn features: (1) A relative-offset analysis of 3D query-key pairs, which shows that video attention decomposes into an input-stable positional prior that carries most of the attention mass and a small content-dependent residual. (2) A position-content collaborative sparse attention mechanism, which retrieves positional keys from offline-calibrated tables, selects content keys online with a head-adaptive ratio between the two sources, and compensates for the discarded keys to approximate full attention. (3) A step-adaptive budget allocation algorithm, which models how early-step sparse attention errors are amplified along the denoising trajectory and derives the optimal sparsity budget per step. Extensive experiments across four video diffusion models and three tasks show that PoCoAttn consistently achieves a better speed-fidelity Pareto frontier than state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, improving PSNR by 1.9 to 5.0 dB over baselines and delivering up to 4.1$\times$ attention and 2.0$\times$ denoising speedups over dense FlashAttention-3 attention.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-based navigation systems for orbit must be trained almost entirely on synthetic imagery, because real labelled images of a given spacecraft in flight rarely exist. Benchmarks like SPEED+ are built for that situation: the training set comes from a simulator, and the test sets are real images of a satellite mockup under two kinds of lighting. That simulator approximates how light reflects off mylar and aluminium, and like every scientific simulator it is misspecified. Its error is radiometric, since it misses the specular highlights, hard shadows and sensor saturation common in real images. We probe the resulting synthetic-to-real gap, the central risk for such systems, with a deliberately simple monocular pose estimator. In a 2x2 study of backbone adaptation and photometric augmentation, trained on synthetic images and tested on both real hardware-in-the-loop domains, we find three things. (1) The model does not know it is failing: regions conformally calibrated to 90% on synthetic data cover only 9-15% of rotation errors on sunlamp and 21-38% on lightbox, so a wrong pose carries the confidence of a correct one. (2) Transfer is asymmetric: scaling data and features improved synthetic rotation error from 1.65 to 1.03 rad but moved the harshly lit sunlamp domain by only 0.05 rad, under twice its seed s.d., whereas adapting the backbone's last stage carried 63-83% of its extra synthetic gain to both real domains. (3) Once the backbone adapts, augmentation helps only in the direction it models: our photometric stack improved the diffusely lit lightbox domain from 1.17 to 0.99 rad on every seed but moved sunlamp no more than seed-to-seed variation, while with the backbone frozen it helped sunlamp more. Every post-review experiment was pre-registered, and the code, per-image predictions and pre-registration record are at https://github.com/lokindoo/spacecraft-pose-estimation.

Abstract: Directly aligning trajectory-distilled video generators slashes rollout costs and optimizes the deployed few-step model without teacher fine-tuning or re-distillation. However, conventional flow-matching preference objectives assume single-head local velocity supervision, mismatching the finite-interval, block-average transition interface of trajectory-distilled generators such as Parallel Decoding Distillation (PDD) and Parametric Trajectory Distillation (PTD). Naively supervising only the primary initial-subinterval velocity sub-head rather than the fused block-average velocity at block starts (DirectAlign) induces a severe training--inference mismatch that degrades quality below the unaligned baseline. We introduce BlockAlign, an interface-consistent preference optimization framework that constructs states at valid block boundaries, queries the fused block-level velocity predictor, and supervises span-normalized transition residuals-seamlessly supporting DPO-, NFT-, and rollout-style objectives while preserving the native few-step sampler. We further build a unified post-training pipeline integrating offline pairs, reward-free semi-online continuations, and online rollouts with hierarchical preference selection and objective-aware block scheduling. Experiments on multi-reference video generation across MiniMax-H3 Ref2VA and an in-house model RefVid-V4 show that BlockAlign consistently outperforms unaligned distilled models and DirectAlign in structural plausibility, reference consistency, and visual quality. Notably, our approach unlocks PDD's 5x few-step rollout speedup for 3x faster end-to-end online training, achieving statistically significant human preference gains over PDD baselines (54.0% vs. 33.3% wins/losses, or 61.8% non-tie win rate on RefVid-V4; 51.3% vs. 34.7%, or 59.7% non-tie win rate on MiniMax-H3) and up to an 82.7% win rate against DirectAlign.

Abstract: Existing multi-granularity methods for monocular 3D human pose estimation emphasize spatial joint features while underusing skeletal structure, including global connectivity and temporal continuity. Moreover, modeling entire frame sequences incurs high computational costs. We propose a Structure-Aware Multi-Granularity Spatiotemporal Transformer that integrates structural priors across spatial and temporal representations for accurate and efficient pose estimation. A three-level feature extractor captures human topology at the joint, bone, and limb levels. Gated Adaptive Spatial Attention (GASA) embeds structural constraints into spatial attention, while Multi-Granularity Feature Enhancement (MGFE) uses hierarchical pooling and fusion to strengthen local details and global semantics. Temporal Segment Fusion (TSF) aligns temporal contexts across granularities to capture both short- and long-range motion patterns. Hierarchical Cross-Attention (HCA) promotes spatiotemporal interaction between granularities, and Keyframe Attention (KFA) selects informative motion segments to reduce redundancy and computational cost. Experiments demonstrate improved accuracy and efficiency over conventional Transformer-based approaches, highlighting the effectiveness of structure-aware multi-granularity modeling for monocular 3D human pose estimation.

</details>

This paper presents VisDrone-ReID, a unified multi-category Re-ID benchmark derived from the VisDrone2019-MOT dataset through a principled conversion pipeline. The pipeline resolves structural incompatibilities between multi-object tracking annotations and Re-ID evaluation requirements, addressing sequence-scoped identity collision, temporal redundancy, and the absence of a principled identity-level train/test split. The resulting benchmark comprises 9,516 identities and 150,016 images across ten object categories - pedestrian, people, bicycle, car, van, truck, tricycle, awning-tricycle, bus, and motor, sourced from 80 unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) sequences captured across urban environments. A leave-one-out (LOO) evaluation protocol is adopted to provide a comprehensive assessment. Baseline experiments across four representative Re-ID models spanning CNN and transformer architectures establish the first systematic performance reference on this benchmark. Results reveal a consistent Rank-1 to mAP gap across all models, suggesting that VisDrone-ReID presents a genuine retrieval challenge beyond nearest-neighbour matching. Per-category analysis identifies pedestrian as the most challenging category and reveals that vision-language pretraining provides a measurable advantage on semantically distinctive categories. VisDrone-ReID provides a consistent platform for studying multi-category object re-identification under the scale variation, viewpoint challenges, and category diversity characteristic of real-world surveillance scenarios.

Abstract: In visual storytelling, a believable character must not only look consistent across scenes but also \textit{behave} in ways that reflect their personality. Evaluating seven T2I systems, we confirm a persistent semantic gap between abstract trait labels and visual evidence, and find that visual identity consistency does not imply personality consistency. To bridge this gap, we propose \textbf{Trait-Behavior-Visual (TBV)}, a training-free prompting framework that decomposes personality traits into renderable visual constraints. TBV improves Personality Alignment Score by up to $+22.1\%$, with larger gains in models with stronger language-conditioning capacity.

Abstract: Can a vision-language model look at a picture of a chain reaction and tell what will happen? Recent multimodal models describe scenes fluently and solve textbook physics problems, which suggests they should also predict how a scene of ramps, ledges, pendulums, seesaws and dominoes plays out once a ball is pushed. We tested this claim on 30 models from 10 families and reached a clear conclusion: unaided, no model predicts the outcome reliably. We built DominoBench, a benchmark of still images of 2D rigid-body scenes whose outcome a physics simulator computes. It holds 854 items in 427 minimal pairs, in which a change of one to three grid steps in one quantity decides whether one person survives, and every item comes with a four-rung hint ladder ending in the exact calculation that decides the outcome. Unaided, frontier models from Anthropic, Google and OpenAI are right about the person whose fate flips in at most 72% of items and answer both twins of a pair correctly in at most 34%. Pointing at the deciding step helps little, and exact geometry given as text barely helps, yet once the deciding calculation and the quantities it needs, including speeds that a still image cannot show, are written out, the same models reach 82-92% on the deciding person, while small open-weight models stay near chance. Physical prediction from a still image therefore fails at the quantitative step: the strongest models use the deciding quantity when they are given it, but do not produce it on their own. For models that plan or act in the physical world, a fluent account of a scene is thus no evidence of a correct prediction: such models must find and compute the deciding quantity unprompted, and DominoBench tests whether they do.

</details>

Abstract: Remote sensing image segmentation plays a critical role in various applications. Interactive instance segmentation, a specialized task within image segmentation, aims to generate precise segmentation results for target objects based on user input prompts. However, previous studies on interactive instance segmentation primarily focus on single-view images, limiting their ability to comprehensively understand complex 3D remote sensing scenes. In this study, we extend interactive instance segmentation from a single-view to a multi-view framework, which can also be easily converted to a 3D segmentation format, thus achieving more accurate and comprehensive 3D remote sensing scene perception across multiple views. First, we construct a multi-view interactive instance segmentation dataset for remote sensing, comprising five distinct scenes, each captured from about one hundred different views. Second, we propose InterRSGaussian, a novel instance segmentation method based on 3D Gaussians. InterRSGaussian enables users to provide point clicks on target objects from any arbitrary view and generates view-consistent instance segmentation results across all input views without requiring manual annotations. The resulting multi-view segmentation can further be projected onto 3D point clouds for 3D segmentation. To improve training efficiency and segmentation performance, we introduce a prior distillation network for initialization. Overall, this study extends traditional 2D remote sensing scene perception into a 3D multi-view framework, enabling a more comprehensive understanding of target objects in complex environments with user interactivity.

Abstract: Scene chronology aims to reconstruct a scene across visits separated by hours or days, during which objects may change in position, presence or shape. Such reconstruction must retain every earlier state, yet each revisit often provides only a few images. Storing a complete model for each visit duplicates the unchanged scene, whereas updating a single shared model alters earlier states. We present ReScene, which stores each visit as an immutable compact update, so that any earlier visit can be recovered exactly by applying the stored updates in order, without retaining past images. To localise each update, ReScene attributes the disagreement between the preceding reconstruction and the new images to individual Gaussians through their compositing weights. This attribution guides the suppression of outdated Gaussians and the refinement of persistent ones, while newly visible surfaces are initialised from learned multi-view depth. A small human evaluation confirms the ranking in estimated net corrections but suggests a smaller gain than detector-based evaluation. LEDGE thus distinguishes two limits of self-correction: knowing what to fix and being able to fix it.

Abstract: The Internet of Things (IoT) is transforming intelligent transportation systems by connecting roadside cameras, edge computing units, vehicles, and traffic infrastructure into a shared sensing and decision-making ecosystem. However, transmitting raw traffic video over vehicle-to-everything (V2X) links is bandwidth-intensive, while deploying large multimodal models directly on resource-constrained vehicles is often impractical. To address these limitations, this article proposes VISTA (Vision-Informed Semantic Traffic Awareness), a multimodal vision-language edge-intelligence framework that combines predictive video representation learning with lightweight language-based reasoning to provide communication-efficient traffic situational awareness. In the proposed architecture, roadside surveillance units employ the Video Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (V-JEPA) and an attentive-pooler module to convert traffic video clips into compact future-scene embeddings. These embeddings are transmitted through V2X links to vehicle-mounted systems, where a TinyLlama small language model, enhanced with a custom visual adapter, interprets the embeddings alongside natural-language (NL) traffic queries. VISTA enables vehicles to obtain interpretable traffic information, reason about upcoming scene dynamics, and identify potential collision risks without receiving raw video streams. Experimental results in a digital-twin urban traffic environment show that VISTA achieves 97.77% accuracy and an 89% F1 score for future collision prediction while reducing the communication payload by several orders of magnitude compared with raw RGB video transmission. These results demonstrate the feasibility of embedding-based multimodal intelligence for low-latency, communication-efficient, and safety-oriented IoT-enabled transportation systems.

Abstract: Real-time camera-controlled video re-filming aims to synthesize target views from a streaming source video under online user-specified camera trajectories. This task poses two key challenges: maintaining synchronization and content consistency across views, and achieving low-latency rendering with long-horizon stability. Existing approaches are predominantly designed for offline full-sequence processing and rely on bidirectional attention or costly depth-based geometric preprocessing, making them unsuitable for low-latency streaming scenarios. To address these limitations, we develop a teacher-to-student framework that distills a high-fidelity bidirectional re-filming teacher into a few-step causal autoregressive student. We introduce Cross-Frame Injection to interleave each incoming source frame with its synchronized target latent, thereby establishing direct cross-view correspondence and improving content consistency. To ensure stable long-horizon generation, we further propose Shared Relative RoPE, which bounds temporal indices within the pretrained range, and Cycle Streaming Tuning, which exposes the student to extended cyclic trajectories during streaming training. These designs enable low-latency online video re-filming with precise camera control and sustained spatiotemporal consistency over long streaming rollouts.

</details>

The single orbit yielded greater point concentration within plant structures and fewer visible gaps, while the ribbon skirt yielded fewer scattered points and slightly lower proportions of isolated points. Additional observations also increased point dispersion associated with plant movement. A curlicue trajectory tested in a different field in 2024 produced distinguishable individual plants without visible duplicated structures, although substantial voids remained, mainly on the soil surface. These results demonstrate the feasibility of recovering recognizable maize organs from consumer UAV imagery and reveal trade-offs between acquisition strategies, reconstruction completeness, and point dispersion. Abstract: Visual token pruning improves the efficiency of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by removing redundant visual tokens, yet the reliability of existing selection signals has not been systematically examined. We analyze token selection under semantics-preserving perturbations and across representative pruning criteria, revealing two limitations. First, attention-based importance shifts non-uniformly under perturbations, so magnitude alone cannot distinguish stable evidence from unstable saliency (local instability). Second, attention- and diversity-based methods select substantially different subsets despite comparable performance, showing that useful evidence is not confined to a unique subset (global inconsistency). These findings motivate reliable token-set construction. We instantiate this perspective in CoRePruner, a training-free framework combining instruction-aware diversity with reliability-aware attention. Across image benchmarks, multiple MLLM architectures, and token budgets, CoRePruner improves performance, especially under aggressive pruning, and remains competitive on video benchmarks. Our results establish reliability as a central consideration in visual token selection.

</details>

Abstract: Image deraining aims to recover rain-free images from rain-degraded observations. Recent frequency-aware deraining methods have demonstrated promising performance by removing rain artifacts in the spectral domain. However, since rain artifacts and background textures often appear in several correlated subbands, prior frequency-domain deraining models still enhance frequency subbands separately. Such band-wise processing limits information exchange across subbands. Without context from other subbands, rain and clean content that appear together in one subband cannot be separated. This leads to either residual rain streaks or over-smoothing of fine details. To address this limitation, we propose AFRNet, an Adaptive Frequency Redistribution Network for image deraining. First, we introduce an Adaptive Frequency Recombination Module (AFRM) that performs cross-band modulation among wavelet subbands, enabling information interaction and adaptive recombination of degraded frequency components. We then develop a Subband Compensation Block (SCB) that compensates for decoder subband degradation by leveraging encoder subband features through inter-subband attention in the wavelet domain, alleviating detail loss during reconstruction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AFRNet achieves competitive performance and consistent results across diverse deraining benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Visual adversarial perturbations can substantially impair large vision-language models (LVLMs) despite introducing only subtle input changes. To understand how these small changes cause substantial errors, we investigate their optimization and propagation through the model, revealing two key findings. First, stronger attacks rely on finer iterative optimization, while subtle input perturbations induce representation discrepancies that are amplified layer by layer through the vision encoder and persist in the language model. Second, disrupting the alignment between within-patch input patterns and the model's computation reduces clean-adversarial representation discrepancies and substantially weakens attacks. Together, these findings suggest that adversarial effectiveness depends on both fine-grained perturbation patterns and their coupling with the model's computation. Motivated by these findings, we propose SmoothShift, a lightweight, training-free defense that combines local smoothing and spatial transformations. Smoothing weakens fine-grained perturbation patterns, while spatial transformations disrupt their alignment with the encoder's patch grid. SmoothShift requires no model updates, auxiliary networks, or additional model inference. Experiments cover three LVLM families on four datasets and robustly fine-tuned Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) models on eight classification datasets. Evaluations across eight attack methods and multiple perturbation budgets demonstrate improved robustness while largely preserving clean performance. SmoothShift also provides robustness gains under defense-aware attacks using mixed-gradient BPDA with EOT.

</details>

Abstract: Semantic visual classification commonly aligns visual embeddings with semantic spaces induced by pretrained text encoders. However, such spaces are typically high-dimensional, weakly aligned with visual feature geometry, and insufficiently discriminative for downstream classification tasks. We propose TaSS, a framework for learning a task-adaptive low-dimensional semantic space that preserves semantic structure while explicitly optimizing class separability. TaSS is constructed through a parameterized geometry transformation of pretrained text embeddings that enlarges inter-class margins via distance reshaping and improves intra-class compactness through prototype-preserving semantic constraints. The resulting semantic space maintains meaningful semantic relationships while producing a discriminative geometry tailored for classification. Frozen visual encoder features are subsequently aligned to TaSS using a lightweight MLP, enabling improved discriminative learning without fine-tuning pretrained backbones. We further provide theoretical analysis showing that TaSS preserves semantic consistency while enforcing enhanced inter-class separation and controlled intra-class variance. Extensive experiments on skeleton-based human action recognition, video classification, and image classification benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over existing semantic alignment approaches, with gains exceeding 3 percentage points on average and up to 8.5 points on challenging datasets.

Abstract: Identifying the drug and dose that produced a cellular response requires preserving treatment effects within each cellular background. A joint-embedding predictor can match its targets while ignoring the intervention if its encoder preserves differences across backgrounds but erases differences between treated and control populations within each one. We call this failure interventional contrast collapse. PerturbJEPA encodes the difference in mean gene expression between treated and control populations as an effect vector. It aligns the displacement between their latent centroids with this vector and regularizes the vectors to preserve variation across population pairs. We show that the measured displacements cannot all coincide within a batch when effect-vector variation exceeds the root-mean-square alignment error. Trained with separate drug and dose ranking objectives, the model predicts candidate responses from a common control population and matches them to the observed response without cell pairing or expression reconstruction. We evaluate 3,592 Tahoe-100M queries on held-out combinations of known interventions and cellular backgrounds, each with over 700 drug-dose candidates in a fixed, background-specific catalog. PerturbJEPA achieves 91.46% macro joint drug-dose Recall@1, exceeding STATE by 3.40 percentage points (95% drug-cluster bootstrap interval of 1.72 to 5.07 points). Controlled diagnostics show that treatment-control differences can shrink even when representations remain globally dispersed. With the drug ranking objective fixed, adding effect-vector regularization to alignment raises joint Recall@1 from 18.57% to 87.47%.

Abstract: Exemplar-free continual learning seeks to minimize joint risk over all seen tasks, yet historical data are unavailable for direct optimization. We observe that, for classification, preserving the model's decisions on previously learned classes helps retain old knowledge. This motivates Decision-Preserving Regularization (DPR). Penalizing changes in all pairwise old-class logit margins reduces algebraically to a centered relative-logit penalty. We further apply spectral whitening to remove the classifier's spectral scale bias. Because the global classifier supplies the decision constraints for the next stage, it must remain calibrated across all seen classes despite the drift of stored old-class statistics during representation updates. We therefore fit a closed-form affine map from paired current-task features to transport old-class Gaussian means and covariances into the updated feature space, then calibrate the global classifier using these distributions and current-task features. Across CIFAR-100, Tiny-ImageNet, ImageNet-Subset, and ImageNet-1K under cold-start protocols, comparisons with state-of-the-art methods show that DPR achieves higher accuracy in most settings while remaining highly efficient. Code is available at https://github.com/bnufw/dpr.

</details>

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models often synthesize policy-violating content, necessitating robust safeguards and rigorous evaluation frameworks. However, current approaches remain largely monolithic in both execution and evaluation, limited to coarse-grained categorization within broad unsafe concepts. Such approaches fail to account for the specific constituent factors of a violation, resulting in weak learning signals and allowing model collapse to bypass safety checks. To overcome these limitations, we propose Example-Based Spatial Guidance (EBSG), a training-free method that utilizes user-editable exemplar packs to provide granular spatial guidance for precise concept steering. Unlike existing methods that coarsely steer the entire image away from a broad concept, EBSG decomposes these categories into specific sub-concepts via exemplar text-image pairs, providing explicit, localized steering signals for more robust safety control. Furthermore, we introduce a vision language model (VLM)-based evaluation protocol that provides a fine-grained assessment, avoiding the pitfall of conventional binary evaluators that permit model collapse to bypass safety checks. Empirically, EBSG achieves state-of-the-art erasure performance across diverse safety datasets and concept categories, spanning four nudity benchmarks, seven I2P harmful-concept slices, and four MJA metaphor categories, while supporting multi-concept removal and transferring to a modern backbone, SD3.

</details>

Abstract: Stereo Video Super-Resolution (SVSR) aims to reconstruct high-resolution stereo videos by exploiting temporal cues across consecutive frames and complementary spatial cues between stereo views. However, existing methods struggle to recover high-frequency texture details under rapid motion and complex illumination, compromising reconstruction quality and stereo consistency. Event cameras provide valuable motion and texture cues with high temporal resolution and wide dynamic range, yet their potential for SVSR remains underexplored. To bridge this gap, we present the first investigation of event-guided SVSR and propose a novel framework. Specifically, we develop an Event-Guided Texture Enhancement (EGTE) module that combines bidirectional temporal feature propagation with event-guided texture refinement to restore high-frequency details in complex motion regions. Subsequently, we introduce a Cross-View Texture Interaction (CVTI) module to model cross-view spatial correspondences and aggregate cross-view complementary texture information. With this design, our method effectively integrates event-driven motion and texture information, inter-frame temporal dependencies, and cross-view spatial relationships to recover motion-affected details and maintain stereo consistency. Extensive experiments on both real-world and synthetic datasets demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms existing super-resolution methods in reconstruction quality and stereo consistency. CFS derives a target direction by contrasting forget and retain distributions, then applies a per-token projection proportional to each token's current alignment with this direction, yielding counterfactual representations that suppress the target while limiting collateral damage. Across 4,000 prompts derived from Celeb-100, spanning 100 celebrity identities, CFS achieves the strongest evaluated tradeoff between identity erasure of target identities and preservation of adjacent and generic concepts, while remaining stable across intervention strengths where other steering-based methods degrade. Quantitative nudity-suppression benchmarks and qualitative artistic-style examples provide evidence of transfer beyond identities on SD~1.4.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models offer a unified approach to perception, reasoning, and control for autonomous driving, but accurate action prediction alone does not establish compliance with traffic regulations. This paper presents a rule-guided VLA framework that incorporates symbolic traffic-rule feedback into policy optimisation. The results show improvements in both action prediction accuracy and encoded-rule compliance over the supervised baseline. The best-performing variant increases the overall rule pass rate from 92.9% to 97.6%, with zero observed violations of the encoded road-user-priority check in the evaluated samples. These findings support symbolic rule feedback as a means of improving VLA policy alignment with explicit traffic-rule criteria.

Abstract: Dataset distillation (DD) reduces storage and training costs by compressing large datasets into small collections. Most existing methods focus on image classification, where each image typically has a single category label. In contrast, object detection requires accurate category and bounding box annotations for multiple objects, together with visual content consistent with those annotations. Existing detection distillation pipelines rely on learned models and can alter the spatial relationships between objects and their backgrounds. We introduce GRASP, which packs dense native supervision into limited pixels without network training or inference. GRASP jointly searches over image selections and spatial layouts using convex allocation subproblems that penalize insufficient object resolution, excessive image compression, and padding that source context cannot fill. During composition, each selected image is cropped around all annotated objects and expanded into surrounding context without reducing object scale. The resized regions are then combined into distilled images, preserving the spatial layout within each region and retaining all original class labels and bounding box annotations. We evaluate GRASP on MS COCO and PASCAL VOC with compression ratios ranging from 0.25\% to 2\%. GRASP constructs the 1\% MS COCO dataset in under two minutes. Under a common training protocol using hard labels, GRASP achieves the state of the art with both CNN and ViT backbones. Code is available at https://github.com/tade1s/GRASP

Abstract: Shadows are a ubiquitous radiative attenuation phenomenon in remote sensing images and can severely affect high-resolution remote sensing interpretation tasks. In shadow removal, although introducing a mask prior provides explicit positional information for the restoration network, it also causes non-shadow regions to be modulated by the shadow prior, producing a systematic brightness shift; notably, this phenomenon is independent of whether the mask is accurate. In remote sensing images, non-shadow regions usually occupy a larger proportion, so a small shift over these regions incurs a much higher cost on whole-image metrics than an equal gain in shadow regions. Adopting a task-decoupling perspective, we therefore decompose shadow removal into two tasks, shadow-region restoration and non-shadow-region identity, within a cascaded architecture, and handle them sequentially. Based on this insight, we propose STARS, a cascaded shadow removal framework that separates regional responsibilities across stages. The method retains the advantages of the shadow mask while resolving the degradation of non-shadow regions, without requiring a complex parallel dual-branch structure. Meanwhile, to introduce large-model prior knowledge into remote sensing tasks, we further design a Feature Selection and Fusion Adapter (FSFA). We conduct extensive experiments on three benchmark datasets, UAV-SC+, ISTD+ and WSRD+ 2026, demonstrating the superiority of STARS. The framework shows that decoupling regional responsibilities can resolve the intrinsic contradiction of mask priors, providing a new direction for image restoration with region heterogeneity.

</details>

Abstract: Generating consistent multi-view RGB images and normal maps from a single input image provides complementary appearance and geometric cues for 3D reconstruction. However, maintaining cross-view consistency requires information exchange across viewpoints, and dense cross-view attention becomes computationally expensive as the number of views and spatial resolution increase. In this paper, we propose Global-Local Proxy Attention (GLPA) to reduce cross-view communication costs while preserving fine-grained within-view processing. Specifically, GLPA retains full-resolution interactions within each view and uses compact global proxies for cross-view communication. To further leverage the exchanged information, GLPA incorporates iterative proxy refinement, which rebuilds the proxies from updated features and reuses the same attention parameters for subsequent interactions within a single denoising step. Experiments on FLUX show that GLPA achieves a $2.65\times$ speedup in attention computation per denoising step over dense attention, while improving reconstruction IoU from 0.6849 to 0.7019 and reducing NVS LPIPS from 0.086 to 0.076. Results on Stable Diffusion further demonstrate the effectiveness of GLPA, with a $1.80\times$ attention speedup and improvements across all five quality metrics.

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have achieved strong performance in generation based video editing, but suffer from high inference latency due to the long token sequences formed by conditioning inputs and generated videos. Although training-free sparse attention reduces this cost, existing methods typically apply a fixed retention ratio or a single threshold shared across all tokens. In this work, we view video editing as conditional generation and identify a key insight: attention demand varies significantly both across different key sources (e.g., source videos, reference images, audio, text, and generated videos) and across queries within each source. Motivated by this, we propose QASK-Edit (Query-Adaptive Sparsity over Key Sources for Video Editing), a training-free sparse attention framework that allocates computation independently for each query and key source. Specifically, for each query, QASK-Edit considers both the attention mass and approximation risk of each block. It then evaluates candidate routing thresholds against each key source's risk budget, choosing exact computation or approximations to dynamically adjust sparsity for each query. Extensive experiments on three video editing models demonstrate that QASK-Edit achieves up to $1.46\times$ end-to-end speedup over dense attention pipeline while preserving both editing quality and unedited region fidelity. Furthermore, QASK-Edit generalizes directly to Reference-to-Video generation, delivering up to $1.36\times$ speedup while maintaining generation quality.

Abstract: Most neural reconstruction methods focus on reproducing scene appearance, yet inferring physical states from visual observations is essential for machine understanding of the physical world. Reconstructing the internal temperature and soot fields of radiating thermofluids such as flames is challenging yet essential for improving energy efficiency and assessing heat exposure and fire spread. In flames, temperature and soot concentration jointly govern visible emission, and soot self-absorption alters the light that reaches each camera. With limited views, changes in the two fields can offset one another in the recorded RGB images, making temperature and soot difficult to disentangle. ThermoSight, a physics-guided neural framework for scientific imaging, jointly reconstructs continuous three-dimensional temperature and soot volume fraction fields from limited calibrated flame images without field-level ground truth during optimization. A differentiable spectral model links these fields to measured RGB through thermal emission, self-absorption, and calibrated camera responses. Temperature-dependent emission spectra and wavelength-dependent attenuation constrain shared fields across RGB channels, reducing temperature-soot ambiguity. Chromaticity consistency, observation-derived spatial constraints, and soot regularization complement intensity fitting. In a synthetic ablation, wavelength-resolved attenuation reduces temperature RMSE in the hot, sooty region from 234 to 112 K relative to a three-channel approximation, with nearly identical RGB PSNR (65.23 vs. 65.24 dB). Measured data yield coherent fields and competitive RGB reconstructions. These results demonstrate that physics-guided neural fields recover interpretable internal physical quantities beyond visually consistent appearance.

</details>

Abstract: Remote-sensing (RS) image scene classification aims to assign a semantic category to an overhead image, and in practice must often do so from only a handful of labelled examples, since annotation requires expert interpretation and the resulting labels transfer poorly across sensors and regions. Recent progress has come from adapting vision-language models (VLMs) such as CLIP, through learned prompts or feature adapters. Although effective, these methods construct their representation entirely in the spatial domain. RS scenes are dominated by periodic texture and regular geometry, which the Fourier spectrum describes compactly through an amplitude component, carrying texture and contrast statistics, and a phase component, carrying spatial structure. Existing frequency-domain adaptation of VLMs either exploits amplitude alone or does not separate amplitude from phase, leaving the structural component unused. In this article, we propose PhaseCLIP, which inserts phase-capable spectral modules and a feature-gating module into a frozen CLIP visual encoder and trains only 1.30% of its parameters. Because the amplitude and phase pathways share an identical parameter layout, a single weight set is learned on phase and transferred exactly to amplitude, giving a two-stage scheme that adds no parameters. Experiments on five public RS datasets, EuroSAT, PatternNet, UC Merced, LSRS and RSSCN7, against eight baselines under a matched protocol, show that PhaseCLIP attains 84.28% mean accuracy and is the best method in 23 of 30 dataset-by-shot cells, with phase contributing 1.93 points overall and 4.36 points on the most difficult dataset. Because the modules act on the spectrum of image patches and transformer tokens and leave the text encoder untouched, the approach can be extended to other RS sensors and tasks, and to other domains in which class evidence lies in spatial structure.

</details>

Abstract: Image-to-3D foundation models have improved 3D reconstruction, but typically produce monolithic representations without explicit part-level structure. Part-aware generation and learning-based assembly methods often require part-level supervision, category-specific priors, or paired part-layout training data. We instead consider a practical but underexplored setting in which independently prepared or generated 3D parts have no predefined inter-part relationships, shared coordinate frames, or consistent scales, and must be assembled into a reference-consistent object from a single image. Given a reference image and fixed 3D part geometries, VA-Assembly optimizes each part's translation, rotation, and scale. We condition a frozen view-conditioned diffusion model on same-view renderings of a reference-derived monolithic 3D representation, producing score gradients geometrically aligned with the part configuration. This guidance provides multi-view structural cues under large initial misalignment. By repurposing pretrained visual-structural priors as transformation-level supervision, VA-Assembly requires no annotated part layouts, predefined part taxonomies, category-specific assembly training, or bounding-box-based layout initialization. Experiments compare VA-Assembly with geometric registration and compositional layout baselines using each baseline's supported transformation components, showing improved part-wise transformation estimation. Ablation studies show that view-aligned conditioning, rather than diffusion guidance alone, is critical for reliable transformation gradients. Classical methods make different assumptions about images, from simple averaging to directional edge-aware interpolation, and the directional methods cost markedly more compute. We ask a practical question that a per-image quality ranking does not answer: for a given image, is the expensive directional method worth its cost? It ranges from $-6.6$ to $+6.8$ dB, and on 31% of images it is zero or negative, meaning a cheap method matches or beats the expensive one. The gain is also predictable from content: it correlates with mean image saturation at $r=-0.86$ ($p<10^{-6}$). We then build an interpretable depth-2 decision tree that recommends the best cheap method from image descriptors, beating an always-pick-the-most-common baseline by 20.0 percentage points under leave-one-image-out validation. Abstract: Robust semantic segmentation in adverse weather is essential for safety-critical autonomous systems. On the official ACDC test server, LiDSeg-PIDNet achieves 75.07% pooled all-condition mIoU, comparable to supervised HRNet-W48 at 75.0%, while remaining substantially smaller. The gains generalize zero-shot to Foggy Driving and Foggy Zurich without training on real-fog images. LiDSeg adds only 1.9-3.1M parameters across several models, with total model sizes of 5.2-39.5M parameters and no inference-time reconstruction overhead. Abstract: Flow-matching point-cloud upsamplers refine sparse inputs into dense point clouds by integrating a learned velocity field with a fixed temporal discretization. However, local reconstruction requirements vary substantially across a shape, while a shared solver budget applies the same temporal resolution everywhere. We propose Multi-Rate Flow Matching (MRFM), a patch-adaptive framework that selects an integration budget for each local patch at inference time while keeping the learned velocity field frozen. MRFM relaxes the discrete budget into a continuous variable, optimizes it online through differentiable soft rollouts using an input-derived geometric fidelity objective, and projects the result back to a discrete budget for the final solver rollout. Experiments on PU1K and PUGAN show strong reconstruction quality, while matched-budget permutation and multi-factor analyses demonstrate that the benefit depends on patch-specific allocation rather than on the step distribution alone. MRFM is accuracy-oriented and intentionally uses additional test-time optimization to obtain more suitable local temporal resolutions.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts Multimodal Large Language Models (MoE-MLLMs) offer scalable inference through sparse expert activation, yet substantial computational redundancy remains among the activated experts. Existing expert skipping methods typically calibrate thresholds based on target skipping ratios or final-output divergence, overlooking the accumulation of skipping-induced error across layers and the varying semantic importance of individual tokens. To address that, we propose ProSE, a training-free, semantic-guided expert skipping framework that probes layer-wise reconstruction error to promote representation consistency compared with default activation. Specifically, we construct query-aware semantic masks to identify salient visual tokens and progressively calibrate modality-specific skipping thresholds for each MoE layer. By minimizing masked reconstruction error under a given budget, ProSE maintains semantic consistency after skipping and allows adaptive expert allocation under limited activation budgets. Extensive experiments on 9 benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements on average over existing expert skipping methods, particularly under aggressive skipping settings. Abstract: Vision models built on Mamba are often explained through their selective step size Delta, on the assumption that a token the scan does not admit cannot influence the prediction. We test that in a concept bottleneck model for lung-nodule malignancy built on a single gated, bidirectional selective state-space block. The answer becomes an interpretability framework: a guarantee, a procedure that checks it, and a pre-registered audit of what it explains. For a fixed gate pattern, masking the input of the causal convolution of the block and giving closed tokens zero weight at readout makes the concept and task logits exactly invariant to the closed tokens. Gating Delta is neither necessary nor sufficient. On LIDC-IDRI, a path-by-path knockout over 15 cross-validated models agrees with this in every run: the largest logit change is exactly 0 whenever the conditions hold and between 4.1 and 6.7 when they do not. The gated model reaches a pooled out-of-fold AUROC of 0.8425 +/- 0.0085, level with an ungated Mamba bottleneck (0.8428). The guarantee belongs to the masked model, not to the gate as an explanation. Post-hoc rankers, which inherit the guarantee on this model, order tokens better than the gate. Gate maps from a model trained on permuted labels are as close to the real maps as two real-label seeds are to each other. Under retraining, the tokens of the gate carry more task information than random tokens and, on shifted crops, more than central ones, but no more than the tokens chosen by hidden attention. We therefore read the gate as a certified support set produced by mask supervision. Splitting the Oracle Impurity Score into own-concept and cross-concept parts reveals differences the aggregate score misses. Partitioning the readout reduced leakage slightly under an earlier recipe, and the final model sits at, rather than below, the level implied by correlations among the true concepts.

Abstract: While the ability of video generation models to accurately depict scientific phenomena has received growing attention, determining whether these models genuinely understand the underlying principles remains a critical challenge. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate video samples in isolation, making it difficult to expose models that simply fit superficial visual priors. In scientific experiments, a small change in one condition produces a markedly different outcome even when the rest of the setup remains fixed. Capitalizing on this sensitivity, we introduce SciWorldBench, a benchmark comprising paired scientific experiments. Each pair varies a single condition in an otherwise fixed setup, yielding two mutually exclusive expected outcomes. Each generation prompt specifies the condition but omits the expected outcome. We evaluate ten text-and-image-to-video (TI2V) models using an independent vision-language model (VLM) that assesses each video without access to the condition text or the label identifying the correct outcome. A pair is scored as correct only when both videos depict their respective scientifically correct outcomes. Across all ten models, we find that the two videos in an experimental pair frequently depict the same outcome even though the conditions require different results. This recurring failure suggests that current video world models struggle to apply the underlying scientific principles reliably during generation. We will publicly release the benchmark dataset and open-source the evaluation code.

</details>

Abstract: Image generators are good at drawing scenes in which things that belong together appear next to each other. In almost every image they generate, and in almost every benchmark used to evaluate these images, "belonging to the same whole" and "adjacent to each other" go together. Therefore, we cannot know whether the model truly grasps the scene as a whole, or simply makes each part connect with its adjacent parts. We propose GJBench to disentangle the two. It requires the model to draw a scene as a scattered jigsaw puzzle: an image is cut into interlocking pieces, then moved apart and rotated. Thus, the spatial plausibility of the scene exists only in how these pieces collectively point to a whole. The model may have a reference image, a target layout, both, or neither. We separately evaluate three requirements: whether the scene is broken down into a specified number of separate pieces, whether the pieces interlock, and whether the pieces can reconstruct the reference scene. While every open and closed model we evaluated could draw these scenes well as a whole, none could keep them together as scattered puzzle pieces, and the three requirements categorized failures into different patterns. The models draw separate pieces but miscount them in different ways, and unless their outlines are provided, the pieces rarely interlock, and their content does not reconstruct the reference image. We also found that the target layout helps the puzzle pieces fit together, while the reference image improves neither their count nor their fit. These findings show that keeping a scene continuous across space that the canvas no longer shows remains an open problem for image generators.

</details>

Abstract: Palmprint recognition and synthesis methods focus on extracting discriminative textures or generating images with varied textures and appearances. However, they do not generally provide an explicit image-level representation that separates palmprint texture from its underlying base appearance for independent analysis or controlled editing. We introduce Palmprint Decomposer, an interpretable framework that represents a palmprint through independently editable factors and reconstructs the image through a multiplicative composition model. A color map and a light map characterize the base appearance, while a structure map and a global texture strength parameter describe texture modulation. However, accurate reconstruction alone does not guarantee separation of palmprint texture from base appearance, as fine palmprint textures may leak into the estimated base appearance. To mitigate this ambiguity, we train the decomposer exclusively on synthetic data with direct factor supervision, using asymmetric decoding paths and decomposition constraints to suppress texture leakage. Instance-Wise Refinement further improves real-image reconstruction by directly optimizing the predicted factors for each input without relying on the trained network. Experiments across seven datasets demonstrate high reconstruction fidelity, with the extracted structure maps generally achieving verification performance comparable to the original images. Compared with existing methods, our approach achieves better factor separation while preserving more discriminative palmprint texture information.

</details>

Abstract: Tool-handover interaction calls for preparation before a response is warranted. The model waits until interaction cues appear, then prepares while assessing whether the accumulating evidence supports a response or calls for cancellation. We introduce EviBridge, a framework that integrates predictive visual representations into a vision-language interaction model for joint state and tool prediction. A trainable evidence interface maps features from a frozen V-JEPA 2 encoder into the language decoder alongside native visual inputs, preserving their temporal context and using only observations available at each decision time. The framework has two instantiations. EviBridge-E distinguishes recent from historical evidence and encodes the timing and spatial location of each observation. EviBridge-I preserves the temporal order of pooled V-JEPA 2 features and interaction turns and generates dedicated interaction tokens. We evaluate on FuGIBench, a streaming-video benchmark for preparation, response, and cancellation in tool-handover assistance. The best-performing variants of EviBridge-E and EviBridge-I achieve Overall scores of 0.6448 and 0.5892, compared with 0.2178 and 0.5327 for their respective task-adapted native baselines. These gains span target handovers, long waits, and cancelled requests, demonstrating the value of predictive visual evidence for interaction decisions.

Abstract: Despite recent advances, vision-language models (VLMs) still struggle with fine-grained visual understanding, often failing to effectively utilize critical evidence already encoded in their visual representations. Existing approaches address this limitation through repeated visual processing or explicit reasoning, introducing substantial computational overhead. Abstract: Natural photographs contain dense, overlapping structures that can be weakly expressed in feature maps from whole-image vision encoders. We introduce forced focused attention (FFA) and its Python implementation, vividRGB. FFA is an encoder-agnostic inference-time procedure that applies a frozen encoder to enlarged, overlapping local crops, averages inverse-aligned reflections, and reconstructs a feature field in source coordinates. We evaluate 50 high-resolution photographs from grassland vegetation, fungal networks, airborne forests, coral reefs, and phytoplankton microscopy, using DINOv3 ViT-B/16. Across the five domains, FFA at 5% or 10% focus produces higher mean RGB-edge boundary F1 than direct DINOv3 encoding and automatic SAM3 masks. Domain means range from 0.244 to 0.596 for FFA, versus 0.028-0.076 for direct DINOv3 and 0.020-0.090 for SAM3. Qualitative feature maps also show finer appearance structure. These results concern RGB-edge preservation rather than verified species, traits, or biological segmentation. Narrower focus generally improves boundary preservation, while extraction time and GPU energy use increase sharply and gains diminish at finer settings. FFA may be useful for relatively small, high-resolution ecological image collections where dense visual cues matter. The alpha release vividRGB 0.1.0 and versioned research resources support reproducible cue inspection.

Abstract: Medical image understanding with vision-language models (VLMs) typically relies on autoregressively generating reasoning and answer tokens. These unnecessary tokens make the inference cost scale with the response length and force the answer to be parsed from free-form text, incurring avoidable latency and decision errors. To this end, we introduce \textsc{MedJev}, a \emph{system one} vision-language decision model that maps medical images, questions, and candidate answers directly to decisions in a single multimodal forward pass, without any autoregressive generation process. For fast inference, \textsc{MedJev} removes the upper language layers and the generative output pathway, while keeping the full vision pathway and all visual tokens. On this shortened backbone, a decision head scores each candidate by pretrained query--key matching and refines the scores with a lightweight multilayer perceptron (MLP) residual, coupling the pretrained matching structure with task-specific nonlinear discrimination. Built on Qwen3.5-4B, \textsc{MedJev}-4B improves both accuracy and speed over the original model on each of four medical image classification and visual question answering benchmarks, with an absolute accuracy gain of \textbf{7.93\%} and a \textbf{6.04$\times$} end-to-end speedup. Further comparison with 20 cutting-edge models shows that \textsc{MedJev}-4B achieves state-of-the-art performance and inference speed among 4B--9B general and medical VLMs, indicating that direct multimodal decision making is an effective route to fast and effective medical image understanding.
