With the notation removed (a post-hoc check), the F1 scores tie (0.575 vs 0.594), and the check flags only 13% of number errors, because most errors are misses that both engines share. What remains: the OCR baseline has a lower body character error rate (0.012 vs 0.053 rasterized). The pipeline raises extra-number recall at page level (+0.145; the document-level interval touches 0), mostly by restoring page numbers, but lowers heading-level agreement (0.133 to 0.061) and leaves spurious formulas unchanged; its formula repair never fires. A pre-specified precision gate for its flags (0.80, human-judged) is met at 0.929, yet the post-processing hypothesis fails on its improvement items. We recommend scoring numbers after removing notation and checking critical figures against the source. By the pre-specified criteria, the study stays a technical report.

Abstract: Rapid advances in image and video generation have enabled high-fidelity synthesis at higher resolutions and longer durations, making the quadratic cost of dense self-attention a major bottleneck as visual token sequences grow. Sparse attention seeks to preserve generation quality at lower computational cost by retaining informative query-key interactions and omitting less consequential ones, with end-to-end gains depending on the cost of constructing sparse support and the efficiency with which the system maps the resulting workload to hardware. We survey sparse attention for visual generation through a 4+1+3 analytical framework that links algorithmic design to system implementation. The framework examines how static and dynamic methods use source information and the evidence derived from it to decide which interactions to retain and at what granularity, while accounting for the roles of reuse in reducing repeated computation and correction in compensating for omitted contributions. We relate these algorithmic choices to their implementation on hardware through the framework's three system dimensions of data organization, execution, and dataflow. We summarize common evaluation metrics and discuss open challenges and research directions, providing researchers and practitioners with a reference for the joint design of sparse attention algorithms and systems.

Abstract: Visual token pruning reduces the inference cost of Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, but attention-based scores do not directly measure a token's effect on the predicted action. We define action sensitivity as the magnitude of the first-order action change when a token is replaced by its camera's mean token and estimate it for all tokens with a single backward pass. Computing it online is costly: scoring on the full (unpruned) graph needs an extra forward pass at every control step. Scoring on the pruned forward pass is cheaper but causes scoring-graph lock-in: removed tokens lose their direct paths through later layers and tend to receive lower scores, making those locations less likely to return. On replayed observations, even with a random conditioning mask, pruned-graph scoring re-selects 59% of the mask's tokens, versus 6% expected by chance; attention histories show a similar bias. POKE (Probe Offline, Keep Efficiently) computes full-graph action sensitivities offline and distills them into a student with under 0.3M parameters. At inference, the student scores the current observation without backpropagation or previous masks, and score-guided diversity selection retains the tokens. Keeping 32 of 512 visual tokens on LIBERO, POKE achieves 95.2% success on OpenVLA-OFT and 90.8% on $π_{0.5}$ at $1.85\times$ and $1.26\times$ speedups, outperforming attention-, diversity-, and distillation-based pruners, with larger margins on LIBERO-Plus.

</details>

Abstract: Few-step autoregressive video diffusion enables efficient long video generation, but inference still incurs substantial computational costs from repeated denoising and KV cache operations. Existing acceleration methods reduce parts of this overhead, while historical KV reading and cache construction remain costly across video chunks. To address these challenges, we propose ScoutCache, a training-free framework that jointly optimizes denoising, historical KV reading, and cache construction. We observe that shallow feature changes provide useful information for estimating clean latent updates, while historical KV dependence varies across Transformer layers and attention heads but remains relatively stable across video chunks. Based on these observations, we develop Conditional Denoising Readout (CDR) to estimate intermediate denoising updates from shallow feature changes and known noise variations. We further introduce Output Risk Guided Historical KV Reading (ORHR) to allocate historical reading budgets based on attention output perturbations, and Content and Coverage Guided Sparse Cache Construction (CCSC) to reduce cache construction costs by selecting informative spatial tokens while maintaining spatial coverage. Extensive experiments on Self-Forcing and LongLive using VBench and MovieGen demonstrate that ScoutCache achieves up to 2.28$\times$ and 1.86$\times$ speedups, respectively, while consistently improving generation quality without modifying pretrained model parameters.

Abstract: Even in multi-camera setups, occlusions can leave individual body joints with only a single usable 2D observation. Such an observation defines a viewing ray but leaves the joint's depth undetermined. We introduce Body of Evidence (BoE), a multi-view 3D human pose estimator that predicts per-camera joint depths alongside 2D joint locations. To reduce monocular depth ambiguity, we propose geometry-conditioned depth prediction, which combines image features with previously reconstructed 3D body geometry as contextual depth cues. We further introduce learnable depth-aware triangulation (LDAT), which augments differentiable algebraic triangulation with predicted depth constraints, enabling reconstruction even from a single contributing camera while preserving the benefits of geometric triangulation when multiple views are available. The reconstructed 3D poses subsequently guide depth prediction in the next iteration, allowing depth estimates and 3D geometry to progressively refine each other. Experiments on CMU Panoptic demonstrate state-of-the-art in-domain performance and superior average performance across evaluated camera subsets and unseen camera arrangements. BoE is particularly effective under sparse camera coverage, outperforming the previous state of the art by 10.6 AP25 points in the challenging three-camera setting.

Abstract: Dataset distillation (DD) replaces large training datasets with smaller synthetic ones to reduce computational costs. While traditionally used for classification, we present, to our knowledge, the first DD study for line-level handwritten text recognition (HTR). Unlike classification tasks, which use discrete labels, HTR employs unaligned sequences from a virtually infinite output space as labels. To adapt classification-based DD methods to this scenario, we define text pseudo-classes by clustering transcriptions based on character n-grams. We adapt gradient matching (GM), distribution matching (DM) and trajectory matching (MTT) to a Convolutional Recurrent Neural Network (CRNN) trained with Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC). In this process, synthetic line images are generated by optimising only the pixels of real starting lines (either randomly chosen or selected as text medoids), while keeping their original transcriptions fixed. Across four datasets (IAM, George Washington, READ\,2016, LAM) and three budgets (1--10\,\% of the training lines), no distilled set outperforms its real initialisation lines. Random lines prove better than medoids, and further optimisation only degrades recognition. Methodologically, DM lowers its objective without benefit, while GM and MTT barely move theirs. We turn these findings into guidelines for evaluating data compression in HTR.

</details>

Abstract: Egocentric hand motion provides physical motion supervision for dexterous manipulation and vision-language-action pretraining. Reliable use of this supervision requires accurate and temporally coherent 4D hand reconstruction in metric world space. Existing methods typically lift framewise estimates produced under weak perspective into world coordinates. Abstract: Learning visual policies requires turning interaction experience into guidance for individual decisions. Trajectory outcomes obscure action quality, while unguided exploration can revisit familiar situations. We present \textsc{LightAct}, an experience-learning framework that connects skill-guided exploration with policy internalization. Interaction segments are summarized into experience entries and consolidated into a game-specific skill that guides root collection toward broader state coverage. At each root, candidate actions are compared through short rollouts under a shared frozen policy. Environment-reported score gains are averaged across continuations and normalized into relative action rewards, producing reusable supervision for reinforcement learning. Policy training uses a compact dataset of just 1,024 decision roots collected in a single source game and paired with their action rewards. Across 170 GameWorld tasks spanning 34 games, \textsc{LightAct} improves overall progress by 23.6, 29.2, and 13.0 percentage points at the 2B, 4B, and 9B scales, respectively, with our strongest model competitive with reported proprietary-model results. Ablations show broader root-state coverage with skill guidance and consistent gains across three policy optimizers. Case studies show how rollouts distinguish actions with identical immediate gains.

Abstract: Public hubs offer many personalized LoRAs per text-to-image backbone, yet composing two of them in one image remains fragile. In FLUX, keeping a pool of $K=10$ or $20$ subject LoRAs loaded makes their deltas interfere, and even loading only the two requested LoRAs keeps both subjects in fewer than 4% of images: one subject vanishes, the two fuse, or the image splits into a collage. We present MORE (Merge Once, Route Early), a training-free two-stage pipeline for FLUX. Merge once: the whole pool is folded into a single fixed-rank adapter by the subspace-regression router of SSR-Merge, made reliable by ridge, rank and calibration choices, and factored exactly into a shared part and per-subject parts. Route early: during the first 30% of the denoising steps each subject's part is gated to its layout region; a decaying soft attention bias ties every region to its own subject tokens, and a cyclic swap guidance extrapolates away from the prediction with the subjects' regions permuted, which counteracts region homogenization. Abstract: Cross-domain few-shot segmentation adapts natural-image pretrained encoders to medical or industrial domains from a few pixel-annotated support masks. Within frozen DINOv3, intermediate per-head attention messages already carry target-background distinctions that baseline final-layer similarities and predictions do not consistently reflect. We propose the State-Message Interaction Adapter (SMIA) to learn how per-head messages should update each receiving token representation while keeping pretrained attention and FFN parameters frozen. Inserted right after per-head aggregation, SMIA conditions the transformation of each head's aggregated output (message) on each patch's pre-attention feature (state), allowing similar contextual messages to produce distinct or opposing updates depending on the receiving position. Because scarce support masks cover only limited state-message combinations, a shared low-rank bilinear map confines the learned interactions to low-dimensional state, message, and output subspaces. Added before each adapted block's frozen FFN, these updates yield adapted features that are refined by frozen FFNs and deeper blocks for linear readout. With only 0.51M trainable parameters and single-pass, support-free inference, SMIA achieves six-dataset mean mIoUs of 77.40% at 5 shots and 80.99% at 10 shots across 20 medical and industrial tasks, surpassing the strongest prior methods by +3.61 and +3.93 percentage points. Abstract: CT-to-PET synthesis estimates functional PET images from anatomical CT scans for auxiliary analysis, representation pretraining, and missing-modality completion. Brownian Bridge diffusion models can produce multiple PET candidates of different quality under the same CT condition, exposing an intra-condition quality hierarchy. Candidate quality spans complementary spatial, structural, and intensity dimensions, while DPO-only fine-tuning may compromise absolute synthesis fidelity. We propose MedicalDPO which converts this hierarchy into preference supervision automatically constructed from paired CT-PET training data. Multiple candidates are ranked across the three fidelity dimensions, and strict cross-metric verification retains preferred-rejected pairs with consistent quality separation. Relative denoising-error preference optimization is jointly trained with the original Brownian Bridge objective to anchor the pretrained CT-to-PET mapping, while condition-balanced multi-pair rotation distributes preference supervision across CT conditions. Further analyses demonstrate metric complementarity and clear pair separation, while ablations show that the original training objective prevents the substantial degradation caused by DPO-only fine-tuning.

Abstract: Recovering 3D pose and shape of birds from single images is bottlenecked by the long tail of avian diversity: out of ~10,000 species, only a few dozen have 3D-labeled training data, and in-the-wild test sets routinely contain species never seen at training. Generalizing across clades is therefore a central problem. The current state of the art, AniMer+, mitigates it with a family-level contrastive loss that conveys only a binary same-family indicator per pair and discards the continuous structure of the species tree. We propose a taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck prior on the shape code of the AVES parametric bird mesh model: a multivariate Gaussian whose covariance decays exponentially with Linnaean rank distance, with a learnable selection strength and noise scale. The prior generalizes family contrastive into a continuous, generative form, exposing the graded cross- and within-family similarity that a binary indicator cannot, and brings continuous comparative phylogenetics in as a biology-grounded inductive bias for the long tail. Our method improves over AniMer+ on both metrics of the unseen-species CowBird benchmark and on CtrlAVES3D, while remaining comparable on seen-species CUB. A permutation ablation isolates taxonomic structure from generic Gaussian smoothing, and a rank-granularity ablation identifies cross-order distinctions (the structure beyond a family-vs-not indicator) as the dominant source of the unseen-species gain.

Gaze conveys the approach side: when gaze is rotated around the object, the approach turns with it in 59.1% of cases, against 20.5% for a text model retrained with clock words. Abstract: Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a model trained on a labelled source domain to an unlabelled target domain without access to the source data. Almost the entire SFDA literature assumes mutually exclusive classes, yet many deployed settings are multi-label. This paper formulates SFDA for multi-label classification and establishes the first benchmark for it: five adaptation tasks over six datasets spanning remote sensing, natural, clipart-style and driving imagery, with four single-label SFDA methods extended to provide the first baselines and a published multi-label method run alongside them, over three seeds. The benchmark shows that extending a single-label method is unreliable rather than merely suboptimal: half of the extended baselines fail to improve on the unadapted source model at all, and on two of the five tasks none of them does. The failures are not incidental. Nearest-centroid pseudo-labelling, softmax-based confidence, the diversity term of information maximization and confidence-weighted prototype construction each rely on mutual exclusivity, and each fails differently without it, so replacing the softmax with per-class sigmoids is a valid but insufficient extension. Multi-label Prototype Alignment with Contrastive Teaching (MPACT) is proposed in response, a mean-teacher framework whose objective, prototype construction and alignment term are each reconsidered for overlapping label sets. It attains the best average on every reported metric and is the only method that improves on the unadapted source model on all five tasks. The analysis further introduces a prototype-agreement diagnostic separating feature-space alignment from self-reinforcing agreement with the classifier, and reports a negative result for confidence-based pseudo-label filtering.

</details>

Abstract: Visible artifacts are an unreliable cue for detecting generated videos, because newer generators learn to remove them. We instead detect a trace left by the generation pipeline itself. Most video generators decode latents with a 3D causal VAE that groups consecutive frames in time, so the fidelity of generated frames rises and falls periodically with their position in a group. We call this periodic trace VAE Rhythm. Real videos are captured frame by frame and do not have it. To reveal it without training, we propose a phase scan. The scan reconstructs a clip with an off-the-shelf VAE from successive start offsets and measures the periodicity of the reconstruction error across these offsets. We find VAE Rhythm in most of the 39 variants of 31 generators we test, including the closed-source Sora, Kling 3.0 and Seedance 2.0. We use the rhythm as a zero-shot score, as a plug-in for existing detectors and as a data engine that pairs real videos with their VAE reconstructions to train our detector CaDet. On DF26, the training-free rhythm score outperforms all 15 retrained baselines, and as a plug-in it improves all 19 tested detectors by 6.7% to 23.7% AUROC. CaDet achieves the best AUROC on AIGVDBench, DF26 and three 2026 closed-source models, and its variant trained without any generated video still exceeds 90% on AIGVDBench.

</details>

Abstract: Feed-forward 4D driving-scene reconstruction enables efficient modeling of dynamic autonomous-driving scenes, but current methods suffer from two complementary failure modes: semantic leakage of traffic participants into the static representation and geometric drift of Gaussian locations across viewpoints. We propose \textbf{IGRecon4D}, a feed-forward dynamic scene reconstruction framework that addresses these failures with training-time instance-prior distillation and LiDAR-anchored Gaussian positioning. Specifically, an instance-level semantic branch provides a potentially dynamic participant prior, which is distilled into dynamic-mask learning through the Instance-Dynamic Consistency Constraint to encourage high-recall localization of traffic-participant regions prone to dynamic-static leakage. In parallel, sparse LiDAR measurements guide dense depth estimation, and the resulting depths are back-projected to anchor Gaussian positions for improved multi-view geometric consistency. Experiments on the Waymo Open Dataset and zero-shot evaluation on nuScenes demonstrate improved full-image reconstruction on the main benchmark and stronger traffic-participant-region reconstruction under cross-dataset evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Weakly-supervised Video Anomaly Detection (VAD) models assign anomaly scores from high-dimensional features, and their predictions remain unexplained. Saliency maps show where a model looks but not what it relies on, and the descriptions produced by Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are generated by branches that the anomaly score never reads. To address this faithfulness gap, we propose \textbf{Explain The Unexplainable (ETU)}, a classifier-agnostic framework that audits frozen VAD models through counterfactual interventions in their feature space. ETU maps video features to a discrete dependency graph of agents, actions and outcomes ($A_r \to A_a \to A_o$), regenerates the features by graph-conditioned diffusion with selected nodes of the graph masked, and measures the change in the score of a frozen detector; one set of interventions is applied unchanged to every detector. The generator is trained only to reconstruct the input features, so it cannot learn the contrast it is asked to measure, and we validate the audit with experiments that test every alternative explanation of its results, from the cost of regeneration to random deletions of matched size. On UCF-Crime features obtained from a CLIP encoder, we show that substituting the scene semantics results in the largest drop for most detectors, and that removing the outcome chain of the graph ($Δ_{rel}$) changes the anomaly score of all six audited detectors far more than removing the same number of random nodes, even though the random deletion perturbs the features about as much. We show that this targeted behavior comes from the divergence objective of the graph embedding. Without it, the two interventions become indistinguishable, and with a divergence objective in the generator as well, the score change of the detector follows the displacement of the reconstruction rather than the semantics that were removed.

</details>

Abstract: Anomalies are local events, yet weakly supervised video anomaly detection scores them globally. Most methods pool an entire frame into a single vector, rank frames by that vector, and derive a location afterwards as a saliency map of the score. Full-frame pooling mixes the anomaly with its background, so a detector can reach competitive benchmark scores from scene context without being able to show where in the scene the anomaly occurs. We introduce Region-VAD, which first selects a region and then detects the anomaly from that region alone. Region-VAD selects an explicit rectangle over the tokens of a frozen foundation model and scores anomalousness only from the tokens inside the box, through attention pooling followed by a standard temporal head. The rectangle is not learned, it is the box in which the frozen tokens change most between consecutive segments, in all four feature projections of the encoder, and it is found by exhaustive search over the rectangles of the token grid. Only the pooling and the temporal head are trained, from video-level labels without boxes, masks or frame-level annotations, and no trained component reads a token outside the selected region. Experiments on five datasets, with one localizer configuration shared by all datasets and backbones, show that Region-VAD achieves state-of-the-art anomaly localization on UCF-Crime and MSAD at competitive detection performance.

</details>

Abstract: Radar represents a key modality for weather-robust perception. Unlike optical sensors such as camera and LiDAR, radar can penetrate fog, rain and snow due to its longer wavelength, and recent hardware advances have steadily improved its spatial resolution, thus making it increasingly relevant for autonomous systems. While processing raw radar data requires substantial memory computation, most existing work has focused on preprocessed point-cloud data similar to LiDAR. However, the sparse radar points and sensor-relative anisotropy make LiDAR-oriented encoders suboptimal. To address this problem, we present DRaGAN, a dynamic graph attention network for multi-radar point clouds. DRaGAN introduces a sensor-relative Fourier encoding of angle and range to capture fixed-aperture radar characteristics, gated short- and long-range hub nodes along each sensor's line of sight for efficient local-global message passing, and attention-guided dynamic rewiring that prunes weak edges and adds a bounded number of latent connections restricted to two-hop neighborhoods. This reduces the number of processed edges in deeper layers while preserving representation quality. On TruckScenes, DRaGAN improves test NDS by 10.10 points over the evaluated RadarGNN baseline and reduces the reported model FLOP count by 63.7%. This trade-off includes an increase in model latency from 40 to 53.16 ms and in trainable parameters from 1.2M to 1.7M on the evaluated implementations. Code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/chr-is-tof/DRaGAN.

Abstract: Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly and follow two main paradigms: one involves concatenating visual and textual tokens for input into Large Language Model (LLM), while the other injects visual knowledge into Feed-Forward Networks (FFNs) of the LLM. However, the former introduces a large number of redundant visual tokens, substantially increasing computational overhead. The latter reduces sequence length but removes images from the input stage entirely, which may hinder the activation of image-related knowledge. Moreover, naively injecting visual knowledge into the FFNs could induce activation distribution shifts due to cross-modal semantic discrepancies. To address these challenges, we develop Beyond Token Stacking (BTS), a novel visual enhancement framework for MLLMs. Specifically, we propose a Multi-scale Visual Modulation (MVM) mechanism that injects multi-scale visual information at the input stage via modulation without increasing the token sequence length. Furthermore, we develop an Adaptive Fused-feature Calibration (AFC) module that performs per-channel affine calibration to decouple visual injection from distribution alignment in the FFN layers, improving multimodal fusion stability while preserving language priors. Experiments show that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>

Abstract: Recent inversion-free, flow-based image editing methods evolve the source image toward the target via a differential velocity field. We observe that this velocity field does not explicitly account for which regions should be preserved, leading to unintended drift in non-editing regions. To address this, we formulate source fidelity control as a stochastic optimal control problem in which a selective fidelity term on the editing residual is enforced along the editing trajectory. Solving this problem and approximating its solution yields the Residual Gating Principle: each latent component is pulled back toward the source with a gain that decays exponentially with the local residual magnitude, so that small deviations are suppressed while large, semantically meaningful edits pass through unimpeded. The proposed method, RAGE, requires only one lightweight gated interpolation per sampling step, involves no mask estimation, attention maps, retraining, or architecture-specific modifications, and is applied on top of FlowEdit-based editors. On PIE-Bench, RAGE achieves the best overall rank among 17 methods and substantially improves background preservation on both Stable Diffusion 3 and FLUX backbones while maintaining competitive editing quality.

</details>

Abstract: Semi-supervised object detection has recently been extended to DETR, where oriented boxes introduce an ambiguity that does not arise for axis-aligned boxes: the map from parameters to rectangles is two-to-one, since $(c_x,c_y,w,h,θ)$ and $(c_x,c_y,h,w,θ+π/2)$ denote the same rotated rectangle. Losses and matching costs are computed from the parameters, so two boxes that coincide in space can be far apart in the metric that drives training. A correctly localized student box is then penalized by the regression loss, and the inflated matching cost leads the Gaussian mixture model (GMM) filter to discard it. We propose Oriented Semi-DETR, which removes this ambiguity wherever a pseudo box enters training. Swap-aligned Target Selection regresses towards the encoding closer to the student prediction. A Swap-invariant Reliability Cost minimizes over the two encodings inside the matching cost, so the filter decides on geometry alone. Swap-aware Pseudo-label Denoising starts denoising earlier, ramps its loss weight linearly, and weights each query by the teacher score and encoding margin. All three are parameter-free and require no additional forward pass. Across all label ratios of DOTA-v2.0, Oriented Semi-DETR outperforms DETR-based supervised and semi-supervised baselines, with the largest gains at the strict IoU threshold.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification for novel view synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assesses rendering reliability at unseen viewpoints. This reliability depends on the discrepancy between rendered and true colors, which cannot be measured without ground-truth images. We therefore quantify uncertainty in this unknown discrepancy through upper bounds on per-pixel RGB mean absolute error. However, learned bounds may underestimate these errors and overstate rendering reliability, motivating calibration to a prescribed coverage level. In this paper, we propose QuantileGS, a post-hoc framework that combines learned rendering-error quantiles with scale-normalized conformal calibration to produce spatially adaptive error bounds. The framework comprises three phases. Phase 1, Rendering-Error Computation, measures RGB mean absolute error between observed images and predictions from a frozen 3DGS model. Phase 2, Ordered Gaussian Quantile Fields, learns two scalar attributes per Gaussian to predict lower and upper rendering-error quantiles. Phase 3, Scale-Normalized Conformal Calibration, calibrates the upper prediction on held-out images, using the quantile gap to scale the correction. Under the stated assumptions, the resulting bounds provide finite-sample marginal coverage. At a nominal coverage level of 90%, calibration raises mean empirical test coverage from 69.6% to 89.6% on LLFF and from 80.1% to 92.1% on Mip-NeRF 360. On LF, coverage remains conservative at 96.3% before and 96.4% after calibration. Applications to active view selection, Gaussian pruning, and RGB-D simultaneous localization and mapping illustrate how these bounds inform downstream decisions.

Abstract: Many materials and tissues, including carbon-nanotube sheets, collagen and paper, comprise overlapping strands whose arrangement influences their effective properties. Measuring this geometry from micrographs requires individual strands to be traced through crossings and signal gaps, which merge distinct strands or split one strand into disconnected fragments. We present PLECTA (from Latin plecta, a braid), a deterministic method that segments individual strands using only a binary image whose foreground pixels mark strand centrelines. At each junction, PLECTA selects which strand segments continue into one another while allowing segments to terminate. It formulates this decision as an exact maximum-weight partial-matching problem, with an explicit penalty for unpaired segments and no restriction on the number of segments entering a junction. The output permits crossing pixels to belong to multiple strands. On synthetic scenes, PLECTA resolved strand identities more accurately than three released alternatives at every density tested. The same ranking held on an independently published generator run at its own settings. A separate stage uses the corresponding greyscale micrograph to estimate which strand lies on top at each crossing. Abstract: Foundation models have demonstrated strong feature extraction capabilities across various medical imaging tasks, yet their potential for MRI reconstruction remains largely unexplored, particularly within unrolled reconstruction networks and under artifact-corrupted inputs. In this work, we explore whether and how features from pretrained foundation models can benefit accelerated cardiac cine MRI reconstruction. To this end, we propose FM-Recon, a framework that refines and integrates extracted features from frozen image encoder of foundation models into an unrolled reconstruction network. Through extensive experiments on the CMRxRecon2023 dataset, we systematically evaluate foundation model features across different reconstruction settings. Our results show that foundation models can extract informative representations from artifact-corrupted inputs and improve reconstruction performance, particularly at moderate acceleration factors. These findings demonstrate the potential of foundation model features for accelerated MRI reconstruction and provide insights into their effective integration into unrolled reconstruction networks.

Abstract: Existing Large Vision-Language Model benchmarks primarily evaluate visual understanding in general everyday scenarios, leaving historical consistency with a specified era and country largely underexplored. To address this gap, we propose AnachroBench, a controlled discriminative benchmark for evaluating historical consistency in visual scenes. It contains 1,600 image pairs from eight countries spanning the late 19th to the early 20th century, each pairing an original historical image with an anachronistic version containing a seamlessly inserted modern object. AnachroBench evaluates three aspects: Inserted Object Perception, which tests whether models detect the inserted modern object; Era-Country Consistency, which assesses whether the scene is consistent with the specified era and country; and Conditional Era-Country Consistency, which measures consistency judgments only when the inserted object is correctly perceived. A false consistency judgment occurs when a model perceives the modern object but still judges the scene as historically consistent. Overall, AnachroBench reveals a gap between inserted object perception and era-country consistency judgments, providing a targeted evaluation of this behavior.

</details>

Abstract: Streaming video generation must sustain quality beyond the training horizon while handling self-generated history and growing cache costs. To address these challenges, we present MovieRoll, a base-to-distilled framework that connects streaming fine-tuning, long-rollout distillation, and long-horizon inference. We first establish a robust multi-step base through Window-wise Teacher Forcing (WinTF), which jointly denoises chunks at progressive noise levels with bidirectional attention within each window and causal access to preceding history. To retain historical context efficiently, hierarchical KV-cache compression (HieraKV) exploits layer-wise attention patterns, retaining full history in shallow layers and sink--recent history in deeper layers. This reduces cache memory by up to 78% while improving generation quality. Building on this base, cumulative long tuning with distribution matching distillation (CuLT-DMD) holds parameters fixed throughout each long self-rollout and averages local gradients before updating. Each update therefore combines supervision across temporal positions while retaining only one local computation graph, enabling efficient long-rollout training of a few-step generator. To extend generation beyond the training horizon, KV recomputation bounds context for both models, while guidance annealing (AnnealCFG) reduces guidance toward the clean endpoint to stabilize MovieRoll-Base. Complementing these modeling advances, FBCA attention and PS-VAE decoding support efficient training and streaming output. On HeliosBench, MovieRoll-Base remains competitive at 30s after training on videos of up to 15s, and MovieRoll-Distilled achieves the highest total scores among the compared methods across single- and multi-event evaluations up to 60s, combining sustained naturalness with active motion.

</details>

Abstract: We present a transformation of vector embeddings representing image patches into scores for point reflection symmetry at the crossing points of patches. The transformation is heavily inspired by the Gradient Product Transform, but it uses the vector embeddings instead of the grayscale gradients. We therefore call it the "Scalar Product Transform" (SPT). Unlike deep learning models for symmetry detection, the method does not require a specific training step tailored for symmetry detection, but it works with arbitrary pre-trained patch embedding models. We have tested the method with DINOv3 and Franca. In our tests, the method outperformed the Gradient Product Transform and was even better than EquiSym, a convolutional neural network specifically designed and trained for symmetry detection. Although the runtime of the SPT is greater than that of EquiSym, the SPT has the advantage that it directly yields a bounding box for the symmetric region.

Abstract: Recent advances in video generation call for reliable detection grounded in explicit forensic evidence. However, current methods primarily depend on single-pass chain-of-thought reasoning that overlooks brief or localized artifacts, or on predefined multi-agent workflows that offer limited flexibility in selecting relevant inspections. Even with adaptive investigation, optimizing the investigation policy alone does not directly improve the forensic capabilities of fixed experts. We introduce WeVisForensics, an agentic framework in which a main investigator agent coordinates a self-evolving library of forensic inspector sub-agents spanning visual and audio cues. The investigator learns which artifact type to examine, over which window, and when to conclude, and these interactions provide shared feedback for both levels of agents. Our dual-timescale policy optimization strategy, DTPO, closes this loop through fast reinforcement learning of the investigator and slower refinement of the inspectors, guided by annotated artifact intervals, to report subtle artifacts they once dismissed. Furthermore, we curate ForenCue-30K, combining role-specific forensic annotations and interaction trajectories to supervise individual inspections and their coordination, with an accompanying reinforcement-learning pool. Experiments show that WeVisForensics averages 95.2% detection accuracy across in-domain, out-of-domain and agentic benchmarks, 9.0% above the strongest prior detector, while grounding its verdicts in localized evidence.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting enables object-level operations on Gaussian primitives, yet existing segmentation methods are largely trained and evaluated through rendered views. This mismatch can produce accurate rendered masks from incomplete 3D selections since alpha-blended feature supervision concentrates learning on dominant contributors while leaving occluded or low-contribution Gaussians weakly supervised. We propose VODA, a plug-and-play supervision objective that exploits local depth structure to group per-pixel contributors and directly supervises all Gaussians in selected depth clusters, bypassing contribution-weighted gradient delivery. To evaluate volumetric completeness, we introduce Removal-3D, a synthetic object-removal benchmark, and the Removal Depth Error (RDE), a metric comparing predicted and ground-truth depth changes. Across four rendering-based baselines, VODA consistently improves volumetric segmentation on Removal-3D while maintaining or improving performance on LERF and ScanNet. Further analysis shows that VODA recovers lower-contribution Gaussians, while simple gradient reweighting and spatial propagation fail to reproduce comparable gains.

</details>

Abstract: Understanding long videos requires locating evidence, resolving visual details, and integrating observations into an answer. Even when relevant evidence is present, performing these operations within long, dense visual sequences burdens both perception and reasoning. Video agents enable selective inspection, but returning frames to the planner still accumulates visual information in its reasoning context. Delegating perception reduces this burden, yet sparse sampling can miss decisive details, and per-frame descriptions can preserve context growth. We introduce VideoNexus, a training-free framework that separates detailed video perception from evidence-driven planning and reasoning. The planner controls where to inspect, what to resolve, and how densely to sample. Perception sub-agents process native audio-video in independent contexts and return compact, temporally grounded evidence for iterative search and verification. Using Gemini 3.8 Flash for both planning and perception, VideoNexus achieves 88.6% accuracy on full LVBench, exceeding same-backbone static ingestion and built-in agentic processing by 3.6 and 1.1 points, respectively, at 44% of the latter's measured API cost. A paired LVBench subset ablation shows lower planner input and higher accuracy. With Kimi K3 as the shared backbone, VideoNexus improves over the official Video-GAIA ReAct harness by 8.5 points in our evaluation. These results support compact evidence exchange for combining detailed observation with efficient planning and reasoning.

</details>

Abstract: High-resolution brain MRI is important for neuroradiological assessment, yet routine clinical protocols often rely on fast, anisotropic 2D acquisitions, particularly in resource-constrained settings. These acquisitions may differ in orientation, alignment and anatomical coverage, resulting in partially overlapping image stacks. Existing learning-based super-resolution methods typically rely on single, under-constrained inputs or fixed multi-stack configurations with idealised acquisition geometry. We introduce MS-HVED, a sequence-agnostic framework for flexible multi-stack brain MRI super-resolution. Available axial, coronal and sagittal stacks are independently encoded into multi-scale Gaussian posteriors and fused using a field-of-view-aware Product-of-Gaussians formulation that limits contributions from unsupported regions. Physics-informed domain randomisation simulates heterogeneous clinical acquisition conditions, including obliqueness, partial coverage and imaging artefacts. MS-HVED achieved competitive or superior reconstruction fidelity across synthetic benchmarks and improved downstream tissue segmentation relative to low-resolution inputs. To assess generalisation beyond the training cohorts, we performed external clinical validation on 30 routine brain MRI examinations from a Nigerian cohort, representing an under-represented population in medical imaging research. These examinations formed the largest component of a blinded reader study involving 44 real cases and seven expert neuroradiologists. In the Nigerian cohort, MS-HVED was preferred over the supervised baseline for overall image quality in 187 of 210 paired assessments. Across all clinical cohorts, MS-HVED achieved higher overall-quality scores with all available inputs (5.93 vs. 4.49, p<.001), with the preference persisting when axial or coronal inputs were omitted (p<.001). Abstract: Camera-based heart rate (HR) and respiratory rate (RR) estimates vary in reliability from window to window, and a common remedy is to fuse several extraction methods with weights from reference-free signal quality indicators. However, few studies test whether these indicators identify the most accurate method in each window or outperform simpler fusion. We evaluate 11 quality indicators and 8 inter-method similarity measures for fusing 7 HR and 10 RR extractors on four public datasets (75 test subjects). Filters, indicator directions, weighting parameters and a learned ridge baseline are selected on a separate five-subject set before testing. Fusion is compared with equal averaging, a constant typical-rate predictor, the best single method selected in advance and in hindsight, and a per-window oracle with a permutation-based null, using subject-level bootstrap confidence intervals. For HR, quality-weighted fusion reduced mean absolute error by 10.9% [5.2, 18.4] relative to averaging and by 15.6% [8.1, 23.1] relative to the method selected in advance, and was statistically indistinguishable from the best method selected in hindsight. Weighting methods by their agreement did not improve on averaging. For RR, the indicators could not identify the more accurate estimates: sharpened quality weighting increased error (-2.6% [-4.8, -0.8]), fused estimates were uncorrelated with the reference (r <= 0.22), and averaging was less accurate than the typical-rate predictor (-3.1% [-6.1, -0.4]). With a second respiratory reference, apparent RR gains reflected estimates moving toward its typical rate. Quality-guided fusion is worthwhile for video HR; for RR, 30-s windows improved estimates but not fusion.

Abstract: This article deals with the problem of detecting the position of power lines in airborne laser scanning point clouds. We address both the problem of segmenting power lines from background like ground and vegetation and of identifying individual lines. For segmentation we compare a rule-based approach with one based on transfer learning (Point-SAM). For individual line detection, we derive the mathematical formulas for RANSAC applied to 3D catenaries and compare it with a non-parametric method (TriplClust). All methods are evaluated on a newly created dataset "PowLiDFR", which we make publicly available. The samples have been taken from the French project LiDAR HD and cover a variety of terrain layouts and point cloud resolutions. Abstract: Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) aims to empower models to continuously acquire new knowledge while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. With the advent of vision-language models like CLIP, leveraging their cross-modal alignment capabilities to address CIL challenges has emerged as a dominant paradigm. Existing methods leverage CLIP's global representations (i.e., the visual [CLS] token and the textual [EOS] token) for cross-modal alignment. While achieving effective classification, they overlook the rich patch-level visual features and token-level textual semantics inherent in CLIP. For example, the [CLS] token aggregates local information to capture the general concept of "cat", while the patch-level details retain specific localized features essential for recognition, such as the tail, fur, and face. We propose PACE (PAtch-token Cross-modal Enhancement) to uncover the long-neglected local representations within CLIP. First, based on the global representations like [CLS] token and [EOS] token, we identify the top-k most crucial visual patches and textual tokens. Subsequently, to better utilize the extracted key local features, we design a cross-modal fusion module. This module leverages local textual semantics to refine visual representations, while augmenting textual semantics with local visual features. As the fusion module is shared across all tasks, directly fine-tuning it on new tasks would interfere with the knowledge learned from previous tasks, leading to catastrophic forgetting. To alleviate this, we employ a projection-based update strategy to effectively preserve the critical parameter space of previous tasks during the adaptation to new tasks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PACE significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple CIL benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Question answering over ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing scenes poses a tension for multimodal large language models: global overviews obscure small objects and local attributes, whereas detailed crops consume substantial context. Compressing inspected crops into short sequences of continuous visual latents offers a compact memory once crop tokens are discarded, yet diagnostic studies on natural images find that models often ignore latent content, leaving unclear whether compact latents can retain fine-grained targets lost in downsampled UHR remote sensing scenes. To isolate evidence retention from region selection, we encode high-resolution crops into latent memory tokens for frozen backbones and evaluate both their predictive utility and content dependence. Across two generations of Qwen-VL on LRS-GRO, a 64-token memory achieves 7.6- to 9.9-fold compression of retained crop tokens and improves accuracy by 2.5 to 4.8 percentage points over the downsampled global image alone, recovering most of the high-resolution crop gain with consistent behavior on XLRS-Bench. Substituting memory from another image or another region of the same scene reduces accuracy by 5.8 to 7.4 percentage points and yields lower accuracy than removing memory altogether, demonstrating that predictions depend on the encoded regional evidence. Restoring the source crop within the same model reduces the accuracy loss caused by zeroing or removing memory. These findings establish that compact visual latents retain useful local evidence in UHR remote sensing and identify evidence availability as a key factor governing their contribution.

</details>

Abstract: Weight Factorization (WF) methods aim to reduce a model's latency, memory, or both. These typically decompose a pretrained model into two or more factors that approximate the original model weights with fewer parameters and floating point operations (FLOPs). However, translating fewer FLOPs into reduced latency is challenging for WF methods in practice due to overhead introduced by replacing a single large weight matrix with multiple smaller ones. To address this, we propose Balanced Twin Weight Factorization (BTWF), which uses the unique structure of factorized models to simultaneously lower latency and increase learning capacity. Specifically, BTWF operates in the shared basis WF setting, where two or more layers are decomposed into a shared basis matrix and a layer-specific mixing matrix. If these shared layers have the same input (i.e., are twins), then we need only perform this shared forward pass once, reducing inference time. However, twin layers are often of varying sizes, and methods from prior work tend to favor the larger layer. In contrast, our approach boosts performance by combining Factor Component Balancing (FCB), which ensures every shared member is well represented in the selected factors, with Block Contribution Balancing (BCB), which considers downstream model effects. Further, we also show that an overparameterized BTWF, where some layer's factors result in higher FLOPs than the original during training, can boost task performance without additional resources at test time. Experiments on six models over nine benchmarks show BTWF obtains a 2-6% gain over the state-of-the-art without expensive finetuning (1-3.5% gain with it) at comparable computational costs.

</details>

Abstract: Mechanical blueberry harvesting requires a balance between removing ripe fruit and avoiding premature removal of unripe berries that could remain on the bush for a later harvest. However, operators generally lack real-time information about the maturity composition of the fruit being collected. This study develops an event-triggered machine-vision system that measures the maturity of harvested blueberries directly on the harvester. When a filled crate is moved by the machine, an RGB image is automatically captured and analysed using a three-class detector that identifies ripe, semi-ripe, and unripe berries. The system operates locally on the existing CPU-only industrial computer and therefore requires neither continuous image processing nor dedicated GPU hardware. During field testing, 79 crate images containing 48,634 visible blueberries in manually verified reference annotations were evaluated. The vision system estimated an overall ripe-fruit fraction of 90.22%, compared with 90.97% determined manually. Per-image ripe-fruit estimates showed a mean absolute error of 0.935 percentage points and a Pearson correlation of r=0.967. Maturity estimates remained similar between the two sides of the harvester for which the light conditions varied despite poorer absolute counting performance under direct sunlight. All field events were processed within the available 14 s per-side crate interval. The proposed approach provides a practical means of quantifying one component of harvest selectivity while the machine is operating. Abstract: Interactive video world models enable exploration through text and camera controls, yet direct object interaction requires finer control. Hands provide a natural interface, but translating continuous hand movements into coherent visual responses remains challenging because of complex hand-object dynamics and demanding real-time computation. To address these challenges, we introduce LynnReal-Hand, a hand-controlled video world model that unifies precise interaction modeling with efficient streaming generation. Building on this foundation, we learn a causal flow map for few-step generation and introduce region-aware score distillation that combines complementary video and image priors to refine interaction dynamics and visual appearance. To further reduce inference latency, we propose Ultra-Fast-VAE, a compact video decoder that preserves high reconstruction fidelity with substantially lower computational cost. Extensive evaluations on HandBench demonstrate superior hand-following accuracy and visual quality compared with existing approaches. Our full-sequence model achieves an FVD of 181.67 and a MANO Dice score of 93.02, while the three-step causal model maintains a MANO Dice score of 92.49. Abstract: Persistent spatial understanding is essential for embodied agents to operate reliably in environments they repeatedly encounter. It requires recognizing revisited places despite viewpoint changes, distinguishing them from similar-looking rooms, and updating memories as environments change. Yet existing evaluations often leave physical place identity across separated visits implicit. We introduce ReSpaceBench, a benchmark comprising 3,244 questions across 590 synthetic and real-home scenes, organized into 9 tasks spanning room association, persistent state tracking, and downstream reasoning. Controlled walkthroughs define visits by physical room entry and deliberately include same-type room distractors and cross-session object changes. Our evaluation of 32 models and 3 agentic video-understanding frameworks reveals substantial limitations: the strongest evaluated zero-shot model achieves 50.5% overall accuracy and 52.2% on a 450-question subset, compared with 90.1% for human annotators. The evaluated agentic frameworks offer no overall improvement over direct inference with the same base model. Analysis of agent execution traces reveals evidence omissions and incorrect cross-visit room associations as major failure modes, suggesting that iterative evidence seeking alone does not ensure consistent spatial memory. Further analyses expose a gap between recognizing familiar rooms and identifying the correct visited room, while annotation-guided sampling highlights the importance of preserving intervening visits and transitions. We also provide RS-30K, a separate training dataset from synthetic sources with no template, scene, route, or video overlap with the benchmark. Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) often fail to match a point or an object across two images, even when the answer can be decoded from their own hidden states. We trace this failure from the visual tokens to the answer letter, in Qwen3-VL (2B-32B), InternVL3.5-8B, and Gemma-3-12B, on synthetic shape matching, same-instance point correspondence (HPatches), and semantic correspondence (SPair-71k), and find two distinct failure points, a small set of attention heads that carries the comparison, and a repair that touches 0.02% of the parameters. Two failure points: exact copies are compared but not read out. An answer-position probe exceeds the calibrated output by 15 to 25 points in every family, for nameable and unnameable shapes alike, and steering along the probe's direction moves no more answers than random directions. Semantic correspondences are present in the visual tokens but never reach the answer. One pathway: redistributing the cross-image attention of 16-32 heads, with its mass preserved, removes 17-22 points of synthetic and 15-32 points of HPatches accuracy, against at most 7 for random or attention-matched heads. A lever that training finds on its own: training only these heads' query and key projections raises SPair-71k accuracy by 28.3 points (Qwen3-VL-8B) and 25.1 points (InternVL3.5-8B), beats both layer-matched and attention-matched heads, and transfers to HPatches and BLINK. An unconstrained all-layer adapter routes 21-51% of its gain through the attention of the same heads, against at most 7% for random heads, and does so by changing what they read (83-86% of the effect) rather than their weights. Every decision rule was frozen before its run, and every reported number is recomputed by independent verification scripts.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding is becoming increasingly important for real-world interactive systems. Such systems must be proactive for natural and useful human interaction: they should respond as soon as sufficient evidence becomes available while avoiding premature, redundant, or incorrect responses. We present BLIP-Live, a framework unifying data, metrics, harness, and training around a shared formulation of proactivity as effective causal response decisions balancing the two competing objectives of responding vs silence. First, we construct BLIP-Live-780K dataset containing human annotations of proactive response content and timing, which defines this desired behavior. We then propose PAUC-F, a metric to effectively measure our desired proactive behavior. PAUC-F combines existing response timeliness metrics with firing precision to reward early useful responses without rewarding excessive firing. With our desired behavior defined and measurable, we finally investigate model-agnostic inference harnesses that draw out proactive streaming capabilities of offline vision language models (VLMs), together with training pipelines that learn to further improve their proactive responsiveness. Across both settings, we find that combinations of sliding visual windows, online interaction memory, and structured response outputs consistently yield strongest proactive performance. Our experiments result in two models, with our BLIP-Live-Qwen model achieving state-of-the-art performance across four proactive streaming benchmarks, with gains up to 44% over baselines and outperforming multiple closed API models. Abstract: Long video generation should keep the subject and background consistent while supporting rich, natural motion. However, autoregressive models are often distilled with distribution matching distillation (DMD) over short windows, which does not train them to keep this consistency over long ranges. As a result, the subject and background can drift over time. Methods that manage the key-value (KV) cache retain or retrieve history to reduce drift. Yet this history may itself have drifted. We propose Reference Forcing, which uses the initial frames as an appearance reference. We first build a teacher that uses attention masks to force each region of a new frame to attend to the same region in the initial frames. The teacher finds these regions with off-the-shelf segmentation models. We then distill this behavior into a separate attention path of the student, which needs no external models at inference. We further introduce value correction, which corrects drifted values in the KV cache toward the matching values of drift-free initial frames. Experiments show that Reference Forcing achieves state-of-the-art subject and background consistency among long-video methods on different base models, and its advantage grows with video length. Ablations show both components are needed to gain consistency without losing motion. More visualizations and code are available at https://zhaosheng-thu.github.io/reference-force/

</details>

Abstract: Structure-level wildfire vulnerability assessment is critical for reducing losses in wildland-urban interface communities, yet existing methods often fail to combine parcel-scale ignition pathways with community-scale geographic coverage. This study introduces Wildfire-SSR (Satellite and Street-view for wildfire Risk), a geolocation-aware multimodal framework that integrates macro-scale satellite context with micro-scale street-view evidence. A Vision-Language Model extracts wildfire-relevant building and site attributes from pre-event street-view panoramas and converts them into structured semantic text. These features are spatially aligned with satellite imagery through a decoupled cross-attention fusion decoder, while a frozen satellite-pretrained DINOv3 encoder captures overhead visual context. Alternative geolocation embedding strategies are evaluated to anchor text-derived features within each satellite patch. The framework is tested on approximately 37,000 structures affected by the 2025 Eaton and Palisades fires in Los Angeles, using wildfire risk labels derived from relative post-event damage patterns to better represent inherent structural vulnerability. Rotary Positional Embedding provides the strongest spatial grounding, achieving a building localization F1 score of 0.6735 and a harmonic mean F1 score of 0.2476 for five-class pixel-level risk classification, outperforming unimodal baselines and an unanchored multimodal configuration. The model also shows a conservative bias in higher-risk classes, reducing severe-risk underestimation, and completes end-to-end assessment of the study area in 37 minutes on a single GPU. Wildfire-SSR reframes cross-view fusion as localized semantic alignment and provides a scalable tool for mitigation prioritization, resilience planning, and insurance-oriented wildfire risk assessment.

</details>

Abstract: Test-time augmentation (TTA) aggregates model predictions across multiple transformed views of an input during inference. Because histology images lack a canonical orientation, $D_4$ TTA---pooling across the 8 rotations and reflections of a square---is a standard default in computational pathology. However, the rise of self-supervised histology foundation models (FMs) with inherent rotational invariance calls its continued utility into question. We benchmark 9 histology FMs and 6 general-purpose models across four patch-level and two whole-slide image (WSI) datasets. We find that $D_4$ TTA remains a reliable post hoc improvement, but benefits diminish from $+1.98$ percentage points (pp) for general-purpose backbones to $+0.50$ pp for histology FMs on TCGA-UT, and shrink further for the largest models. To eliminate computational overhead, our experiments demonstrate that entropy routing---applying TTA selectively to uncertain predictions---recovers most of the gain while augmenting only ${\sim}13\%$ of samples. Finally, we show that patch-level TTA improvements do not transfer to slide-level multiple instance learning (MIL). Code: https://github.com/lars-chen/histology-tta

Abstract: When a vision-language model describes an image, it names one object at a time and it rarely repeats itself, yet how the model plans this order is far from obvious. We characterize the mechanism a VLM uses to decide where to look before it looks. The mechanism has four main parts. First, binding heads match the relevant text tokens against the image and resolve them into pointers to the matched image region. Second, the same binding heads bind each written answer to the region it came from, and the model turns it into a progress signal: a cursor, the position in the image up to which the list has been written. Third, a select step chooses the nearest unwritten region after the cursor, or stops. Finally, gaze heads execute the chosen pointer and return a concept of what is drawn there, which language heads and the late MLPs turn into a word in the language of the question. We validate each part of this mechanism through causal patching in Qwen3-VL models on a synthetic visual retrieval task, and test our mechanisms against alternative hypotheses. We further show that the mechanism holds across input formats, natural photographs, and languages, and that its binding, relay, and gaze heads appear at every model size from 2B to 32B. This work allows us to investigate how a VLM turns a question and an image into a sequence of answers, layer by layer and token by token. More broadly, it opens a way to study how models plan over complex multi-step tasks with long rollouts. Our project is available at https://plan.baulab.info/

</details>

Abstract: Face forgery detection methods often perform well on seen manipulations but generalize poorly to unseen ones. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can recognize facial parts and assess their visual plausibility. Existing MLLM-based methods typically express these judgments through textual explanations, but seldom treat individual part decisions as explicit learning targets. We present FaceJev, an MLLM-based method for face forgery detection through direct visual decisions. Following the Jev formulation, FaceJev answers eight independent questions about facial parts using a shared image encoding and reads anomaly probabilities from the native answer logits. A face is classified as forged if any part is predicted to be abnormal, directly linking the detection result to the part decisions. We construct PartForensics, a dataset of approximately 160k images with binary anomaly targets for the eight facial parts, to supervise these decisions. We further propose FS-GRPO, which optimizes each part decision through an exact reward comparison with its alternative, using both detection and part rewards. FaceJev outperforms the strongest baseline by 7.25 percentage points on unseen manipulations. Extensive ablation studies validate our design choices and the effectiveness of the main components.

Abstract: Visual agents answer questions about ultra-high-resolution photos by zooming into the relevant regions. However, existing agents often answer from common sense instead of reading the evidence. Reinforcement learning (RL) on the final answer cannot correct this, because a guess earns the same reward as reading. We propose Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization (CrossRPO), which trains agents on contrast sets. A contrast set asks one question about several versions of an image that differ only in a small region with the evidence and have different answers, so only reading the evidence solves the whole set. CrossRPO rewards solving the whole set, and we prove that it does not reinforce a set answered from common sense. We build contrast sets for training (ViRA-Train) and evaluation (ViRA-Bench) by editing one small object in images of 21 to 120 megapixels, and we photograph further sets with real changes (ViRA-Bench-Real) to exclude editing traces. We train two Visual Reasoning Agents, ViRA-9B and ViRA-27B, with CrossRPO on only about 3,500 items of ViRA-Train each. CrossRPO achieves 63.3% set accuracy on ViRA-Bench at the maximum resolution, against 59.3% for GRPO. ViRA-9B is the most accurate open model of its size on all benchmarks, and ViRA-27B outperforms Claude Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6-Luna, both at their highest thinking level, on most benchmarks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies are vulnerable to adversarial visual perturbations, while defenses often require task-specific adversarial fine-tuning. We investigate whether robustness learned on source manipulation tasks can protect independently specialized target policies without target-side adversarial training. We first show that even a single source defense update improves robustness on held-out tasks, establishing cross-task defense reuse independently of multi-source aggregation. To combine source updates, we introduce Source-Norm Transferable Robustness Vector (SN-TRV), which retains the direction of their mean while calibrating its magnitude using source-update norms. The resulting update is applied to the target visual encoder without target adversarial data, target-side gradient optimization, or additional inference modules. Evaluations across three VLA backbones, fixed and adaptive attacks, and different source defense recipes show improved robustness over naive averaging and identify limits in attack coverage. Physical PiPER trials increase average success under printed adversarial patches from 20.3% to 45.8%.

Abstract: This report presents the design and implementation of Auriga Robotics' 1:10 scale autonomous vehicle, developed at Shahid Beheshti University's Robotics and Intelligent Automation Laboratory. The platform integrates a custom mechanical chassis, a distributed embedded system based on ESP32 microcontrollers, and a Jetson Orin NX module for high-level perception and control. The embedded layer manages sensor interfacing, real-time data acquisition, and actuator control, while the Jetson handles environment perception through deep-learning--based lane, sign, and traffic light detection, as well as decision-making and trajectory control. System communication is achieved via structured UART links, ensuring synchronized operation between perception and control subsystems. The vehicle's software stack incorporates ROS, modular firmware design, and multiple control algorithms---Stanley, PID, and Pure Pursuit---to achieve stable autonomous navigation in structured driving scenarios. The overall system demonstrates robust performance and reliability in urban-like environments, serving as a modular research platform for future autonomous driving studies.

Abstract: In multi-robot navigation, rigid global schedules are sensitive to execution deviations, while local methods lack long-horizon coordination. We propose a passage-focused framework combining soft global coordination with flexible local execution. A passage-induced graph of convex sets supports priority-ordered planning with two levels of soft coordination. Passage-aware route selection accounts for passage usage and traversal directions, while route-restricted continuous space-time planning penalizes temporal conflicts. The resulting references guide a model predictive path integral controller that avoids collisions online. Adaptive temporal and geometric tracking accommodates execution deviations, while selective replanning maintains coordination without rigid trajectory reservations. Across 1,200 instances in 4 different environments with 5-30 robots, the framework achieves a near-perfect success rate and outperforms evaluated baselines in makespan and flowtime. Hardware experiments with 8 robots demonstrate successful completion of all trials without collisions. These results highlight the effectiveness of passage-focused coordination in balancing long-horizon cooperation with flexible execution.

</details>

Abstract: Robots in homes, warehouses and farms must pick up objects they have never seen, usually without a 3D model or a category label. A grasping policy that works directly on the point cloud from a depth sensor needs only the geometry the robot observes, so it applies to any new object. Offline grasp generators such as Contact-GraspNet work this way, but they rank grasps by learned plausibility rather than by whether the grasp actually lifts the object. Reinforcement learning (RL) can optimize for that directly and make a point-cloud grasping policy much better on average, yet it leaves specific grasps persistently unsolved. We present a human-in-the-loop failure loop that targets those grasps: situations an RL policy keeps failing are mined automatically, sent to a virtual-reality application where a person picks the object up with their own hands, and returned to training as two grasp points. The person contributes only where to grasp; the robot chooses its own approach. In simulation, RL raises success on unseen objects from 55% with Contact-GraspNet to 83%. Adding 58 human demonstrations nearly doubles success on the hard cases RL kept failing, from 24% to 45%, and recovers performance that RL alone loses on similar cases that were never demonstrated, without reducing success on unseen objects.

Abstract: Single-point linearization provides a computationally efficient basis for optimization-based trajectory planning of nonlinear robot manipulators. However, planning performance depends on the selected linearization point. This paper presents an offline performance-driven approach that treats the location of a single linearization as an optimization variable. Candidate operating points are sampled from a common cubic seed trajectory, and each corresponding affine model is used to solve an identical constrained finite-horizon quadratic program (QP). The resulting control sequences are executed on the original nonlinear dynamics, and the operating point minimizing the nonlinear trajectory objective is selected through grid search and bounded refinement. Evaluation on four point-to-point motions of a three-degree-of-freedom UR5 manipulator yields optimal normalized operating-point locations between 0.325 and 0.935, demonstrating trajectory-dependent selection. Compared with the best-performing conventional initial, midpoint, or terminal linearization, the proposed approach reduces the nonlinear trajectory objective by 17-18%, 21-22%, 37-38%, and 7-8% across the four tasks, respectively. Additional nonlinear execution with identical PID feedback correction confirms that the performance advantage persists for a representative task. These results demonstrate that systematic operating-point selection can substantially improve single-linearization trajectory planning while preserving a fixed affine planning model.

Abstract: This paper argues that the robotics and AI community should adopt disclosure standards for demonstration videos. As physical and financial constraints limit direct public access to embodied AI, public dissemination of progress in robotics is mainly through curated demonstration videos rather than direct experience with the technology. In a large-scale (N=9,341), pre-registered experiment across six countries, we find that the general public struggles to discern both the visual authenticity (e.g., distinguishing real footage from CGI) and behavioral authenticity (e.g., distinguishing autonomy from teleoperation) of robot demonstration videos. The omission of these details distorts public perception, leading people to overestimate the ability of robots to perform tasks and navigate real world environments. Using evidence from behavioral science, we design a disclosure intervention and demonstrate that the inclusion of clear disclosures in robot demonstration videos significantly improves discernment of visual and behavioral authenticity, as well as understanding robots' capabilities. We propose the adoption of disclosure standards for robot demonstration videos to accurately calibrate public expectations and promote transparency in robotics research.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion policies, which generate short action sequences by denoising random noise, are a leading way to learn robot skills from demonstrations. We prove that a certified step is safe whatever the critic does; that, whenever a certified sample exists, the executed chunk stays within log N nats of the policy conditioned on feasibility, while a safety-filter projection can be singular to it; and that an exact closed-loop identity splits the gain of ranking into availability, spread and the critic's target. Certifying the next chunk is not enough: a generative controller can have safe actions available now and none later. We characterise the safe control authority of a frozen policy under finite sampling, and CAST-V preserves it through a policy-conditioned viability, estimated with a model under a finite-sample guarantee or learned from the controller's own logs (CAST-VL). Abstract: Integrating MLLM-based agents with robotic foundation models has attracted increasing attention as a means of enhancing robotic capabilities. However, the improvement of such integrated systems remains largely decoupled across their agent and model components, with advances in one component not necessarily benefiting the other, substantially limiting the capabilities of the combined system. As both MLLMs and robotic foundation models become increasingly capable, fully realizing their combined potential calls for co-evolution, an iterative process in which improvements in agents and robotic foundation models reinforce one another. Through a code-as-policy formulation, the agent develops and refines executable failure-detection and recovery programs, generating targeted corrective experience. Post-training consolidates the resulting corrective behaviors into the robotic model's end-to-end policy, improving autonomous task execution. Changes in the updated policy's failure distribution, in turn, guide subsequent revisions to the agent's programs. Experiments on BEHAVIOR-1K, RoboCasa, and RoboTwin demonstrate sustained improvements in autonomous VLA task performance across successive co-evolution rounds.

Before and during policy training, multiple-choice training keeps the VLM's visual abilities in one pass, so the policy still answers questions about driving scenes. Training questions are built against shortcuts. Each must be answered right in two option orders. Against drift, a KL term keeps each stage near its starting model. One pass offers VLAs a more plausible path to System 1. SuperGlance answers visual multiple-choice questions as well as its base VLM does by decoding. Abstract: Recovering the forces behind a motion is ill-posed: different controllers can reproduce the same movement while generating different contact forces. Physics-based motion imitation resolves this ambiguity through simulation, but relies on fixed-gain proportional-derivative (PD) control. We show that fixed gains impose a trade-off between motion and force tracking, as contact and swing place conflicting demands on joint stiffness and damping. We introduce ImpedanceMimic, a reinforcement-learning policy that modulates per-joint stiffness, damping, and position setpoints from motion and force measurements, but requires only motion at inference. Across 129 participants and six activities, ImpedanceMimic outperforms matched fixed-gain PD under every integration scheme, direct-torque, muscle-actuated and existing imitation controllers in kinematic and force fidelity, approaching a dedicated motion-to-force regressor. By expressing gains as multiples of inertia-scaled priors, a single policy controls subject-specific biomechanical models without explicit anthropometric input and transfers zero-shot to unseen humans and a humanoid robot. The learned impedance forms a low-dimensional representation of motor state, shared across bodies, that generalizes to withheld activities: stiffness scales with load, and damping anticipates unloading.
