Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) can teach robot teams to navigate shared spaces, but trial and error causes collisions that real robots cannot afford. Control barrier function (CBF) safety filters prevent these collisions by correcting unsafe actions, yet in a team the filter becomes a centralized optimization whose number of constraints grows quadratically with the team size. We propose an approach that builds the filter's safety into the RL policy and examine how such a system behaves during filter-free deployment. However, the filter that keeps training safe also hides what the policy must learn: it masks the cost of unsafe actions, which actor-critic methods then overvalue, and it keeps near-collision states out of the training data. Our approach therefore learns only from executed safe actions with implicit Q-learning and distills the filter into the policy at synthetic near-collision states. We prove that filter distillation loss bounds the policy's constraint violation and hence how far agents can cross their safety margin, so errors never accumulate. When distillation states cover deployed states, the loss also bounds the collision probability at every step. Without a filter and acting only on local observations, our policies have no collisions in 1,800 test episodes with multiple agents. A policy trained with three agents transfers zero-shot to teams of twenty at constant per-agent computation and stays collision-free with up to eight agents. We test our policies on Crazyflie quadrotors with up to five flying at the same time.

Abstract: Reasoning about what an action will cause requires internalizing how a system behaves. As conditions seldom stay fixed, this must often happen within a few interactions. Yet systems that look identical in a snapshot can respond differently to the same action, depending on physical conditions the snapshot hides. Context-based models capture this separation by summarizing recent transitions into a latent context, but do not specify how that context should change the equations of motion. Physics-informed neural networks build those equations into the model, but describe only a single system and cannot recognize when it has changed. We present Physics-Informed Causal Reasoning (PICR), which bridges the two. Only a context, re-inferred from recent interactions, is updated; it conditions a fixed physics-informed network that determines the robot's inertia and potential energy, so the robot adapts to new physical conditions from its own experience without retraining or a hand-specified robot model. On a Franka Panda arm in NVIDIA Isaac Sim, PICR predicts motion under changing and unseen payloads, reducing error on the latter by 1.6-3.8x over baselines, and extends to counterfactual queries under alternative torques. Our analysis shows that the context, inferred solely from interaction, encodes payload physical properties, tracks their changes, and governs the arm's response. The gravity and inertia terms induced by the context further demonstrate its physical validity, each agreeing with the simulator at the level of individual Lagrangian components.

</details>

Abstract: When a vision-and-language navigation policy generates an action sequence autoregressively, the robot waits for a chain of dependent predictions. A small discrete action vocabulary makes it possible to score all short-horizon plans in parallel. We introduce VLN-Flash, a training-free decoder that evaluates the complete action-prefix tree in a single forward pass. Branch-specific attention and matched token positions preserve the original policy's conditional action distribution for each prefix in exact arithmetic. We use the score margin between the stepwise-greedy prefix and its strongest alternative at each depth to decide how many actions to execute. The robot then executes the plan with the highest joint score at the chosen depth before observing again. On matched inputs, VLN-Flash achieves a nearly $3\times$ speedup in action decoding over native autoregression on Uni-NaVid and StreamVLN. Compared with the native autoregressive decoders, it improves success rate and success weighted by path length (SPL) on R2R-CE and RxR-CE, with success gains up to 2.7 percentage points. On a Unitree GO2, VLN-Flash improves navigation success and cuts the mean wait between actions by about 160 ms after subtracting network transmission time.

Abstract: In indoor 3D localization inside multistory buildings, an elevator or an escalator accelerates for a few seconds and then cruises at constant velocity for tens of seconds. Gait excitation disappears, the specific force degenerates into a constant gravity reaction, and instantaneous velocity becomes unobservable within a 1-2 s window. The output of short-window learned inertial odometry consequently degrades toward the conditional mean of the training set, and vertical displacement is systematically underestimated. We propose Local-Token Causal Attention (LTCA), a long-horizon architecture that compresses each 1 s inertial window into a 1 Hz motion token and sustains, over a bounded 60 s causal cache, the motion state established at the onset event. We further introduce a pluggable barometer branch that, when online, supplies a direct elevation constraint to the vertical channel through local differential height observations, and we train it with paired online/fallback conditional training so that the barometer also regularizes the representation shared by the two modes during training. On MSIO, our benchmark of nine buildings, the RoNIN and TLIO instances reach 3D trajectory errors (ATE-3D) of 2.83/4.18 m and 2.44/5.12 m on the Seen and Unseen splits, respectively, with the barometer online. With the barometer disabled at inference, the same weights fall back to pure inertial inference and reach 3.24/4.28 m and 2.64/5.23 m, still below every native short-window baseline. We further validate the framework on the public RoNIN and TLIO benchmarks.

</details>

On the development set (84% of questions about convergence, mostly one location) the event stream answers 74.3% of questions at 0.13 kbps. A reader that receives nothing answers 57.4%, and the video reader 59.6% at 100 kbps and 64.7% on the source frames. When the ground instead runs our perception on the video, video matches the stream at 100 kbps, about 800 times the stream's rate, and falls behind at 64 kbps and below. A track stream matches it at 96 times the bytes and loses 0.03 at 17 times. On held-out data the result is weaker: with events taken from annotated tracks, an oracle, the stream beats H.265 at 100 kbps (+0.13), but with onboard perception it does not (+0.01 [-0.04, +0.06]) and is no better than receiving nothing. Sent alone through emulated links the event records answer 0.739 at 1 Mbps and still 0.724 at 0.3 kbps; an every-frame track stream holds down to 1 kbps and video with ground-side perception down to about 100 kbps. On a Jetson Orin Nano the unchanged pipeline runs at 4.5 fps and misses most events.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel approach for localizing the source of a three-dimensional scalar field using a team of heterogeneous cooperative mobile robots. Each robot measures the field intensity at its location and shares the information within the team. Measurement noise is explicitly considered, and heterogeneity arises from robot-dependent sensing accuracies, leading to heteroskedastic data. The proposed framework combines a weighted centroidal Voronoi tessellation-based formation strategy, defined on a spherical surface and parameterized by sensor noise variances, with a Weighted Least Squares estimator to compute the field gradient at the formation center. By explicitly accounting for sensor heterogeneity in both formation design and estimation, the method achieves robust gradient estimation under noisy measurements. In particular, we show analytically that this construction is nearly optimal. The covariance of the gradient estimate is derived in closed form, providing a lower bound on the attainable estimation variance for any formation on the sphere, and the proposed tessellation is shown to asymptotically satisfy the conditions under which this bound is attained. The estimated gradient is then exploited within a gradient-ascent strategy to guide the team toward the source location. Simulation results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach compared to alternative strategies and highlight its intrinsic ability to reconfigure online in response to robot failures, thereby enhancing its adaptability.

</details>

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) augment vision-language-action policies (VLAs) with future prediction so that the policy can anticipate how its actions will affect the physical world. Existing WAMs supervise complete future observations. However, observations typically change little over an action chunk, so the future loss is dominated by copying static background content rather than learning task-relevant robot and object motion, limiting policy learning. Moreover, existing WAMs rely on large video generation models or separate world models, incurring heavy training and inference compute that hinders closed-loop deployment. To improve policy performance, we propose visual residual learning, supervising only the feature change from the current frame to the future frame in a frozen DINOv3 space. Compared with complete future features, this residual learning target yields a higher feature rank with richer and more diverse representations of scene changes, and spatially concentrates on the robot arm and manipulated objects rather than the background. To further improve computational efficiency, we present ResWAM, a residual world-action model with 2.3B parameters in total, including a lightweight 0.5B dual-branch DiT that jointly trains an action branch and a residual branch via conditional flow matching. Without robot pretraining (i.e., pretraining on external robot action trajectories beyond the benchmark demonstrations), ResWAM achieves top average success rates on LIBERO-Plus (81.1%), RoboTwin 2.0 (93.8%), and RoboDojo (9.40% success, 13.41 score) at the lowest training (2.7-4.0 TFLOPs) and inference compute (1.0-1.4 TFLOPs), and leads real-robot Open, Put, and Stack (80.0% average, +10.0% over $π_{0.5}$) at 92.7 ms latency.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) enables robots to acquire complex skills, but standard RL formulations do not guarantee that learned policies satisfy safety constraints, which limits their use in safety-critical settings. Existing safe RL methods with provable guarantees typically support only restricted classes of constraints and require unbiased estimators of the objective and constraint functions, which prevents their direct integration with state-of-the-art algorithms such as proximal policy optimization (PPO). In this paper, we extend the recently introduced robust safe gradient flow-based reinforcement learning (RSGF-RL) framework to overcome these limitations. Our formulation accommodates a general class of trajectory-level safety constraints, including chance constraints such as collision avoidance with a prescribed probability, and our analysis allows for biased value and gradient estimators. We show that, with high probability, the constraint violation throughout training is bounded by the estimator bias plus a term that vanishes as the number of rollouts grows, and decays to this level if the initial policy is unsafe. We also establish the convergence properties of the iterates to approximate KKT points of the original safe RL problem. Finally, we show that RSGF-RL implemented with PPO-style estimates enables zero-shot sim-to-real deployment of whole-body control policies on a Unitree G1 humanoid and collision-free waypoint navigation on a Crazyflie drone.

Abstract: Physics-based control is central to robot learning, but differentiating through implicit physics steps typically requires Hessian or Karush--Kuhn--Tucker (KKT) linear solves; we ask whether gradient steps suffice. Each simulation step minimizes an energy under many contact and collision constraints. Without constraints, we view control over $K$ steps as $K$-level optimization with strongly convex lower levels, recast as one min--max problem. Our Penalized Single-loop $K$-level method (PSK) uses only gradients and a projection, updates all levels in parallel, and reaches an $ε$-stationary control in $O(ε^{-3})$ iterations, with a $K$-independent exponent but a $K$-dependent constant. Contact-rich constraints, however, typically violate a constraint qualification (CQ). We replace each constraint by a smooth wall that acts on a band of constant width. Under mild assumptions that need no CQ, every exact solution of the banded lower levels is then feasible and banded Fritz--John, and PSK keeps the $O(ε^{-3})$ complexity. Without a CQ that is uniform in the band width, however, a shrinking band loses the $K$-independent exponent: with width $ε$ and fixed $K\geq2$, deterministic zero-respecting methods need $Ω(ε^{-(K+3)/4})$ calls on a hard instance whose control dimension grows with $1/ε$. We prove our assumptions for smoothed kinematic and dynamic multi-robot navigation among segment obstacles. In navigation experiments, PSK, tuned per case, reaches every goal with sixteen levels and, at equal time, reaches a lower loss than tuned unrolling in 17 of 18 cases, while implicit differentiation is lower in 10.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion-based driving planners can generate multiple trajectories for the same scene, but these candidates vary in quality. Can their internal states reveal which candidate will be better before denoising is complete? We systematically study this question in three pretrained planners: Alpamayo, ReCogDrive-IL, and HDP, using linear probes to predict final trajectory quality from intermediate states and evaluating candidate ranking and selection on held-out scenes. Across all three planners, we observe that states from the first denoiser evaluation already support informative selection, while further denoising does not consistently improve selection quality. Further analyses show that readout depends on the representation and reader, and that selection can coexist with limited overall ranking accuracy. These findings motivate an early-selection method that selects after the first denoiser evaluation and completes only the chosen candidate. This reduces computation relative to fully denoising every candidate and improves open-loop trajectory quality over random selection across all three planners. In particular, it reduces Alpamayo's average displacement error by 17.8% and 13.4% in one- and four-camera configurations, respectively. Abstract: Robotic manipulation missions must satisfy timed and conditional requirements that can be expressed in signal temporal logic (STL). While a practical means of monitoring these requirements is from camera images, this is challenging because STL robustness depends on physical margins, such as distances and clearances, that are not directly observed. Rather than learning a separate visual monitor for each formula, we learn a shared latent representation from multi-view images and decode physically meaningful margins for individual predicates. Co-training the encoder with predicate decoders increases the certification rate from 58.7% to 96.3% compared with a frozen encoder, despite nearly unchanged predicate sign accuracy. STL semantics then compose these margins into robustness estimates for any bounded formula over the trained predicates, without retraining. We further calibrate the decoding errors of selected STL clauses using conformal prediction, yielding two-sided intervals that certify both satisfaction and violation and propagate through interval STL to any formula composed from the clauses. In simulated kitchen missions (LIBERO), our monitor correctly predicts satisfaction or violation in 98.9% of 4662 evaluations across 18 unseen formulas. For missions lasting up to 17 min, calibrated bounds establish satisfaction or violation for 96.9% of missions without incorrect conclusions. A single calibration also provides simultaneous 91.0% coverage across all 255 conjunctions of eight clauses, allowing new formulas without recalibration.

Abstract: Accurate and timely air pollution monitoring and tracking are critical for protecting public health and supporting effective environmental management. This paper presents an adaptive Density-Driven Control (D$^2$C) framework for coordinating multiple unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) to track and map dynamically evolving pollutant distributions. The proposed framework extends the D$^2$C strategy to guide UAV trajectories based on predictive pollutant propagation and dynamically updated reference densities. A sensor-driven surrogate plume update mechanism is employed, in which reference samples are propagated using locally sensed concentration gradients and wind estimates through gradient attraction, wind advection, inter-sample repulsion, and stochastic diffusion, enabling UAVs to maintain an accurate spatio-temporal representation of the pollution field. The framework utilizes a coordinated structure where individual UAVs share their local measurements with a central coordinator to maintain a unified set of reference samples, facilitating systematic target allocation and trajectory planning. Simulation results under both mild and strong wind conditions demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in terms of Sinkhorn distance. Specifically, in scenarios where the plume drifts beyond a fixed monitoring domain under high-velocity, time-varying wind fields, the tested baseline methods fail to maintain tracking, whereas the proposed D$^2$C framework successfully adapts to the evolving distribution. These findings highlight D$^2$C as an efficient and resilient solution for atmospheric monitoring in dynamic environments.

Abstract: Flow-matching robot policies generate each action chunk through repeated network evaluations, one per denoising step. Cross-cycle caches such as C$^3$ache reuse computation from earlier replanning calls, but whether they beat simply taking fewer steps is unclear. We compare the two in Fast-WAM, a 6B-parameter world action model, on LIBERO-Goal. Two uncached steps keep the success of ten: on all 500 initial states they are non-inferior within two percentage points, for one checkpoint and noise seed. Under PyTorch's default compile mode, they also cut a replanning call on an RTX A6000 from 202 to 92 ms. This leaves little room for caching. A cache that keeps two fresh steps cannot be faster than two uncached steps: every call still encodes the images, runs the video expert and computes those two steps. Caching the first of two steps, refreshed every fourth call, saves at most 11--14% of a call by our cost model. Cached policies are also fragile. Their losses tracked the share of the flow path the cached steps cover: under Fast-WAM's current time shift, C$^3$ache's eight cached steps cover 80% of the path instead of 44%, and the cached policy succeeds on 63% of states against 95% uncached. Resampling the noise on every call cost caches covering 60% or more of the path 14--40 points. These results argue for fewer-step baselines and for testing reuse under the deployed sampler and noise settings. We release our evaluation harness, logs and a checklist.

</details>

Abstract: Progress in autonomous navigation in off-road environments requires not only advances in algorithms, but also robust and capable physical platforms suited to both development and deployment. Today, off-road autonomy is typically developed and tested at one of two extremes of vehicle scale, each with different drawbacks. Full-size vehicles are capable but expensive, require large logistic infrastructure for deployment and maintenance, and expensive when pushed beyond their limits. RC-scale vehicles are small and inexpensive, but limited in the terrain they can traverse and the payload that they can carry, which includes their sensors and compute. We present the AlphaTruck platform, which leverages recent advances in compact sensing and compute to integrate multimodal sensing, embedded CPU-GPU compute, and an onboard autonomy stack for high-speed, off-road navigation, all integrated into a large-scale, 1/5th RC vehicle chassis. AlphaTruck is designed as both a research testbed and a field-deployable platform for commercial and defense missions such as survey, search and rescue, and agriculture, with the capacity to carry task-specific payloads. At 1/5 scale, the autonomy stack, sensors, and batteries all compete for the same mass, power, and cooling budget, so the platform must balance five needs: mobility over rough terrain, rich sensing, onboard computation, payload capacity to carry the autonomy stack while leaving room for task-specific equipment, and practical operation and repair. Thus, AlphaTruck navigates off-road environments autonomously at 6-10 m/s using only onboard sensing and compute.

Abstract: A distributed multi-robot object transportation strategy is proposed for objects of unknown shape, size, geometry, mass and mass distribution without any prior knowledge about the object or number of robots involved in the transportation process. Robots follow the boundary of the object and dynamically determine viable pushing points. A novel object-model-free heading-error-driven pushing effort regulation method is proposed to promote goal-directed object motion; robots regulate their pushing effort using individual and collective heading errors. Robots dynamically relocate around the object boundary if a pushing point is lost or the required pushing effort exceeds its capability to correct its heading. The proposed method is validated using simple and complex shapes with convex and non-convex boundary regions and dynamically changing weight distribution of objects. Detailed analysis on the effects of robot distribution and initial object pose on the transportation performance in terms of required time and path efficiency is also presented. Scalability analysis revealed that object transportation time is dependent on the number of pushing surfaces available which in turn depends on the object size, shape, and set inter-robot safety distance. The proposed system yielded highly efficient object paths with consistent elapsed transport times over several trials robust to external disturbances such as non-uniform friction between contacting surfaces and robot wheel slip.

Abstract: Water--land cross-domain robots require continuous localization across land, transition and underwater phases, where sensor characteristics and availability change abruptly. Existing datasets and conventional metrics are not designed to capture transition-induced degradation and failures. We present \textbf{M-CROSS}, to the best of our knowledge, the first multisensor simulation dataset and benchmark for SLAM across continuous water--land transitions. It provides synchronized LiDAR, sonar, stereo camera, IMU, DVL, and depth measurements; 6DoF pose ground truth; sensor-wise medium-state annotations; and medium-dependent sensor degradation models. We establish a transition-aware protocol comprising the Trajectory Completion Rate (TCR), Piecewise Absolute Pose Error (PW-APE), Transition End Error (TEE), and Transition Survival Period (TSP), which measure trajectory completion, single-domain accuracy, transition-induced error, and localization survival under prolonged partial immersion, respectively. Experiments with 6 representative SLAM systems show that existing methods fail to maintain both localization accuracy and trajectory continuity across the full set of water--land transitions, exhibiting transition-induced drift, trajectory discontinuities, and tracking failures. M-CROSS is publicly available https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MCross-Dataset

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) world models predict future embeddings instead of pixels and plan in latent space. Two challenges remain: 2D images leave the metric geometry that manipulation acts on implicit and entangled with appearance, and what these models imagine stays hidden, as they are mostly judged in simulation by short-horizon latent planning. We present JEPA4R, a JEPA world model that observes single-depth-camera point clouds, predicts and plans in their latent space, and acts through a policy head on its frozen latents; we call this 4D point planning. Abstract: Safety-critical high-speed legged locomotion requires motion planners to balance safety foresight with online computational efficiency. Although short prediction horizons reduce rollout costs, they provide limited safety foresight, and strict safety constraints severely restrict the feasible trajectory space. We propose a recovery-guided model-based diffusion (MBD) framework for safety-critical legged locomotion. It incorporates a learned terminal recovery critic into short-horizon trajectory optimization. Specifically, the critic approximates the worst-case future clearance margin along rollouts of a designated recovery policy. We introduce a feasibility-guided refinement strategy that combines within-horizon constraints and terminal recoverability at each sampling stage, while retaining feasible elites to mitigate sampling-support loss. Across 50 paired randomized Unitree Go2 obstacle-avoidance trials, our approach achieves zero collisions and 98% strict-clearance success, while requiring substantially less computation than long-horizon MBD baselines. Abstract: Experiments near vehicle handling limits must keep three questions apart: what a maneuver requests, what the closed-loop system executes, and whether the task is completed. Scenarios are composed from road, friction, obstacle, and reference objects; planners and controllers attach through a shared observation and control interface; a vehicle adapter runs either a license-free four-wheel model or an external dynamics solver; and synchronized records feed task evaluation, replay, and response inspection. Two case comparisons show why sideslip or tire-utilization peaks cannot replace task constraints. Abstract: In 2025, there was a total of 358 weather-related disasters worldwide affecting millions of people. For unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) to autonomously aid these people, they need to be able to navigate around complex obstacles and reach their destinations to deliver supplies or quickly locate survivors in disaster-stricken areas. Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as an important paradigm in robot learning, jointly predicting future scene states and robot actions from current observations and instructions. However, future states entangle task-related changes with large amounts of task-irrelevant content, such as static backgrounds, which weakens the model's ability to capture changes relevant to task execution. To address this, we propose Difference World Action Model (DiffWAM), a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework that enhances world representations by jointly learning future-state prediction and consecutive visual difference prediction. We define consecutive visual differences as differences between the latent representations of consecutive observations, providing an explicit learning signal for temporal variation. The framework comprises a World Expert for future frame prediction, an Action Expert for action generation, and a Diff Expert for predicting these latent differences along the trajectory. A structured unidirectional attention mask conditions the Diff, World, and Action Experts solely on the current-frame representation while blocking attention between experts. This design allows both the World and Diff Experts to be removed during inference, introducing no additional inference overhead. DiffWAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on the LIBERO and RoboTwin 2.0 benchmarks and substantially improves over FastWAM on real-world manipulation tasks. Specifically, it attains average success rates of 98.8%, 92.65%, and 88.3%, outperforming the state-of-the-art baseline (i.e., FastWAM) by 1.75, 1.55, and 15.0 percentage points, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: As older adults increasingly live alone, emergency support becomes an important function for home companion robots. In Australia, demand for the care and support workforce is projected to nearly double between 2020-21 and 2049-50. We present the design and implementation of a domestic companion-robot system on a Unitree Go1 quadruped platform that combines companionship with emergency awareness and response. A rule-based escalation policy combines signs detected from visual and speech input to determine the current risk level and adjust how long the robot waits for the user to confirm safety before entering emergency mode. Additional detected signs can shorten this waiting time. Users can confirm safety, request immediate help, stop robot motion, or reset the system. We verified the implemented decision rules and escalation timing through 105 automated trials and 25 staged physical trials. Additional visual evidence shortened the waiting time, and the system entered emergency mode when the waiting time ended without confirmation.

Abstract: A robot may know how to move an object yet still need to learn where it belongs. When a person rejects a placement, the robot must decide whether the correction applies only to that situation or expresses a broader rule. We study this problem in CRAFT (Contextual Requirement Adaptation with Frozen Tools), a framework for learning local requirements without retraining robot skills. It records physical success separately from whether an action meets the requirement. Our scope ladder uses memory when an action has already been judged in a nearly identical situation. Elsewhere, it combines memory, explicit rules and a learner for requirements beyond those rules, weighting their predictions by how well they predicted earlier feedback. Controlled studies show gains over correction memory alone in utility, which rewards acceptable actions and charges for mistakes and questions. In camera-grounded robot simulations, the ladder asks fewer questions than language-model agents with persistent memory, but some of those agents take fewer wrong actions and succeed more often after requirements change. All execution failures and timeouts remain in the evaluation. These results support combining specific memories with broader rules, while leaving reliable adaptation to changing requirements unresolved. All evidence comes from simulation with generated requirements and simulated feedback.

Abstract: When a robot practices without human resets, every attempt still costs wall-clock time, actuator wear, and sometimes a person's attention, yet the schedulers that decide what to practice next treat attempts as free. We present an open, low-cost testbed for measuring these costs: a self-built SO-101 arm runs two demonstration-trained ACT policies that reset each other, with a vision-based success detector and a ledger that records the time, joint travel, and human interventions of every attempt. We formulate cost-aware practice scheduling, in which task selection is rewarded by measured competence gain net of embodied cost, and specify in advance the comparison that will test it. Its first two conditions, run on hardware for 51 minutes of robot time, show that a fixed retry limit did not reduce the share of execution time lost to failures (36%, against 27% for a feasibility rule; not significant) and saved no robot time. The logs suggest why: failures showed no sign of clustering, so a retry right after a failure was as likely to succeed as any other attempt. The ledger also shows that most of the retry rule's extra human time came from cubes knocked out of reach rather than from the rule itself, and a cost-aware rule replayed on the logs recovers the sensible choice without a hand-set limit. We also report lessons from building the system, including a coverage effect in which concentrating demonstrations in a smaller workspace raised success there from 8/20 to 10/10.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves vision-language-action (VLA) policies by drawing several action candidates and executing the best one. We find that its returns diminish quickly when the candidates are resampled from one decoder: they differ by noise rather than by plan, and for each of three resampling sources, going from four to eight candidates adds only 0.06-0.07 to the average number of completed tasks on CALVIN. We introduce PlanDiv, an action head that makes the candidates differ by plan: a lightweight planner proposes K latent plan hypotheses, a shared masked-diffusion executor decodes one action chunk per plan from a single backbone pass, and the policy executes the chunk that its own likelihood ranks highest. The plans are learned from demonstrations alone, without plan labels or rewards. With the backbone, data and training recipe held fixed, eight plan-conditioned candidates raise five-task chain success on CALVIN ABC$\rightarrow$D from 56.9% to 69.9%, while eight candidates from autoregressive, plan-free or same-plan resampling reach at most 64.9%. The same-plan baseline is PlanDiv's own checkpoint with the plan held fixed, so this gap comes from the plans, not from drawing more samples. On LIBERO-Long, four plans remove 40% of single-candidate failures, against at most 21% for matched resampling. On a real Franka arm the gain appears where a task admits more than one solution: PlanDiv is on par with a single-output OpenVLA-OFT+ baseline on a one-solution task and succeeds in 51 of 54 trials against 40 of 54 on two tasks with two solutions.

Abstract: Advancing from individual embodied intelligence to swarm embodied intelligence is a key step toward scalable, general-purpose embodied intelligence. Complex tasks often require multiple agents to divide responsibilities, coordinate handovers, and manipulate shared objects collaboratively. However, existing robot learning methods rely primarily on individual demonstrations, which provide limited supervision for modeling dependencies among agent actions, while collecting multi-robot demonstrations at scale remains difficult. Human egocentric demonstrations offer a scalable alternative, yet distributed observations from multiple participants cannot directly supervise decentralized policies. EgoSwarm collects more than 50 hours of collaborative experience using a robot-free multi-person egocentric system. We introduce the Swarm Interaction Packet (SIP) and Swarm Interaction Token (SIT) to recover cross-agent interaction relations from distributed observations and transform this experience into local supervision consistent with deployment-time information constraints. We further propose SwarmVLA, which transfers knowledge of swarm behavior to decentralized policies conditioned on local observations, local history, and limited neighbor messages. Abstract: An autonomous vehicle must foresee the consequences of its actions, especially in the rare, time-critical situations where crashes happen. A world model offers this foresight, but only within the coverage of its training data, which poses an exploration-exploitation dilemma. Within this coverage a planner can hardly improve on the data, and beyond it the model's errors mislead it. Logs of competent driving rarely contain the failures that would widen the coverage, and agents that learn from interaction gain them only by failing themselves, without being shown what to do instead. We propose SAFER, a framework that learns offline from failures that others make, judged by an expert, and keeps learning online from its own driving. Offline, partially trained agents and scripted, recoverable perturbations Stage failures in simulation, and an expert Annotates every visited state with its action and value, so that much of what the planner explores is covered before it drives. In planning, a chunked latent world model Foresees the consequences fast enough for real-time use, and a risk-aware sampling-based planner Evaluates candidate maneuvers by their return minus a bounded collision penalty. Online, a loop continuously Refines the model wherever this driving leaves the coverage of the offline data. In closed-loop evaluation against the compared methods, SAFER reaches the highest route completion and reward per step on the CARLA Leaderboard routes, 0.95 and 0.77 from a privileged bird's-eye view, and the highest episode reward in MetaDrive, 53.4 in regular and 49.0 in safety-critical traffic. Project page: https://ys-qu.github.io/safer-website.

Abstract: We present RoboKit, a high-performance, general-purpose toolkit for parallel differentiable optimization in robotics, built on three principles: (i) Performance and flexibility: GPU-parallel multi-seed solvers pair sampling with versatile optimizers, running 2-60$\times$ faster than task-specific baselines across seven tasks; (ii) Geometry, rendering and physics: hybrid and coarse-to-fine representations accelerate collision, differentiable rendering and simulation, optimizing 4096 grasps in 2.97 s; (iii) Integration with learning: costs are differentiable and simulation is parallel, so RoboKit both distills optimization into policies and trains RL; distilled policies run 9-240$\times$ faster at comparable accuracy, and RL reaches a GPU baseline's scores up to 2.1$\times$ sooner on the same simulator.

</details>

Abstract: Dexterous grasping policies are usually trained for one arm paired with one hand. Every new pairing then needs its own policy, and the number of policies grows with every arm and hand added. We ask whether a single policy can control many arm-hand combinations and transfer to pairings and hands it has never seen. It maps every robot to a canonical joint interface with validity masks, encodes each joint's morphology with attention biased by the kinematic tree, and uses a Transformer-XL memory to infer embodiment-dependent dynamics from recent interaction. Trained on 18 combinations of four arms and six hands, it reaches 89% stable success on seen combinations and 51% on six held-out arm-hand pairs, outperforming GRU and DexFormer 1.0 baselines. Abstract: Safety filters for heterogeneous robot teams fail because of missing constraints, overestimated braking capabilities and conflicts between individually satisfiable constraints. We present OntoCBF, which compiles a domain ontology with a class taxonomy, typed relations, safety axioms and a dynamics profile per robot class into a control barrier function filter with one quadratic program per agent. Description-logic entailment supplies the constraints of any team online, including those that follow from chains of relations and from classes defined by what a robot carries, so the existing safety axioms cover new robot classes, payload classes and combinations. We prove that a subclass may reuse the braking capability of its parent whenever it can produce every velocity of the parent, a property separate from subsumption. For box-bounded double integrators this property is also necessary, and every loss of safety then begins with a conflict. Once three robots interact, the safe set of pairwise barriers contains states from which a collision is unavoidable, and such states persist in larger teams under any fixed capability reduction. In a simulated warehouse where humans ignore the robots, a filter written for the design classes violates safety in 89-100 % of the episodes with unseen classes. In all scenarios, including one with 32 robots, OntoCBF violates safety in 20-70 % of the episodes, each time after a conflict, with a median deepest intrusion of 10-17 % of the keep-out distance. A multi-agent PPO policy trained with the filter in the loop lowers the rate of infeasible quadratic programs, nearly all of them conflicts, by 18-41 %.

Abstract: Autoregressive normalizing flows (NFs) provide a tractable density for learning and sampling continuous robot actions. We show that a single maximum-likelihood objective can train vision--language--action (VLA) policies competitive with leading methods on manipulation benchmarks. Its deep--shallow architecture concentrates visual, language, and proprioceptive prefix conditioning in a high-capacity deep block, while unconditioned shallow blocks provide a shared invertible action transformation. The vision--language backbone and flow are jointly trained by exact maximum likelihood on Gaussian-smoothed action chunks. The same learned density supplies a single score-based correction at inference, refining generated actions to account for training-time smoothing without a separately trained denoiser. Abstract: A Koopman controller can stabilize its learned latent-space linear model yet fail to stabilize the nonlinear plant. We derive a robustness margin for approximate matching and show that a stable open-loop latent model cannot exactly match an unstable equilibrium. TIK recovers the fitted LQR gain at the target, while a bilinear predictor captures state-dependent actuation during large maneuvers. Across four simulated unstable benchmarks, all 40 Standard Koopman controllers have stable latent closed loops, but their deployed nonlinear systems are locally unstable. In contrast, all 40 TIK controllers locally stabilize their respective nonlinear plants. An offline stability test using empirical curvature estimates identifies stable TIK controllers on two cart-pole systems, but is inconclusive under high noise. Abstract: We present XMimic and XSoma, which learn humanoid motion tracking and locomotion on rough terrain in under a minute of training. Both follow SuperMimic, which learns a world model of the robot and optimizes the policy with analytic gradients through short rollouts imagined in this model. Existing implementations of this method do not make use of larger GPUs, as every training iteration is a long chain of small GPU operations that run one after another. XMimic and XSoma therefore shorten this chain and run the steps of every iteration at the same time on up to four GPUs. We compile the complete training loop with JAX, including the MuJoCo Warp simulator. For locomotion, XSoma leaves only the dynamics of the robot to the world model and computes everything else exactly, including the terrain height under the feet. It learns with a horizon of one second and without a critic. On four H100 GPUs, XMimic learns to track all 40 LAFAN1 clips with the Unitree G1 in 0.86 minutes, 3.7 times faster than BlinkMimic on one GPU. Abstract: When a pretrained robot policy fails on challenging tasks, common remedies include retraining the backbone, adding an external visual encoder, or introducing a memory module. We argue that wrong predictions do not imply wrong representations: a policy can retain the required information yet fail to read it out as the correct action. To test this view, we propose ReRead: with the backbone frozen, a corrector re-reads the current and two previous caches to predict an action residual. The corrector sees no images, is trained solely on offline demonstrations from all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, and adds only 4.17% latency. Ablations and interventions show that the correction relies on the backbone's own representations: none of four external visual encoders adds further gain, and swapping in past caches from other trajectories sharply increases action error. On the 11 hardest RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, ReRead improves the success rates of LingBot-VLA, $π_{0.5}$, and Fast-WAM by 23.45, 13.09, and 8.00 percentage points, respectively. In paired evaluation on all 50 tasks, it raises Fast-WAM's average success rate from 90.73% to 92.66%. On a Flexiv dual-arm robot, it raises success on a phase-ambiguous task from 0% to 36%.

Abstract: Robot policy pretraining based on human videos is crucial for improving the policy's generalization ability. One main challenge for this task is the lack of explicit action-relevant representations in such unlabeled data. Recent works tend to estimate 3D hand motion trajectories from videos using 3D foundation models (3D FMs). However, they keep solely the sparse trajectories as supervision, ignoring the rich, fine-grained information produced during the trajectory extraction process. To address this issue, we propose LSA, a hierarchical robot policy pretraining framework that includes three phases: Lift, See, and Act. Specifically, the Lift phase elevates 2D video observations into dense 3D representations by adaptively aligning features in the policy's shallow layers with the intermediate representations extracted from multiple 3D FMs. Building upon these lifted representations, the See phase equips the policy with explicit geometric and interactive awareness through dual guidance. It introduces depth-map reconstruction to help the model comprehend 3D spatial layouts and utilizes hand region cues to explicitly supervise the encoder's attention toward task-relevant interaction hotspots. Empowered by the dense 3D representation learning and precise spatial guidance, our policy achieves robust and accurate robotic manipulation in the Act phase. Extensive experiments on diverse simulation and real-world tasks demonstrate that LSA significantly outperforms current state-of-the-art approaches. The project page is available at: https://yiyuan00.github.io/Lift-See-Act.

Abstract: LiDAR place recognition provides the loop closures of Simultaneous Localization and Mapping(SLAM). Perspective from Z-axis, a gravity-aligned scan is an orthographic map of the scene, and a revisit from another position and heading moves this map rigidly in the plane, a motion that a polar Fourier spectrum ignores and phase correlation recovers. Along the vertical, the heights of the returns describe the structures, but which heights are observed depends on the range, because the sensor's vertical field of view cuts nearby structures low; height thus misleads a global key, yet is decisive once two scans are aligned and compared cell by cell where they overlap. We present \method, a training-free method built on one principle: two LiDAR scans can only be compared through features computed from the same geometric surfaces in both scans. \method retrieves candidates with a translation- and rotation-invariant polar spectrum of the bird's-eye view (BEV), 1.9,KB per keyframe, and verifies each candidate by estimating its planar pose through phase correlation and scoring it with \emph{point-anchored height columns}: every BEV cell keeps a 16-bit mask of the heights of its own returns; within a session, the score is weighted by the expected overlap of the two scans' sensing ranges, computed from their distance. We show that this vertical link pays off in verification but hurts in retrieval, where what a height slice contains depends on the range to the structure. For single robot, \method has the highest AP of the training-free methods without 3D registration on the KITTI development sequences; across robots, on all multi-robot sequences, it matches or exceeds RING++ at 1/350 of its descriptor size and closes more inter-robot loops than the learned BEVPlace++ on all tests.

</details>

To address this gap, we present ExtremeDrive, a real-world dataset and benchmark covering emergency maneuvers, complex traffic interactions, and adverse conditions such as slippery or snow-covered roads, alongside routine driving. Abstract: Since the late 19th century when the first walking toys were developed, it has been known that mechanical stoppers at the hip joint are crucial for generating stable passive dynamic walking. Recent research on passive-dynamic and limit-cycle walkers has also confirmed that mechanical stoppers at the hip and knee joints are effective for generating stable walking motion, but theoretical research on how this mechanical constraint enhances the overall gait stability remains insufficient. This paper introduces a planar X-shaped walker equipped with mechanical stoppers at the hip joint, and investigates the effect of the mechanical constraint on the stability of the wheel gait generated by constant torque drive on a downslope. By simply falling forward while using the stoppers to constrain itself to the target impact posture, the robot can generate a highly stable wheel gait. We divide the motion of one step into four phases, derive approximate analytical solutions for the state error transition function matrix in each phase using a linearized model, and analyze the increase or decrease in the state error norm using metrics such as its maximum singular value. Numerical simulations demonstrate that while two phases are unstable in terms of the increase in the state error norm, the remaining two phases are stable, resulting in overall asymptotic stability.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon vision-language navigation (VLN) requires agents to track instruction progress and recover from local errors before they propagate across subtasks. However, executors trained on expert trajectories encounter policy-induced state shifts during deployment, and their stop signals do not reliably indicate subtask completion. We introduce Learning and Verifying Progress for VLN (LeaP-VLN), an agentic VLN system that couples progress-aware execution with independent progress verification through plan--execute--verify--recover cycles. LeaP-VLN leverages progress both explicitly, through a progress verifier that verifies subtask completion before the agent advances and triggers dual-phase recovery upon route deviation, and implicitly, by co-training the executor on progress reasoning and action prediction to enhance the executor's progress awareness and corrective control. To adapt the executor to this closed-loop interface, we propose a two-stage progress-enhanced curriculum: behavior cloning on expert demonstrations, followed by joint training on demonstrations, expert action corrections, and progress-reasoning supervision. Policy-generated trajectories expose the executor to both intra-subtask drift and inter-subtask state shifts, aligning training with the deviations encountered during closed-loop execution. Extensive experiments on R2R-CE and RxR-CE demonstrate state-of-the-art success rates among monocular methods trained without additional navigation datasets, while real-world experiments further validate the effectiveness of LeaP-VLN in long-horizon navigation.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable proprioceptive state estimation for legged robots fuses inertial sensing with kinematic sensing from leg encoders. The latter assumes a foot is stationary during the stance phase; however, physical foot touchdown does not necessarily provide a stationary reference for odometry, particularly during impact and load transfer. Repeatedly applying kinematic corrections throughout the same contact phase can introduce redundant or unreliable information. We present SG-ODOM, a proprioceptive odometry framework that uses stability-gated contact selection to schedule kinematic updates. Following physical touchdown, a stability gate accepts the contact for state estimation only after the foot reaches a sufficiently stable support condition. The resulting validated touchdown event triggers an estimator correction, while additional timeout updates bound the maximum duration of uninterrupted IMU propagation. The framework is evaluated on multiple legged platforms, both quadrupedal and humanoid robots, against representative state-of-the-art proprioceptive estimators. Across the evaluated sequences, SG-ODOM demonstrates competitive or superior estimation accuracy, reducing Relative Pose Error (RPE) by up to 25% compared to the baseline estimator on outdoor sequences from the GrandTour dataset. Abstract: Tactile sensing is essential for contact-rich manipulation, yet heterogeneous sensing mechanisms and observation formats hinder cross-hardware transfer. Since physical contact is governed by Newtonian mechanics, we ground tactile representations in six-dimensional wrenches, establishing a common physical reference across sensing mechanisms. Specifically, we pair tactile observations from all sensors with wrench measurements, enabling shared physical supervision without requiring direct tactile-to-tactile pairing between sensors. To support learning at scale, we develop a dedicated acquisition platform and collect over 100 million paired tactile observations and wrench measurements from 24 sensors spanning four families of sensing principles. Building on this dataset, our framework combines sensor-specific input adaptation with a shared tactile encoder. By learning to reconstruct wrenches, the encoder maps observations from different sensors into a shared, continuous feature space grounded in contact physics. To leverage these representations for manipulation, we introduce TaF-Policy, which integrates tactile features with visual observations and robot states to generate action chunks. We evaluate the learned representations through wrench estimation, cross-sensor retrieval, few-shot adaptation to unseen sensors, and robotic manipulation. On the core benchmark, our method reduces single-frame force RMSE by 17.1% and improves cross-sensor retrieval R@1 by 13.11 percentage points over the strongest baseline. In manipulation experiments, TaF-Policy achieves average success rates of 51.33% across three simulated tasks and 46.25% across four real-world tasks, compared with 47.33% and 23.75%, respectively, for the strongest baselines evaluated on the same task sets. Abstract: Visuomotor policies condition action prediction on visual and proprioceptive observations. While historical proprioceptive sequences carry rich dynamic priors, current methods treat them as equal to high-dimensional vision, causing the proprioceptive signal to be overwhelmed. To identify what is lost in equal-conditioning fusion, we analyze proprioceptive data and find that strong inter-joint coupling is the key action-determining structure. This finding aligns with the neural sensorimotor loop, in which proprioception first forms a body-centered prior and vision is later calibrated via parietal remapping. Inspired by this, we propose ACiT, Proprioceptive-Prior Action Chunking with Inverted Transformers, which reinforces historical joint-coupling as the primary condition with vision as a later calibration. This design is instantiated through three modules: Inverted State Encoding builds a proprioceptive prior via per-joint tokens and inter-joint self-attention. State-Visual Grounding lets joint tokens query visual evidence, making vision goal- and calibration-only. Inverted Action Chunking decodes per-joint trajectories independently. ACiT improves success rates on most LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world tasks, with the largest gains on those that require dynamics and coordination. The same conditioning design transferred to the VLA backbone pi_0-ACiT yields significant gains with better execution efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Electric wheelchairs predominantly rely on manual joystick interfaces, which present critical barriers to independent mobility for individuals with severe motor and visual impairments. This paper presents AVD (Autonomous Vehicle Drive), a dependable teleoperation architecture for assistive mobility that unifies perception, communication, embedded control, and remote supervision in a single low-cost platform built entirely from commercially available hardware. The central engineering contribution is a safety-oriented design framework: control authority follows a static hierarchy, a manual emergency stop and the occupant's joystick pre-empt remote commands, and the on-board sensing layer has stop-only authority. The compute design partitions primary and safety functions and specifies a heartbeat-driven safe-state transition for the fault cases considered. The sensing stack this framework depends on is characterized against measured data: per-channel acquisition latency, a 2,025-scan LiDAR degradation campaign spanning five target configurations and a control under water mist, airborne dust, and direct aperture occlusion, and attitude measurements across static inclines to 47 degrees. The lowest pooled environmental-condition mean retained 76.6% of the clear-baseline forward-sector yield, and no scan lost the sector completely. A logged vibration-induced inertial dropout occurred while LiDAR and camera logging continued, illustrating complementary channel behavior. The result is a documented prototype and an explicit validation path for a deployable wheelchair implementation.

Abstract: Multimodal Place Recognition (PR) can improve robustness, but most systems rigidly read and process every sensor for every query, even when the primary sensor is sufficient, with implications for energy consumption, second sensor update rate, and domain-specific considerations such as passive stealth. We introduce selective, controllable sensor acquisition, where a user specifies a desired second sensor usage rate and the system acquires the additional sensor only when the primary sensor's estimated performance is sufficiently poor. An online threshold adjustment achieves the requested usage rate by adapting to recent primary sensor performance, directing additional sensing towards queries where fusion is most likely to help without calibration specific to sensor modality, PR method, or environment. The approach requires only a PR method that produces a match score and applies to both single-frame and sequence-based PR. Our system, across multiple datasets, PR descriptors, and sensor combinations, recovers 55% of the available PR performance gain while using the second sensor on only 25% of queries, and 81% while using it on 50%. We further analyse alternative acquisition decision mechanisms and usage-rate control methods, and examine performance when the second sensor is stronger than, comparable to, or weaker than the primary sensor.

Abstract: Robotic reward models (RRMs) use foundation models to replace per-task reward engineering with reusable, task-conditioned evaluation. Yet under one name, their outputs answer different questions, from whether a task succeeded to which execution is better. This survey organizes 114 methods into five semantic families by the question their native outputs answer: Outcome, Absolute Progress, Relative, Temporal Value, and Hybrid. Within this taxonomy, we review how model design determines what an evaluator can judge, how raw experience becomes supervision, and how existing evaluation practice tests the result. The review exposes a gap: agreement with demonstration order and downstream policy gains do not show whether a reward tracks task fulfillment. We therefore introduce RRM-Bench-1K, 1,117 real-robot videos of closed-loop executions with task-state and camera-evidence labels, and four contrast suites that separate the credited condition from its usual cues. A unified study of 27 evaluators shows that the strongest final scores rank whole executions well enough for selection and filtering. Dense credit, however, follows the progression of an execution more closely than its task state, and no evaluator signals when the decisive view is missing. How much fidelity a reward model needs therefore depends on the decision it drives. We outline directions from real-failure supervision to metrics that separate task state from chronology, and open-source RRM-Hub, a toolbox in which 14 existing RRMs can be readily applied and new evaluators tested on the same capabilities.

</details>

Abstract: Non-imaging optical tactile sensors encode contact through changes in optical intensity, wavelength, spectra, or photon counts, offering diverse architectures for robotic touch. Their usefulness depends on whether contact variables can be reliably distinguished from coupled mechanical and environmental effects, and whether the resulting information can be delivered in time to guide action. This review develops a unified framework linking mechanical load transmission, optical encoding, readout, and tactile inference to robotic task requirements. It examines reflective and waveguide-based devices, micro/nanofibers, fiber gratings, interferometric and resonant structures, and mechanoluminescent composites. Three considerations emerge across these approaches. First, high optical sensitivity supports accurate tactile estimation only when target variables remain identifiable under the relevant contact and calibration conditions. Second, spatial coverage and feedback timing must be assessed at the system level, accounting for multiplexing, acquisition, demodulation, and inference. Third, device characterization, offline recognition, and closed-loop robotic performance provide distinct levels of evidence and require different evaluation criteria. Representative studies illustrate how these considerations shape force estimation, contact localization, slip correction and distributed interaction. This review concludes with priorities for calibration transfer, reliable interfaces, scalable readout, and task-based validation, providing a basis for selecting and developing non-imaging optical tactile architectures for robotics.

</details>

Abstract: Classical control barrier functions (CBFs) provide safety constraints for controllers, but designing them by hand is difficult for underactuated vehicles and the resulting safe sets can be overly conservative. Neural CBFs can learn more informative barriers from data, yet a learned barrier is usually verified only for one fixed obstacle configuration. We study whether one neural barrier can be reused across environments by expressing the state relative to each obstacle. The key gap is that these relative coordinates may discard information needed to predict their own evolution. We give a generalized closure condition that represents this missing information as a residual and a lifting theorem that transfers a verified relative-coordinate barrier to every obstacle in a family. A rank test also identifies when symmetry alone cannot justify the transfer. We validate the conditions on a double integrator, a planar aircraft, and a 12-state quadrotor. Coordinates that fail the test lead to obstacle penetration or to a barrier condition that fails near hover, whereas body-frame coordinates close exactly for the quadrotor. The resulting barrier transfers to new maps without retraining and is effective in the reported deployment and fast-start experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Natural, coherent full-face dynamic expressions are central to making humanoid robots socially approachable, yet existing systems commonly realize only limited lip motion or scripted expressions. The limitation is both physical and algorithmic. A compact head leaves little room for densely packed, independently controllable actuation; meanwhile, learned systems often generate an avatar video, landmarks, or facial parameters and then infer actuator commands through visual retargeting. This render-and-invert path adds latency and artifacts, and it does not reliably transfer generated behavior to a physical robot. We present ROSE (RObot facial expression through Shared motion Embeddings), a framework that integrates biomimetic hardware, cross-embodiment motion representation learning, and multimodal motion generation. On the hardware side, we build a flexible tendon-sheath transmission structure that integrates 34 actuators into a 19.5 cm biomimetic head. By decoupling actuator placement from facial action points, it enables coordinated full-face expression within a compact volume. On the algorithmic side, ROSE-VAE learns an identity-independent shared facial-motion latent space from human, digital-human, and robot observations. ROSE-Flow directly maps text, audio, or their combination to trajectories in this motion space without using video as a generator input. ROSE-ACT then converts these trajectories into constrained actuator commands. Our system demonstrates how high-density biomimetic hardware and a shared executable facial-motion representation can jointly unify cross-embodiment learning, multimodal generation, and physical robot expression.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous monitoring and intervention in semi-structured agricultural environments, such as orchards and vineyards, require mobile robots to navigate to locations across diverse field configurations. This entails performing navigation behaviors including in-row driving, row turning and skipping, and inter-row traversal. Recent learning-based approaches have enabled robust in-row navigation from onboard perception, but navigation across rows typically requires a priori knowledge of field geometry to specify and tune row-transition maneuvers, preventing arbitrary transitions between rows. We address this challenge by framing navigation in these environments as a goal-directed navigation task, where a long-horizon goal is expressed relative to the robot and a planner generates local paths based on the goal and the robot's perceptual observations. We present FlowRow, a flow-matching-based local planner which models the distribution of diverse agricultural navigation behaviors as $\mathrm{SE}(2)$ paths using the long-horizon goal and depth perception. To enable systematic evaluation, we introduce RowNavBench, a navigation benchmark for semi-structured agricultural environments developed in Isaac Sim, comprising 900 episodes across grape vineyards, lemon orchards, and apple orchards with varying row lengths, row and plant spacings, plant positions, and weed growth. FlowRow achieves 87% success, compared to 44% for the strongest generative behavior cloning baseline retrained on the same demonstrations. Abstract: Dynamical-system-based motion policies provide a framework for learning reactive robot motions with stability and convergence properties. Many manipulation tasks, however, admit several distinct goals or a continuous set of acceptable outcomes, requiring motion primitives that preserve these alternatives. Accommodating both goal structures within a unified framework with continuous, autonomous dynamics remains an open challenge. We propose a CONDOR-based framework, multi-CONDOR, for learning motion primitives with either multiple discrete attractors or a continuous attracting goal contour. The approach combines imitation learning with losses that encourage agreement between the learned task dynamics and latent reference systems encoding the desired attraction structure. We provide a Lyapunov-based analysis of the convergence properties of the learned motion dynamics. We evaluate the framework on simulated benchmarks with discrete goals, qualitative examples with continuous goal contours, and robotic grasping experiments. In simulation, multi-CONDOR reduces trajectory RMSE by approximately 72% compared with separately learned single-goal motion primitives selected by a state machine. For tasks with two and three goals, respectively, it reduces the fraction of evaluated initial states ending outside the goal tolerance from StableNODE's 0.31% and 7.29% to 0% in both settings. Experiments with a Franka Emika robot arm demonstrate successful grasping of a container by either of its two handles using motions learned from demonstrations. Together, these results show multi-CONDOR combines motion reproduction with the flexibility to converge to alternative valid outcomes.

</details>

Abstract: Cooperative driving extends autonomous driving from individual vehicle decisions to joint maneuvers that improve traffic safety and efficiency. Optimization-based methods impose safety constraints but often fix paths to simplify multi-vehicle planning, limiting spatial flexibility. Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) explores alternatives through interaction, but coordinated learning and safe execution remain challenging. Their integration nevertheless faces challenges in exploration efficiency, experience allocation, and execution reliability. To address these challenges, this paper proposes CoopDistill, an equilibrium-guided multi-agent policy distillation framework. A potential-game (PG) formulation provides a fixed-path longitudinal reference, around which a residual MARL policy learns longitudinal acceleration, conflict-approach timing, and lateral path corrections. A hard-case curriculum concentrates experience on interactions that PG leaves unresolved or substantially delayed. A comparative rollout selector evaluates the joint consequences of learned corrections against PG before choosing the executed action. Experiments in merging, roundabout, and intersection scenarios compare CoopDistill with six baselines at four connected automated vehicle penetration levels. Relative to PG, success improves by 2.1--27.1 percentage points and delay decreases by 7.3--27.3\%, with no observed collisions. Code is available at https://github.com/FanGShiYuu/CoopDistill

Abstract: Frontier multimodal models can now control robots zero-shot from images, yet many manipulation tasks depend on contact forces that images cannot show. Bringing force to an API-only frozen model raises two challenges. First, contact evolves much faster than an API model can decide, so the system must close the loop on force beneath the model. Second, a frozen model cannot interpret raw force streams, so force must be returned in a form the model can reason about. Therefore, we present ForceHarness, a harness that lets frozen models use force without demonstrations or finetuning. After calibrating hand force from a wrist sensor or joint torques alone, ForceHarness addresses the first challenge with self-terminating force primitives that regulate force on their own and return at a contact event. To address the second challenge, a physics translation layer turns each force trace into physical quantities with uncertainty as the tool result. To evaluate ForceHarness against expert force controllers and learned policies under identical conditions, we also build HiddenPhys, a seven-task MuJoCo benchmark with hidden physical variables. On a real arm without a wrist force sensor, ForceHarness raises the zero-shot success rate of GPT-6 Astra from 3.3% to 90.0%. In simulation, it adds 17.5 percentage points on the public ForceBench suite and 34.3 percentage points on HiddenPhys, surpassing force-aware policies trained on demonstrations on both benchmarks and matching hand-written expert controllers on HiddenPhys. Ablations further show that smaller models make significantly better decisions when given physical quantities rather than raw force values.

Abstract: Robots that work alongside people should be able to use what they have seen when new requests arise. The same experience may later support identifying an object or returning it to its original location, although these uses were unknown during observation. ReuseMem learns to use one visual memory for multiple later manipulation goals. It records visual features independently of future goals, retrieves complementary evidence for each request, and combines it with the current scene through a lightweight action interface. Retrieval is supervised by how well candidate evidence supports expert-action prediction across different current states, linking memory selection to its use in manipulation. Repeated visual features are stored once, and memory computations that remain unchanged during action generation are reused. Abstract: LLM agents can use remote tools and cloud sandboxes, but integrating reasoning, session state, and heterogeneous execution sites remains a runtime design problem. We present Linglong Agent Service (agentsvc), a self-hosted HTTP service and embeddable Python runtime that treats execution site as a configurable dimension alongside model, tools, and context. A parent service retains inference orchestration, tool descriptions, and conversation history; each inference request selects one child environment for file, shell, terminal, and MCP operations. Direct HTTP and child-initiated WebSocket tunnels carry the same application interfaces, including portable request context and centrally managed updates. The normal core path requires no third-party Python packages, with an optional Windows pseudo-terminal dependency. We further present online_debugger as the process-scale special case of execution-site separation: an instrumented program submits exception evidence, executes session-scoped inspection tools inside the incident process, and resumes the same diagnostic session. Its default prompt requires failing-then-passing reproduction evidence for source changes, while runtime patch activation and failed-call recovery remain unimplemented. We explain the design through constrained expected-utility reasoning, compare concrete mechanisms with Hermes Agent, Pi, Codex, and Claude Code, and report implementation and regression-test evidence. The current evidence establishes executable contracts, not performance advantages, repair accuracy, or production recovery. The source code is open source under the MIT license at https://github.com/mz24cn/agents.

Abstract: Robots, vehicles, and wearable devices often traverse the same environment many times. Each pass produces its own trajectory from onboard visual or visual-inertial odometry. These trajectories are locally accurate but drift over time and live in unrelated coordinate frames. Turning many such independent trajectories into one globally consistent set is the large-scale multi-session trajectory optimization problem. Existing solutions largely work offline. All sessions must be available up front, one session is fixed as the reference frame, and a single fused map is then optimized. This paper presents Polygraph, a multi-session trajectory optimization pipeline that processes sessions online, one keyframe and one spatial cluster at a time, and never builds a 3D map. Keyframes are grouped into local clusters. When a cluster closes, it is matched against every other session through learned image retrieval. Candidate pairs are verified with sparse feature matching, monocular metric depth, and robust pose estimation. A two-tier consensus gate admits a cross-session edge into the shared pose graph only once independent evidence corroborates it. A cheap incremental solve keeps the graph current, and a periodic robust batch solve prunes outliers. We evaluate Polygraph on stereo outdoor driving, HoloLens and iPad indoor traversals, legged indoor locomotion with a Boston Dynamics Spot, and large-scale outdoor vehicle and handheld routes. Against the closest prior work on the same GPU model, using its reported timings, Polygraph runs faster, commits fewer loop-closure edges, and matches or improves on accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Cooperative mobile manipulation requires robots to coordinate their motions while ensuring whole-body collision avoidance and preserving task-relevant behaviors. Abstract: Learned physics-based models provide structure for robot control, but uncertain payloads and unmodeled dynamics limit their predictive accuracy. Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) couple action generation with predictions of future states. An initial action chunk may be flawed. A future prediction that is not conditioned on the chunk may fail to reflect the changes that the proposed actions would induce in visual representations, limiting its usefulness for refinement before execution. We introduce ThinkWAM, a latent WAM that establishes bidirectional feedback before execution through a proposal-rethink-refine loop: the policy first proposes a complete action chunk, the proposal corrects multi-horizon future latents through a learned residual, and the corrected latents are injected into a second pass of the same action head, which generates the executable chunk. An affordance-guided observation-action memory and a near-fine, far-coarse multi-horizon prediction scheme, both motivated by predictive coding, support this loop, and all prediction stays in latent space. ThinkWAM achieves 98.70% success on LIBERO, 84.76% on LIBERO-Plus without OOD fine-tuning, 85.75%/65.85% on RoboTwin 2.0 Clean/Random, and 65.17% on RoboCasa24. ThinkWAM achieves 64.4% mean success across three real-world OOD conditions. The mean two-pass inference latency per action chunk is 153.48 ms on LIBERO, 164.61 ms on RoboTwin, and 101.2 ms on the Galaxea A1Z. Ablations show complementary gains from rethinking and affordance guidance. Diagnostics indicate that proposal-conditioned corrections change both predicted futures and action trajectories, showing that the corrected future representations improve the final action.

</details>

Abstract: Formation maneuver control enables a multiagent system to cooperatively navigate through constrained environments in collective motion. While various formation controllers exist, they rely heavily on high-quality and dynamically feasible reference trajectories provided a priori, making formation motion planning (FMP) essential. To address the computational challenges for high-dimensional joint trajectories faced by conventional constrained optimization-based solutions, this paper introduces a hybrid optimization-generative framework with a focus on affine formations, which provide the necessary spatial flexibility. By formulating the planning task as a constrained Maximum-a-Posteriori estimation, we decouple multiagent objectives to leverage pre-trained single-agent diffusion priors for dynamic feasibility, obstacle avoidance, and smoothness. We then interleave the generative process with projected gradient descent (PGD) to strictly enforce affine geometric constraints with safety guarantees. Simulations demonstrate that our framework generates highly smooth, collision-free plans that are readily trackable by common distributed controllers and outperforms state-of-the-art planners in various metrics.

Abstract: All-electric heavy-duty robotic manipulators are increasingly expected to operate close to tunnel walls, equipment, and other structures while retaining the force capability required for large-payload tasks. Accurate motion control alone does not address the resulting safety and interaction requirements. This paper develops a safety-aware physical-interaction architecture that combines variable impedance control (VIC) with control barrier functions (CBFs) on top of an established virtual decomposition control (VDC) framework for electromechanical linear actuator (EMLA)-driven manipulators. VIC continuously adapts the task-space stiffness and damping according to predicted proximity and contact risk, whereas a joint-velocity CBF filter enforces Cartesian workspace boundaries and the physical stroke limits induced by the closed-chain lift and tilt mechanisms. To account for nonideal realization of the filtered velocity by the inner VDC/EMLA dynamics, the CBF inequalities are tightened using a measured bound on the joint-velocity tracking error. Four configurations, namely VDC, VIC+VDC, CBF+VDC, and VIC+CBF+VDC, are evaluated under workspace, actuator-limit, and physical-interaction scenarios. VIC reduces the peak and RMS environment-interaction forces by 92.59% and 96.33%, respectively; both robust CBF configurations maintain positive workspace and EMLA safety margins, while VIC+CBF+VDC reduces the CBF intervention metric by 10.30% compared with CBF+VDC.

</details>
